Written Questions & Answers · QP-2408

Engineering Management — August 2024

EXAMINATION OF MARINE ENGINEER OFFICER · Function: Marine Engineering Management at Management Level · M.E.O. Class I

Sr. No. QP-2408 3 hours Total marks 100 9 questions · answer six (India 2024)

Examination instructions, as printed
  • Answer SIX questions only.
  • All questions carry equal marks.
  • Neatness in handwriting and clarity in expression carries weightage
  • Blank pages if any, to be struck by (X) at the end of each question.

Instruction 2 on the paper states that all questions carry equal marks, and six answered questions are required against a printed 'Total Marks - 100'. Every question is therefore recorded at 16 marks, so six answered questions total 96 against the printed 100. The discrepancy is printed on the source copy and is reproduced, not corrected.

Understand this first

A flag State has promised the world that the ships flying its flag are built and maintained to an international standard, and that promise is kept by surveying them. Most flag States cannot survey their own fleet - they have neither the surveyors nor the offices in the ports where their ships trade - so they hand the work to a professional body, usually a classification society, and let it issue the certificates in the flag State's name. That is a sensible arrangement with one obvious weakness: the body doing the surveying is paid by the shipowner it is surveying, and the flag State whose name is on the certificate may never see the ship. So the international answer comes in two halves. Before any work is handed over, the State must satisfy itself that the body is capable and genuinely independent, and must write down exactly which jobs it is handing over - that is the front half. And because capability on the day of appointment proves nothing about capability five years later, the State must keep watching: getting reports, auditing, inspecting some of the ships itself, and measuring how the body's ships actually perform in the world's ports - that is the back half. The Code that sets out the first half in binding terms and the second half as guidance is the RO Code, and the two halves are its Part 2 and Part 3. The idea underneath the whole thing is simple and worth holding onto: the State can delegate the work, but it cannot delegate the responsibility.

Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

  1. What the RO Code is, and how it binds
  2. The power to delegate, and the limit on it
  3. Approval step one - the general requirements the organization must satisfy
  4. Approval step two - the organizational, resource and process assessment
  5. Approval step three - certification of the quality management system
  6. Approval step four - the act of authorization, and what the flag State must specify
  7. Approval step five - the written agreement and the notification to IMO
(b)
  1. What oversight is, and the status of Part 3
  2. Mechanism one - supervision of the delegated duties
  3. Mechanism two - verification and monitoring, and the RO's reporting duties
  4. Mechanism three - auditing
  5. Mechanism four - managing the oversight programme
  6. Mechanism five - the performance indicators
  7. What follows a finding, and why the scheme exists at all

Memory cue. Limb (a) is a PROCEDURE: WHAT (1), MAY (2), then three tests - WHO they are (3), HOW they run (4), WHO CHECKS THEM (5) - then AUTHORIZE (6) and SIGN AND TELL IMO (7). Limb (b) is FIVE MECHANISMS: SUPERVISE (9), VERIFY AND REPORT (10), AUDIT (11), MANAGE (12), MEASURE (13) - bracketed by STATUS (8) and CONSEQUENCE (14).

Model written answer

(a) The mandatory requirements and the approval procedure

1. What the RO Code is, and how it binds

The Code for Recognized Organizations (RO Code) was adopted by the Organization by resolutions MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65), and took effect on 1 January 2015 upon entry into force of the corresponding amendments to the 1974 SOLAS Convention and the 1988 Load Lines Protocol. It is given mandatory force for SOLAS purposes through regulation XI-1/1. It is a single consolidated instrument: before it, the criteria for recognizing an organization were spread across Assembly resolutions and applied unevenly.

The Code's own purpose statement is that it serves as the international standard and consolidated instrument containing minimum criteria against which organizations are assessed towards recognition and authorization, and the guidelines for the oversight by flag States. Its scope paragraph records that it establishes four things, and the four map directly onto this question:

  • the mandatory requirements an organization must fulfil to be recognized by a flag State;
  • the mandatory requirements an RO must fulfil when performing statutory certification and services on behalf of its authorizing flag States;
  • the mandatory requirements flag States shall adhere to when authorizing an RO; and
  • guidelines for flag State oversight of ROs.

Structurally the Code has three parts, and knowing which is which is worth more than any single detail in it:

PartContentStatus
Part 1General - purpose, scope, contents, delegation of authority, communication of information, referencesGeneral provisions
Part 2Recognition and authorization requirements for organizationsMANDATORY - binds both the organization and the flag State
Part 3Oversight of recognized organizationsRECOMMENDATORY - guidance, expressly non-mandatory

Two definitions govern everything that follows, and candidates who blur them cannot explain the Code's structure. Recognition is the flag State's assessment of an organization against Part 2. Authorization is the separate, later act of delegating specified statutory functions to an organization that is already recognized. An organization can be recognized and still not be authorized for a particular ship type or a particular convention.

2. The power to delegate, and the limit on it

Part 1 section 4 is the enabling provision. A flag State may delegate authority to an organization recognized as complying with the Code, to perform on its behalf statutory certification and services under mandatory IMO instruments and its national legislation. Two limits sit immediately alongside it:

  • The flag State shall not authorize functions beyond the RO's capabilities, and in this respect shall take appendix 2 of the Code into consideration. Capability is assessed function by function, not once and for all.
  • Flag States should cooperate with each other with the objective of ensuring that ROs to whom they delegate authority adhere to the Code.

The conventional bases for the delegation itself are collected in Part 2 section 8.1: regulation I/6 of SOLAS 1974, article 13 of Load Lines 66, regulation 6 of MARPOL Annex I, regulation 8 of MARPOL Annex II and article 6 of TONNAGE 69. Under these a flag State may authorize an RO to act on its behalf in statutory certification and services and in the determination of tonnages, only for ships entitled to fly its flag. Such authorizations shall not require ROs to perform actions that impinge on the rights of another flag State.

3. Approval step one - the general requirements the organization must satisfy

Part 2 section 2 opens with the controlling proposition: delegation of authority by a flag State to an organization shall be subject to the confirmation of the capability of that organization to demonstrate that it has the capacity to deliver high standards of service and its compliance with the requirements of this Code and applicable national legislation. The demonstration is the organization's; the confirmation is the flag State's. The heads are:

RequirementWhat the flag State must be satisfied of
2.2 Rules and regulationsThe organization establishes, publishes and systematically maintains its own rules or regulations for the design, construction and certification of ships and their associated essential engineering systems, a version of which is provided in English, and has adequate research capability to keep the published criteria updated
2.3 IndependenceNeither the RO nor its staff engages in activities that may conflict with their independence of judgement and integrity. Those carrying out the statutory work are not the designer, manufacturer, supplier, installer, purchaser, owner, user or maintainer of the item certified, nor the authorized representative of any of them. The RO shall not be substantially dependent on a single commercial enterprise for its revenue
2.4 ImpartialityPersonnel are free from any pressures that might affect their judgement, and procedures prevent external persons or organizations influencing the results. All potential customers have access without undue financial or other conditions, and the procedures are administered in a non-discriminatory manner
2.5 IntegrityThe RO is governed by the principles of ethical behaviour contained in a Code of Ethics, which recognizes the inherent responsibility associated with a delegation of authority
2.6 CompetenceStatutory work is performed by competent surveyors and auditors, duly qualified, trained and authorized to execute all duties within their level of work responsibility
2.7 ResponsibilityThe RO defines and documents the responsibilities, authorities, qualifications and interrelation of personnel whose work affects the quality of its services
2.8 TransparencyAccess to, or disclosure of, information relating to the statutory work carried out on the flag State's behalf

Independence is the head that matters most, and it is worth saying why. The structural weakness of the whole arrangement is that the body doing the surveying is paid by the owner being surveyed. The revenue-dependence rule and the prohibition on certifying what you designed, supplied or maintain are the Code's direct answer to that, and an answer that lists the seven heads without identifying which one the Code is really defending has described the requirements without understanding them.

4. Approval step two - the organizational, resource and process assessment

Being independent and competent in principle is not enough; Part 2 then assesses how the organization actually runs. The flag State examines it against four further sections:

  • Section 3 - Management and organization. A documented quality, safety and pollution prevention policy; documentation requirements and a quality manual; control of documents and of records; planning; the organizational structure; communication internally, with the flag State and between ROs; and management review with defined review inputs and outputs.
  • Section 4 - Resources. Personnel, infrastructure and work environment adequate to the tasks assigned.
  • Section 5 - Statutory certification and services processes. Design and development inputs, outputs, verification and change control; control of production and service provision; subcontracting and service suppliers; control of monitoring and measuring devices; and a complaints and appeals process.
  • Section 6 - Performance measurement, analysis and improvement. Internal audit; vertical contract audit; monitoring and measurement of processes; the control, monitoring and measurement of non-conformities including statutory deficiencies; and improvement through data analysis, corrective action and preventive action.

Two of these are worth naming explicitly in an answer because they are what distinguish a real assessment from a paper one. The vertical contract audit follows a single ship's certification right through the organization rather than sampling processes in isolation. And section 6.5 requires the RO to control and measure statutory deficiencies, not merely its own internal non-conformities - which is how the flag State's interest gets into the RO's own quality data.

The qualification of the people is not left general. Appendix 1 sets out requirements for the training and qualification of the RO's technical staff, including theoretical and practical training, plan approval staff, survey staff and audit staff, and the assessment and maintenance of training effectiveness. Appendix 2 specifies the survey and certification functions ROs perform on behalf of the flag State, by elementary module.

5. Approval step three - certification of the quality management system

Part 2 section 7 is a separate step and is frequently missed. It requires two distinct things:

  • 7.1 - the RO shall develop, implement and maintain an effective internal quality management system that complies with the Code and is based on appropriate parts of internationally recognized quality standards no less effective than the ISO 9000 series.
  • 7.2 - that system shall be periodically assessed and certified by a qualified body, accredited by an accreditation body that is a signatory to the International Accreditation Forum Multinational Recognition Agreement, recognized by the flag State as having the necessary governance and competences to act independently of the ROs or their associations.

The operative word is independently. It is not enough for the RO to run a quality system, nor for the industry to certify itself; the certifying body must be accredited and independent of the ROs and their associations. Section 7.3 adds that IMO itself endeavours to closely monitor the certification and audit process of ROs and will establish the working methods and rules of procedure for that monitoring.

6. Approval step four - the act of authorization, and what the flag State must specify

Only now does the flag State authorize. Part 2 section 8 sets out what it must do, and this is the heart of limb (a) because it is the only part of the Code that is a procedure rather than a standard.

SectionWhat the flag State shall do
8.2 Legal basisEstablish the legal basis under which the authorization is administered, considering: the formal written agreement with the RO; acts, regulations and supplementary information; interpretations; and deviations and equivalent solutions
8.3 Specification of authorizationSpecify the scope of the authorization granted. Nine specifications shall be considered: ship types and sizes; conventions and other instruments including national legislation; approval of drawings; approval of materials and equipment; surveys, audits, inspections; issuance, endorsement and/or renewal of certificates; corrective actions; withdrawal or cancellation of certificates; and reporting requirements
8.4 ResourcesEnsure the RO has adequate resources in technical, managerial and research capabilities to accomplish the tasks assigned, in accordance with the minimum standards in Part 2
8.5 InstrumentsProvide the RO with access to all appropriate instruments of national law giving effect to the conventions, notify it of additions, deletions or revisions in advance of their effective date, and specify whether the flag State's standards go beyond convention requirements
8.6 InstructionsIssue specific instructions detailing the procedures for carrying out the work, and the actions to be followed if a ship is found not fit to proceed to sea without danger to the ship or persons on board or presenting an unreasonable threat of harm to the marine environment; and ensure by appropriate means that ROs cooperate with each other
8.7 RecordsSpecify that the RO maintains records that can provide the flag State with data to assist in the interpretation of convention regulations

The word to underline in limb (a) is 'specify'. A general authorization to 'act on our behalf' does not satisfy section 8.3. The scope has to be written down function by function and convention by convention, and note that it must cover the unglamorous end as well - corrective actions, and the withdrawal or cancellation of certificates. A flag State that has not specified who may withdraw a certificate has not finished authorizing.

7. Approval step five - the written agreement and the notification to IMO

Two procedural acts complete the approval, and both are commonly omitted from answers.

The formal written agreement. Appendix 3 sets out the elements a formal written agreement or equivalent between the flag State and the RO should as a minimum cover: application; purpose; general conditions; the execution of functions under authorization (general authorization, special or additional authorization, the relationship between the organization's statutory and other related activities, and functions to cooperate with port States to facilitate rectification of reported port State control deficiencies); the legal basis of those functions (acts and regulations, interpretations, deviations and equivalent solutions); reporting to the flag State; development of rules and regulations and the exchange of information; and other conditions.

The notification to IMO. Part 1 section 5 requires the flag State to communicate to, and deposit with, the Secretary-General of IMO a list of its ROs, for circulation to interested parties for the information of their officers, together with a notification of the specific responsibilities and conditions of the authority delegated. This is what makes a port State able to know, before it inspects, exactly what the certificate in front of it represents.

(b) The oversight mechanisms in the RO Code

8. What oversight is, and the status of Part 3

Part 3 of the RO Code provides guidance on flag State's oversight of ROs authorized to perform statutory certification and services on its behalf, and on the principles of oversight, which may include ship inspection, auditing and monitoring activities. Its scope paragraph is explicit that Part 3 provides guidance, which is non-mandatory, to assist flag States in developing and implementing an effective oversight programme. The adopting resolution reinforces this: it recommends Governments to use the recommendatory provisions of part 3 as a basis for relevant standards, unless their national requirements provide at least an equivalent degree of safety.

Saying this is a mark, not a hedge. It explains why Part 3 says should throughout where Part 2 says shall, and it is the reason flag States' oversight programmes differ in form while converging in substance.

Part 3 section 4 supplies the vocabulary the rest of it uses - audit (a systematic, independent and documented process for obtaining audit evidence and evaluating it objectively against audit criteria), audit criteria, audit evidence, and audit findings, which the Code notes can indicate conformity, observation (potential non-conformity), non-conformity, or opportunities for improvement.

9. Mechanism one - supervision of the delegated duties

Part 3 section 5.2 sets out what the flag State's supervision of duties delegated to an RO should consider, inter alia:

  1. documentation of the RO's quality management system;
  2. access to the RO's internal instructions, circulars and guidelines;
  3. access to the RO's documentation relevant to the flag State's fleet;
  4. cooperation with the flag State's inspection and verification work; and
  5. provision of information and statistics, such as but not limited to damage and casualties relevant to the flag State's fleet.

The thread running through all five is access. Oversight is impossible if the flag State can see only the certificates the RO chose to issue, so the Code puts the RO's internal instructions and its fleet documentation within the flag State's reach as a matter of course.

10. Mechanism two - verification and monitoring, and the RO's reporting duties

Part 3 section 5.3 requires the flag State to establish a system to ensure the adequacy of statutory certification and services provided. That system should include procedures for communication with the RO, procedures for reporting by the RO and the processing of those reports, additional ship inspections by the flag State, technical and safety consultations between ROs where certificate validity may be affected, the flag State's evaluation and acceptance of the RO's QMS certification by an independent body of auditors, and monitoring and verification of the statutory work itself.

The reporting requirements are the most concrete thing in Part 3 and should be given as a list:

TriggerWhat the RO should doWhen
A major deficiency or serious safety-related issue that would normally be considered sufficient to detain a ship from proceeding to sea pending correctionNotify the flag StateImmediately on becoming aware
A major non-conformity aboard ship or within a company, as defined in the Guidelines on the Implementation of the ISM Code by AdministrationsNotify the flag State(s)Immediately on becoming aware
Either of the aboveThe notification contains the name of the company or ship, the IMO number, the official number if applicable, and a description of the non-conformity, deficiency or issueWith the notification
Dangerous occurrences, accidents, machinery or structural breakdowns or failures on a shipInform the flag StateAs soon as possible
Removal of a ship from the RO's list of classed or certified ships for which it performed statutory workReport to the flag State in writing, with names, official numbers if applicable and the reasons for removal from classWithin 30 days of the removal becoming effective

The monitoring and verification limb at 5.3 also identifies what the flag State should consider implementing: oversight of the RO's quality management systems; observation of, or systematic review of the reports of, QMS audits conducted by qualified persons or organizations external to and independent of the RO; verification and inspection of ships subject to statutory certification; and a complaint and feedback system with corrective action follow-up. Two further items close a real gap: a flag State accepting ships constructed without its involvement should establish that the RO conducting their statutory work conforms to the Code, and for ships built without an identified flag State, flag-specific requirements should be verified before certification.

11. Mechanism three - auditing

Part 3 section 6 is short and worth knowing precisely. The flag State should be satisfied that the RO has an effective quality management system in place, and may rely upon the audits carried out by an accredited certification body or equivalent organizations; intergovernmental cooperation in establishing common auditing practices is encouraged. A flag State auditor should advance three principles:

  • Ethical conduct - the foundation of professionalism; trust, integrity, confidentiality and discretion are essential to auditing.
  • Fair presentation - the obligation to report truthfully and accurately, so that findings, conclusions and reports reflect the audit activities, with significant obstacles encountered and unresolved diverging opinions between the audit team and the auditee reported.
  • Due professional care - the application of diligence and judgement, in accordance with the importance of the task and the confidence placed in the auditor.

The permission to rely on third-party audits is the practical heart of this section: it is what makes oversight affordable for a flag State that cannot audit every RO itself, and it is also the reason section 7 requires the certifying body to be accredited and independent.

12. Mechanism four - managing the oversight programme

Part 3 section 7 turns the individual activities into a managed programme, and the structure is worth reproducing because it is what 'programme' means:

  • 7.2 Objectives and extent - the objectives of the programme, and its extent, which should reflect the size, nature and complexity of the flag State's authorization programme, the scope, objective and duration of monitoring activities, the frequency of monitoring, the number, importance, complexity, similarity and locations of the ROs, applicable standards and contractual requirements, the need for accreditation or registration, and the conclusions of previous monitoring.
  • 7.3 Responsibilities, resources and procedures - who owns the programme; the financial resources to develop, implement, manage and improve it; auditing techniques; processes to achieve and maintain the competence of staff; the availability of staff and technical experts; documented procedures; implementation; and programme records.
  • 7.4 Monitoring and reviewing - the programme is itself monitored to confirm its objectives are being met, and reviewed periodically.

Note that the flag State's own additional ship inspections sit inside this programme rather than beside it. Inspecting a sample of the RO's ships is not a substitute for oversight; it is one of the programme's monitoring activities and it is planned as such.

13. Mechanism five - the performance indicators

The printed stem asks about continued compliance and performance. Part 3 section 7.4.3 is the performance half, and it gives the flag State five indicators to consider when evaluating the performance of ROs:

  1. port State performance of ROs;
  2. results of the RO's internal audits;
  3. results of quality management system audits performed by third-party organizations;
  4. the results of previous performance monitoring; and
  5. condition and compliance of the ships that receive survey and certification from the ROs.

The Code also turns the mirror on the flag State itself. Section 7.4.4 requires the flag State to evaluate periodically its own performance in implementing the administrative processes, procedures and resources necessary to meet its convention obligations, and 7.4.5 lists measures for doing so, including port State control detention rates, flag State inspection results, casualty statistics, communication and information processes and annual loss statistics excluding constructive total losses, together with further indicators such as fleet loss and accident ratios, detained ships in relation to fleet size, verified cases of incompetence or wrongdoing by certificate holders, responses to port State deficiency reports, investigations into very serious and serious casualties and the lessons learned from them, and the results of inspections, surveys and controls of the fleet.

That last item is the connection worth making in an exam: the same casualty investigations that flow from SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 feed back into how the flag State judges its own performance and its ROs'. Oversight is not a closed administrative loop.

14. What follows a finding, and why the scheme exists at all

Where oversight identifies a shortfall, the flag State's options run along the authorization it granted. Because the authorization was specified under section 8.3, it can be narrowed - by ship type, by convention, by function - as readily as it can be withdrawn, and corrective action can be required under the agreement made under appendix 3. Because the RO's obligations are conditions of the authorization and not courtesies, failure to report a detainable deficiency or a removal from class is itself a compliance failure and not merely poor service.

The closing proposition is the one to end on. The flag State may delegate the work; it cannot delegate the responsibility. The certificates carry the flag State's name, the obligation to the other Contracting Governments remains the flag State's, and the whole of Part 3 exists because an assessment made on the day of appointment says nothing about the state of the organization five years later. Approval under Part 2 and oversight under Part 3 are two halves of one duty, and the Code is built that way on purpose.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The examiner has written the two limbs to match the two mandatory-and-recommendatory halves of the Code, and the single most valuable thing you can do in the first thirty seconds is recognise that: limb (a) is Part 2, limb (b) is Part 3. Once you have that, the answer plans itself and you will not drift into port State control, the III Code or the Member State Audit Scheme - all of which are real, all of which are about flag performance, and none of which is in the RO Code. The stem says 'outlined in the RO code' and it means it.

Limb (a) says 'requirements and procedures'. Most candidates answer the requirements and forget the procedures, and it costs them half the limb. Write it as a numbered sequence - instrument, power to delegate, three assessment steps, the authorization act, the agreement and the IMO notification - and the procedural marks arrive automatically. Limb (b) says 'mechanisms', plural, so give five and number them. A limb (b) that says 'the flag State audits the RO and monitors performance' has named one and a half of five.

The two details most likely to separate a good answer from an average one are the revenue-dependence rule in section 2.3 and the thirty-day report on removal from class in section 5.3. Both are specific, both are quotable, and both show you have read the Code rather than reasoned about what it probably says.

The idea underneath the Code

Every flag State makes the same promise under SOLAS, Load Lines, MARPOL and Tonnage: the ships on its register meet the international standard. Almost no flag State can keep that promise unaided. The classification societies already had the surveyors, the rules, the research departments and the offices in every major port, so the statutory work went to them, and it has gone to them for over a century.

The arrangement has one structural flaw that no amount of good faith removes: the surveyor's fee is paid by the party being surveyed, and the party whose name is on the certificate is often thousands of miles away and has never seen the ship. If the RO is too gentle, the flag State's promise is worthless. If the RO is commercially dependent on one owner, its judgement is not free even if its people are honest. If nobody checks the RO after appointment, an organization that was excellent in 2015 can be inadequate in 2024 and the flag State will not know.

The RO Code addresses all three. It requires structural independence, not merely declared impartiality - which is why the revenue rule exists. It requires an externally certified quality system, so that the assurance does not come from the RO's own opinion of itself. And it builds continuing oversight into the arrangement, so that recognition is a state that must be maintained rather than a status once conferred. Read that way, every section of Part 2 and Part 3 is answering one of those three problems, and the Code stops being a list to memorise.

Recognition, authorization and oversight - the three acts

ActWhere in the CodeWhat it decidesWhat it is not
RecognitionPart 2 sections 2 to 7Is this organization the kind of body that may hold delegated authority at all? Independent, impartial, competent, resourced, with a certified quality systemNot permission to do anything in particular
AuthorizationPart 2 section 8, with appendix 3Which functions, for which ships, under which conventions, may this recognized organization perform for us?Not a general power of attorney - the scope must be specified
OversightPart 3Is it still doing them properly?Not a one-off audit - it is a managed programme with objectives, resources, procedures, records and review

If you can hold this table in your head you can reconstruct most of the answer under pressure, because every provision in the Code belongs to exactly one of these three columns.

The parts of Part 2 that candidates skip, and why they carry marks

  • Section 5.9, subcontracting and service suppliers. An RO that subcontracts thickness measurement or radiographic testing has not thereby escaped the Code, and the flag State's assessment reaches the subcontracting controls.
  • Section 5.11 and 5.12, complaints and appeals. An owner who believes a surveyor has been arbitrary must have a route that is not simply 'change class'. Their presence tells you the Code treats the RO as exercising public authority, not merely selling a service.
  • Section 6.3, the vertical contract audit. A horizontal audit samples processes; a vertical contract audit follows one ship's certification through every process that touched it. It is the audit type that finds the failure a process audit misses.
  • Section 6.5, non-conformities including statutory deficiencies. The RO must measure and control the deficiencies found on the ships it certified, not only the faults in its own procedures. This is the provision that makes the RO's quality data useful to the flag State.
  • Section 8.5, instruments. The flag State must tell the RO in advance when national law changes and must say whether its standards go beyond convention requirements. An RO cannot enforce a national requirement it was never told about, and a flag State that has not said so cannot complain that it was not enforced.

How Part 3 hangs together

Read Part 3 as answering four questions in order. What can we see? - section 5.2, the five access items. What must they tell us, and when? - section 5.3, the reporting triggers, with the thirty-day rule as the only hard interval. How do we test it? - section 6, auditing, with permission to rely on accredited third-party audits and three principles for the flag State's own auditors. How do we run this as a programme rather than as a series of visits? - section 7, with objectives, extent, responsibilities, resources, procedures, records, review, and the five performance indicators at 7.4.3.

The elegance of section 7.4.3 is that four of the five indicators are things the flag State does not have to generate itself: port State performance comes from the MOU regimes, internal audit results and third-party QMS audit results come from the RO and its certifier, and previous performance monitoring is its own history. Only the fifth - the condition and compliance of the ships - requires the flag State to go and look. That is a deliberate design: it makes an effective oversight programme achievable for an administration with modest resources, which is precisely the administration most likely to have delegated everything.

The evidence position on this question, stated plainly

The corpus copy of the RO Code is a 61-page scan with no text layer at all - a text extraction returns zero characters. It would have been legitimate to treat this question as the paper's weakest and answer it at policy level. That is not what was done. The pages were rendered and read at source this session, which is the same read-back method MIW requires for verifying any source, and the section structure, the section numbers and the substance in this answer are taken from that reading.

Two limits remain and are stated in the answer object rather than hidden. First, the held copy is the base Code as adopted by MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65); no amendment is held and none is asserted in either direction, because a copy that predates an amendment cannot prove the amendment does not exist. Second, nothing is quoted from a page that was not read. If you are revising from this answer, the section numbers are reliable; the correct posture on RO Code amendments is that MIW has not established the position, not that there have been none.

Examiner traps

  • Answering limb (b) with port State control. The commonest error. PSC is a port State mechanism; the stem asks for flag State oversight mechanisms in the RO Code. PSC appears legitimately only where the Code itself uses it - as a performance indicator at 7.4.3.1 and in the appendix 3 requirement that the RO cooperate with port States to rectify reported deficiencies.
  • Treating recognition and authorization as one act. If they were one act, Part 2 section 8 would not exist. Say that the scope must be specified and give two or three of the nine specifications.
  • Saying Part 3 is mandatory. It is not, and its own scope paragraph says so. But do not overcorrect into saying it does not matter: the adopting resolution recommends it as the basis for national standards unless national requirements are at least equivalent.
  • Inventing an amendment. Do not attribute a date or a resolution number to an RO Code amendment. If you do not know, describe the Code as adopted and effective 1 January 2015 and move on.
  • Naming a class society as an example and then describing IACS. The stem is about the RO Code. IACS is a trade association of classification societies and is not a party to any authorization; its quality scheme appears in the Code only as one of the referenced documents.
  • Forgetting the IMO notification. The list of ROs deposited with the Secretary-General, together with the specific responsibilities and conditions of the delegated authority, is a mandatory procedural step and is a cheap mark for anyone who remembers it.

If you only have ten minutes for this question

Write eight lines for limb (a) and eight for limb (b). Limb (a): the Code and its three parts, with Part 2 mandatory and Part 3 recommendatory; the flag State may delegate but not beyond the RO's capabilities; the organization is assessed against the general requirements - independence, impartiality, integrity, competence, responsibility, transparency, published rules; its management, resources, processes and performance measurement are assessed; its quality system must be certified by an independent accredited body; the flag State then authorizes and specifies the scope, the legal basis, the instruments, the instructions and the records; a formal written agreement is concluded; and the list of ROs and the conditions of delegation are deposited with the IMO Secretary-General.

Limb (b): Part 3 is guidance; the flag State supervises the delegated duties through five access items; it establishes a verification and monitoring system under which the RO reports detainable deficiencies and major non-conformities immediately and removals from class within thirty days; it audits, or relies on accredited third-party audits, applying ethical conduct, fair presentation and due professional care; it runs this as a managed programme with objectives, extent, resources, procedures, records and review; and it evaluates RO performance against five indicators - port State performance, RO internal audits, third-party QMS audits, previous monitoring, and the condition of the certified ships. Close with the one sentence that ties both limbs together: the flag State can delegate the work but not the responsibility.

Common mistakes

These are the places this particular question goes wrong, taken from the ambiguities identified before drafting and from the single error most likely to cost marks.

The one that costs most: Answering limb (b) with port State control, the III Code or the IMO Member State Audit Scheme. Those are real mechanisms and none of them is IN THE RO CODE, which is what the stem asked for. The second trap is treating recognition and authorization as one act - they are two, and section 8 exists precisely because an organization can be recognized as competent in general and still not be authorized for a particular ship type or convention.

The wording of the stem invites four specific misreadings, and each is resolved in the answer rather than left to chance:

  • 'Approve' is not the Code's word. The Code distinguishes RECOGNITION - assessing an organization against Part 2 - from AUTHORIZATION, the delegation of specified functions to an already recognized organization. The answer uses the Code's two words and says why the distinction matters, because a candidate who treats approval as a single act cannot explain why section 8 exists separately from section 2.
  • 'Mandatory requirements' could be read as the requirements on the RO only. Part 2 binds BOTH: scope 2.2 records mandatory requirements for the organization to be recognized, mandatory requirements for the RO when performing services, and mandatory requirements that FLAG STATES shall adhere to when authorizing. The answer says so, because limb (a) asks what the flag State must do.
  • 'Oversight mechanisms outlined in the RO code' is a restriction, not a topic. Port State control, the III Code and the IMO Member State Audit Scheme are all real flag-performance mechanisms and none of them is in the RO Code. They are not written, because the examiner named the instrument.
  • The Code's own scope paragraph 2.2.1 refers to the mandatory requirements for recognition as being in 'part 1' where the structure places them in part 2. The answer follows the STRUCTURE, which is unambiguous from the Contents and from part 2's own heading, and does not reproduce the cross-reference.

Likely oral follow-up

The retrieval questions below are the ones this answer is built to survive. They are the same questions an examiner reaches for when testing whether a written answer was understood or reproduced.

  • Give the route for the RO Code approval and oversight question. — 8 + 8. Limb (a) is a procedure and must read as a sequence; limb (b) is five countable mechanisms, and candidates who name only auditing lose most of the limb.
  • RO Code: recognition or authorization - what is the difference? — The single most common structural error on this question. An organization can be recognized and still not be authorized for a given ship type or convention.
  • Which parts of the RO Code are mandatory, and what did the adopting resolution say about the rest? — Limb (b) asks for the mechanisms 'outlined in the RO code'. Saying that Part 3 is guidance is a mark, not a hedge - it is the reason the Code says 'should' where Part 2 says 'shall'.
  • The five items in the flag State's supervision of duties delegated to an RO. — A countable list in a limb that most candidates answer in generalities.
  • The five performance indicators the flag State should use to evaluate an RO. — 'Continued compliance AND PERFORMANCE' is in the printed stem. This list is the performance half and it is where the discriminating marks are.
  • What must the RO report to the flag State, and how quickly? — The thirty-day rule is the only hard interval in Part 3 and it is the most quotable thing in limb (b).
  • Why does the RO Code care so much about independence and revenue? — This is the structural weakness the whole Code exists to control - the surveyor is paid by the surveyed - and naming the revenue-dependence rule shows you understand why.
  • What must a formal written agreement between the flag State and the RO cover? — The stem asks for PROCEDURES. The agreement is the procedural instrument that turns an assessment into an authorization, and appendix 3 is the checkable answer.

Memory framework

The route, chunked: Limb (a) is a PROCEDURE: WHAT (1), MAY (2), then three tests - WHO they are (3), HOW they run (4), WHO CHECKS THEM (5) - then AUTHORIZE (6) and SIGN AND TELL IMO (7). Limb (b) is FIVE MECHANISMS: SUPERVISE (9), VERIFY AND REPORT (10), AUDIT (11), MANAGE (12), MEASURE (13) - bracketed by STATUS (8) and CONSEQUENCE (14).

Fifteen-second recall: RO Code: MSC.349(92) + MEPC.237(65), effective 1 Jan 2015, mandatory via SOLAS XI-1/1. Part 1 general, Part 2 MANDATORY (recognition + authorization), Part 3 RECOMMENDATORY (oversight). Approve = recognize against Part 2 sections 2-7, then authorize under section 8 specifying scope, then written agreement (appendix 3) and notify IMO (Part 1 section 5). Oversee = supervise (5.2), verify and monitor with RO reporting (5.3), audit (6), manage the programme (7), measure against five performance indicators (7.4.3).

The numbers and thresholds worth carrying exactly:

  • RO Code adopted by resolutions MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65); took effect 1 January 2015
  • Three parts: Part 1 general, Part 2 mandatory, Part 3 recommendatory guidance on oversight
  • Part 2 section 2 - eight general requirements, 2.1 to 2.8
  • Part 2 section 8.3 - nine specifications of the scope of authorization
  • Part 3 section 5.2 - five items in the flag State's supervision of delegated duties
  • Part 3 section 5.3 - removal from the RO's list of classed/certified ships reported in writing within 30 days
  • Part 3 section 6.2 - three auditor principles: ethical conduct, fair presentation, due professional care
  • Part 3 section 7.4.3 - five performance indicators

Keywords that unlock the rest: RO Code, MSC.349(92), MEPC.237(65), recognition, authorization, SOLAS XI-1/1, Part 2 mandatory, Part 3 recommendatory, independence, impartiality, quality management system certification, specification of authorization, oversight programme, vertical contract audit, performance indicators, written agreement

Uncertainty and evidence limits

What this answer deliberately does not assert, and why. These are declared limits rather than omissions, and a candidate revising from this answer should know where MIW stopped.

  • THE HELD COPY IS THE BASE CODE AS ADOPTED, NOT A CONSOLIDATION. The corpus holds the RO Code as adopted by MSC.349(92) / MEPC.237(65). NO AMENDMENT TO THE RO CODE IS HELD AND NONE IS ASSERTED anywhere in this answer. Whether a later amendment exists cannot be established from a copy that would not contain it. The corpus records this as open item RQ-25; this session narrows it - the file is complete through Part 3 section 7.4 and all three appendices - but does not close it.
  • NO CLAUSE OF THE RO CODE IS QUOTED FROM A PAGE THAT WAS NOT READ. The pages read at source this session are enumerated in the sources above. Section numbers elsewhere in the Code are given from the Contents pages, which were read, and are given as structure rather than as content.
  • NO IACS DOCUMENT IS CITED. MIW holds no IACS text. The RO Code's own section 6 reference to the IACS Quality System Certification Scheme is recorded as a reference within the Code; no IACS unified requirement, procedural requirement or quality-scheme provision is described or quoted.
  • NO INDIAN INSTRUMENT IS CITED. The stem asks about the flag State generically. DG Shipping's RO agreements and the Merchant Shipping Act are not described, and the Merchant Shipping Act 2025 - which commenced 15 March 2026, nineteen months after this sitting - does not appear.
  • NO NUMBER OF RECOGNIZED ORGANIZATIONS, no list of authorized ROs and no audit or detention statistic is asserted. None can be sourced and MIW's evidence-declaration standard requires an unverifiable quantity to be omitted rather than given false precision.

Flashcards 8

Limb (a): 1 what the Code is. 2 the power to delegate and its limit. 3 general requirements. 4 organization and resources. 5 QMS certification. 6 the authorization act. 7 agreement and IMO notification. Limb (b): 8 status of Part 3. 9 supervision. 10 verification, monitoring and RO reporting. 11 auditing. 12 managing the programme. 13 performance indicators. 14 consequence.

8 + 8. Limb (a) is a procedure and must read as a sequence; limb (b) is five countable mechanisms, and candidates who name only auditing lose most of the limb.

RECOGNITION is the flag State's assessment of an organization against the mandatory requirements of Part 2 - that it is independent, impartial, competent, properly resourced and operating a certified quality management system. AUTHORIZATION is the separate act of delegating SPECIFIED statutory functions to an organization already recognized, and Part 2 section 8 requires the flag State to specify the scope: ship types and sizes, conventions and national legislation, drawing approval, material and equipment approval, surveys, certificate issuance, corrective actions, withdrawal of certificates and reporting.

The single most common structural error on this question. An organization can be recognized and still not be authorized for a given ship type or convention.

Part 2 is mandatory - recognition and authorization requirements binding on both the organization and the flag State. Part 3, oversight of recognized organizations, is expressly non-mandatory guidance; its own scope paragraph says so, and the adopting resolution recommends Governments use the recommendatory provisions of part 3 as a basis for relevant standards unless their national requirements provide at least an equivalent degree of safety.

Limb (b) asks for the mechanisms 'outlined in the RO code'. Saying that Part 3 is guidance is a mark, not a hedge - it is the reason the Code says 'should' where Part 2 says 'shall'.

Part 3 section 5.2: documentation of the RO's quality management system; access to internal instructions, circulars and guidelines; access to the RO's documentation relevant to the flag State's fleet; cooperation with the flag State's inspection and verification work; and provision of information and statistics such as damage and casualties relevant to the flag State's fleet.

A countable list in a limb that most candidates answer in generalities.

Part 3 section 7.4.3: port State performance of ROs; results of the RO's internal audits; results of quality management system audits performed by third-party organizations; the results of previous performance monitoring; and the condition and compliance of ships that receive survey and certification from the RO.

'Continued compliance AND PERFORMANCE' is in the printed stem. This list is the performance half and it is where the discriminating marks are.

Part 3 section 5.3: immediately on becoming aware of a major deficiency or serious safety-related issue that would normally be considered sufficient to detain a ship; immediately on becoming aware of a major non-conformity aboard a ship or within a company; as soon as possible, any dangerous occurrence, accident, machinery or structural breakdown or failure it is aware of; and in writing within THIRTY DAYS, the names of any ships removed from the RO's list of classed or certified ships, with the reasons.

The thirty-day rule is the only hard interval in Part 3 and it is the most quotable thing in limb (b).

Part 2 section 2.3 requires that the RO and its staff not engage in activities that may conflict with their independence of judgement and integrity, that those carrying out statutory work not be the designer, manufacturer, supplier, installer, purchaser, owner, user or maintainer of the item being certified nor the authorized representative of any of those parties, and that the RO NOT BE SUBSTANTIALLY DEPENDENT ON A SINGLE COMMERCIAL ENTERPRISE FOR ITS REVENUE. Section 2.4 adds that personnel shall be free from pressures that might affect their judgement.

This is the structural weakness the whole Code exists to control - the surveyor is paid by the surveyed - and naming the revenue-dependence rule shows you understand why.

RO Code appendix 3 gives eight minimum headings: application; purpose; general conditions; the execution of functions under authorization; the legal basis of those functions; reporting to the flag State; development of rules and regulations and the exchange of information; and other conditions.

The stem asks for PROCEDURES. The agreement is the procedural instrument that turns an assessment into an authorization, and appendix 3 is the checkable answer.

Also on the platform: QP2401 Q5 - IACS structure and the RO Code (content-family adjacency) · QP2408 Q3 - flag State casualty investigation duty · QP2408 Q9 - the ISM Code, which an RO also certifies

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

Think about what the law could and could not reach before this Convention existed. The old crime of piracy was built around a picture of one ship attacking another for private gain, on the high seas. That picture leaves an enormous hole. If the people who seize a ship are already on board - passengers, or crew - there is no second ship. If their motive is political rather than plunder, it is not for private ends. And if they leave the ship in a port and step ashore in a State that has no connection to the incident at all, that State may have no law under which to try them and no obligation to hand them over. A ship could be taken by force, people could be killed, and the offenders could end up somewhere that could not prosecute and did not have to extradite. The answer was to stop trying to stretch piracy and instead write a treaty that does three things in sequence: it defines the conduct - seizing a ship by force, violence on board that endangers her safe navigation, damaging her or planting something that will, interfering with navigational aids, sending false signals; it makes every Party turn that conduct into a crime in its own law with serious penalties and wide jurisdiction; and it removes the escape route by requiring a Party that finds the offender on its soil to either send him for trial elsewhere or try him itself. Later, after attacks that showed the ship could be the weapon rather than the target, the treaty was extended to cover using a ship to cause death or damage, carrying weapons of mass destruction or their materials, and carrying fugitives - and it added a procedure for one State to board another State's ship with that State's consent. It is worth keeping this separate in your head from the shipboard security regime: that one is about keeping the wrong people off the ship in the first place, and this one is about what happens legally once they are on.
