Model written answer

(a) Primary objectives, key provisions, and how they enhance maritime security

A note on sources before the answer begins. MIW holds no licensed copy of the SUA Convention or of either 2005 Protocol. Every article below is therefore identified by number and by effect; nothing is quoted, and no sub-paragraph numbering below article level is asserted. That is a deliberate limit, not an omission.

1. The gap in the law that the Convention was written to close

The Convention is unintelligible until you see what it was for. The pre-existing international crime was piracy, and piracy is defined around a particular picture: one ship attacking another, on the high seas, for private ends. Three features of that definition leave a hole large enough to sail a passenger ship through.

  • No second ship. If those who seize the vessel are already on board - as passengers or as crew - there is no attacking vessel and the two-ship requirement fails.
  • Not private ends. If the motive is political rather than plunder, the conduct falls outside the definition however violent it is.
  • No duty on anyone. Even where the conduct was criminal under some national law, no treaty obliged the State in which the offenders surfaced either to try them or to hand them over.

The Achille Lauro seizure in 1985 demonstrated all three at once, and the diplomatic aftermath - in which the question of who could prosecute, and who was obliged to, had no clear answer - is what produced the Convention. The response was not to stretch piracy but to write a new instrument built on conduct rather than on the relationship between two vessels.

2. The instruments and their dates

InstrumentAdoptedIn forceWhat it does
SUA Convention 19881988, at Rome1992Creates the principal offences and the State obligations
Fixed Platforms Protocol 198819881992Extends the scheme to fixed platforms on the continental shelf
Protocol of 2005 to the Convention20052010Adds articles 3bis, 3ter and 3quater and the article 8bis boarding regime
Protocol of 2005 to the Fixed Platforms Protocol20052010The corresponding extension for fixed platforms

Note the plural. The stem writes 'its 2005 Protocol' in the singular; there are in fact two, and the one that matters here is the Protocol to the Convention. The fixed-platform companion is mentioned once for completeness and not pursued.

The 2005 Protocol operates by amending the 1988 Convention rather than standing alone, which is why the new offences carry bis, ter and quater numbering inside the original article 3 sequence. As between Parties to the Protocol, the Convention and the Protocol are read as a single instrument.

3. The primary objectives

  1. To ensure that specified unlawful acts against the safety of maritime navigation are criminal offences in the law of every Party, so that the conduct is punishable wherever the offender is found rather than only where the ship happens to be registered.
  2. To ensure that appropriate action is taken against the offenders - which in practice means removing the possibility of a safe haven, by obliging a Party either to extradite or to prosecute.
  3. To establish a framework of international cooperation: an agreed allocation of jurisdiction, agreed procedures for custody and inquiry, extradition and mutual legal assistance, and the sharing of information through IMO.
  4. To deter, by making it predictable that such conduct will be prosecuted somewhere, and after 2005, to prevent the use of ships as weapons or as delivery vehicles for weapons of mass destruction.

The Convention is therefore best described as a criminal-law and jurisdiction treaty. It imposes no construction standard, no equipment requirement and no shipboard procedure, and a candidate who describes it as though it did has misidentified the instrument.

4. Where the Convention applies

Application is governed by article 4 and is keyed to the ship's navigation rather than to the location of the crime. In substance, the Convention applies where the ship is navigating or is scheduled to navigate into, through or from waters beyond the outer limit of the territorial sea of a single State, or the lateral limits of its territorial sea with adjacent States. Where that is not the case, the Convention nevertheless applies when the offender or alleged offender is found in the territory of a Party other than the State in whose waters the ship was navigating.

The practical effect is that the Convention reaches the internationally trading ship and does not intrude on a State's purely internal waterborne traffic. Warships, ships being used as naval auxiliaries or for customs or police purposes, and ships withdrawn from navigation or laid up, fall outside it.

5. Key provisions one - the offences created in 1988

Article 3 is the offence-creating article. A person commits an offence if that person unlawfully and intentionally:

  1. seizes or exercises control over a ship by force or threat of force or any other form of intimidation;
  2. performs an act of violence against a person on board a ship, if that act is likely to endanger the safe navigation of that ship;
  3. destroys a ship or causes damage to a ship or to her cargo which is likely to endanger the safe navigation of that ship;
  4. places or causes to be placed on a ship a device or substance which is likely to destroy that ship or cause damage to her or her cargo which endangers or is likely to endanger her safe navigation;
  5. destroys or seriously damages maritime navigational facilities, or seriously interferes with their operation, if that is likely to endanger the safe navigation of a ship;
  6. communicates information which he knows to be false, thereby endangering the safe navigation of a ship; or
  7. injures or kills any person in connection with the commission or attempted commission of any of the above offences.

Article 3.2 extends liability to attempting any of these, to abetting such an offence or otherwise being an accomplice, and to threatening to commit certain of them where the threat is likely to endanger the ship's safe navigation.

The qualifying phrase is the key to the whole article. Most limbs are tied to conduct that is likely to endanger the safe navigation of the ship. That is what distinguishes a SUA offence from ordinary shipboard crime: a theft or an assault on board is a matter for the flag State's general criminal law, but an assault that puts the ship's navigation in danger engages the Convention and with it the whole international machinery. Say this in the exam - it is the difference between listing the offences and understanding them.

6. Key provisions two - what the 2005 Protocol added

ArticleWhat it covers
Article 3bisThe ship as a weapon and as a carrier. Using a ship in a manner that causes death, serious injury or damage, or discharging from a ship any explosive, radioactive material, biological, chemical or nuclear weapon or other hazardous substance in a manner that causes or is likely to cause death, serious injury or damage - where the purpose is to intimidate a population or compel a government or international organization to do or abstain from an act. It also covers the transport of such weapons and materials, of certain dual-use items intended for a nuclear weapons programme, and of equipment or software for that purpose
Article 3terTransporting a fugitive - unlawfully and intentionally transporting a person on board knowing that they have committed a listed offence and intending to assist them to evade criminal prosecution
Article 3quaterInchoate and secondary liability for the new offences - attempt, participation as an accomplice, organizing or directing others, and contributing to a group acting with a common purpose
Article 8bisThe boarding regime - dealt with at section 14, because it is enforcement rather than offence-creation

The analytical point the examiner is reaching for is a single sentence: 1988 treats the ship as the target; 2005 treats the ship as the weapon or the delivery vehicle. The 1988 offences all describe harm done to a ship or on board her; the 2005 offences describe harm done by means of a ship to people and places that may be nowhere near the sea. That shift tracks the change in the threat picture between the mid-1980s and the years after 2001, and it is why the proliferation limb - carrying the material rather than using it - appears at all.

7. How the scheme aims to enhance maritime security

Four mechanisms, and they are worth separating because 'it makes shipping safer' is not an answer.

  • It closes the impunity gap. Because every Party must criminalize the conduct and must either extradite or prosecute, there is no longer a jurisdiction in which an offender can simply arrive and be beyond reach. That is the Convention's central contribution.
  • It deters. Certainty of prosecution somewhere is a stronger deterrent than severity of punishment in one place, and the Convention manufactures that certainty.
  • It makes cooperation routine rather than negotiated. Jurisdiction, custody, inquiry, notification, extradition and evidence-sharing are settled in advance, so States are not improvising a legal relationship in the middle of an incident - which is precisely what went wrong in 1985.
  • Since 2005, it enables interdiction. The article 8bis procedure allows a suspect ship to be boarded at sea with the flag State's authorization, which moves the regime from punishing an act to preventing one.

The contrast that makes this clear is with the ISPS Code. They are complementary and neither substitutes for the other:

SUA ConventionISPS Code / SOLAS chapter XI-2
NatureCriminal law and jurisdictionPreventive security management
OperatesAfter the act, and across bordersBefore the act, on board and in the port facility
Addressed toStates - their legislatures, prosecutors and courtsShips and port facilities - operators, masters, security officers
MachineryOffences, jurisdiction, custody, extradition, prosecutionSecurity assessments and plans, SSO and CSO, security levels, access control, ship security alert system, declaration of security
Enforced byNational courts, with treaty obligations behind themFlag State certification and port State control

An answer that describes ship security plans and security levels under the heading of SUA has answered the wrong question. Naming the difference explicitly is what earns the analytical marks in limb (a).

(b) The legal obligations and enforcement mechanisms of States Parties

Limb (b) is not a second pass over the offences. It asks what a Party must do, and the answer is six obligations plus one enforcement procedure, each tied to its article.

8. Obligation one - criminalize and penalize (article 5)

Each Party shall make the offences punishable by appropriate penalties which take into account the grave nature of those offences. This is the foundation and everything else rests on it: extradition presupposes that the conduct is an offence in both States, and prosecution presupposes an offence in the prosecuting State's own law. A Party that has ratified but never legislated has not implemented the Convention, whatever its formal treaty position.

The obligation is one of result and seriousness, not of form. The Convention does not prescribe a sentence, a maximum or a statutory drafting technique; it requires that the penalty reflect gravity, which is what prevents a Party from nominally complying by treating an armed seizure as a minor regulatory infraction.

9. Obligation two - establish jurisdiction (article 6)

Article 6 requires each Party to take such measures as may be necessary to establish jurisdiction, and it does so in three layers.

LayerBasisStatus
MandatoryThe offence is committed against or on board a ship flying the flag of that State at the timeShall establish
MandatoryThe offence is committed in the territory of that State, including its territorial seaShall establish
MandatoryThe offence is committed by a national of that StateShall establish
OptionalA stateless person whose habitual residence is in that State commits the offence; a national of that State is seized, threatened, injured or killed during the offence; or the offence is committed in an attempt to compel that State to do or abstain from an actMay establish, on notification to the Secretary-General
Residual and mandatoryThe alleged offender is present in its territory and it does not extradite them to any Party that has established jurisdictionShall establish - the provision that makes article 10 workable

The residual layer is the one to underline. Without it, a Party could find itself obliged to prosecute under article 10 and lack the jurisdiction in its own law to do so. Article 6 and article 10 are designed to interlock, and explaining that interlock is worth more than reciting either alone. The Convention does not exclude any criminal jurisdiction exercised in accordance with national law.

10. Obligation three - custody, preliminary inquiry and notification (article 7)

Where an alleged offender is present in a Party's territory, that Party shall, on being satisfied that the circumstances so warrant, take them into custody or take other measures to ensure their presence for such time as is necessary to enable criminal or extradition proceedings to be instituted. It shall immediately make a preliminary inquiry into the facts.

Three protections and duties run alongside:

  • The person in custody is entitled to communicate without delay with the nearest appropriate representative of the State of which they are a national, or which is otherwise entitled to protect their rights, and to be visited by a representative of that State.
  • The Party shall immediately notify the States that have established jurisdiction, and the other interested States, of the fact that the person is in custody and of the circumstances warranting detention.
  • The Party conducting the preliminary inquiry shall promptly report its findings to those States and indicate whether it intends to exercise jurisdiction.

That last requirement is easy to skip and is doing real work: it forces a Party to declare its intention rather than sit on a detained suspect, which is what allows other interested States to decide whether to seek extradition.

11. Obligation four - extradite or prosecute (article 10)

This is the provision on which the Convention turns. The Party in whose territory the alleged offender is found shall, if it does not extradite them, be obliged without exception whatsoever and whether or not the offence was committed in its territory, to submit the case without delay to its competent authorities for the purpose of prosecution, through proceedings in accordance with its own law. Those authorities take their decision in the same manner as for any other offence of a grave nature under that State's law.

This is the principle known as aut dedere aut judicare - extradite or prosecute. Its practical effect is the abolition of the safe haven, and it is the direct answer to the 1985 problem. Note what it does not do: it does not oblige a State to convict, and it does not remove prosecutorial discretion. It obliges the State to put the case into the system and to treat it with the seriousness it would give any grave offence of its own. Article 10 also requires fair treatment at all stages, including enjoyment of the rights and guarantees provided by the law of the State in whose territory the person is present.

12. Obligation five - extradition and mutual legal assistance (articles 11, 12 and 8)

  • Article 11 - extradition. The offences are deemed to be included as extraditable offences in any extradition treaty existing between Parties, and Parties undertake to include them in every treaty concluded between them. A Party that makes extradition conditional on the existence of a treaty may consider this Convention as the legal basis for extradition in respect of these offences. For the purposes of extradition, the offences are treated as if committed not only where they occurred but also in a place within the jurisdiction of the requesting Party.
  • Article 12 - mutual legal assistance. Parties shall afford one another the greatest measure of assistance in connection with criminal proceedings brought in respect of the offences, including assistance in obtaining evidence at their disposal that is necessary for the proceedings.
  • Article 8 - the master's power of delivery. The master of a ship of one Party may deliver to the authorities of any other Party a person whom he has reasonable grounds to believe has committed one of the offences, having given notification of his intention and the reasons, and furnished the evidence in his possession. The receiving Party shall accept the delivery except where it has grounds to consider the Convention inapplicable, in which case it gives reasons.

Article 8 is the provision that connects the treaty to the ship. It is the only place in the Convention where a duty falls on a master rather than on a State, and it is the mechanism by which an offender detained on board is transferred into the criminal process. A Chief Engineer's practical interest in this Convention is largely here: the ship secures the person and the evidence, and the master hands both over.

13. Obligation six - prevention, cooperation and reporting (articles 13 and 14)

  • Article 13 - Parties shall cooperate in the prevention of the offences, particularly by taking all practicable measures to prevent preparations in their respective territories for the commission of those offences within or outside their territories, and by exchanging information in accordance with their national law and coordinating administrative and other measures to prevent the offences.
  • Article 14 - any Party having reason to believe that an offence will be committed shall, in accordance with its national law, furnish as promptly as possible any relevant information in its possession to those States which it believes would be the States having established jurisdiction. Parties also report to the Secretary-General of IMO information concerning the offences and the proceedings taken, which the Secretary-General circulates.

The reporting duty is what converts individual national prosecutions into a shared picture. It is the same architectural idea that appears in SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 and the Casualty Investigation Code - the incident is dealt with nationally, and the lesson is shared through IMO - and drawing that parallel is a legitimate way to show breadth in a paper that asks about both.

14. The enforcement mechanism at sea - boarding under article 8bis

The 2005 Protocol added the only mechanism in the Convention that operates on the water rather than in a courtroom, and it must be stated with its limits or it becomes wrong.

  1. A Party that suspects a listed offence is being, has been or is about to be committed involving a ship flying another Party's flag may request confirmation of nationality from the flag State.
  2. If nationality is confirmed, the requesting Party shall request authorization from the flag State to board the ship, search her and question those on board to determine whether an offence has been or is about to be committed.
  3. The flag State decides. It may authorize the boarding; it may conduct the boarding itself; it may do so jointly with the requesting Party; or it may decline. It may attach conditions.
  4. A Party may notify the Secretary-General that it grants standing authorization - either that boarding is authorized if no reply is received within a stated period, or that boarding and search are authorized generally.
  5. Safeguards apply. The Convention requires due regard for the safety of life at sea and the safety of the ship and cargo, for the commercial and legal interests of the flag State, and for the environment; that the use of force be avoided except when necessary for the safety of those on board and no more than reasonably necessary in the circumstances; that persons on board be treated with due regard for human rights and human dignity; and that a Party is liable for damage, harm or loss attributable to it where the grounds prove unfounded or the measures were unlawful or exceeded what was reasonably required.

The flag State's primacy is preserved throughout, and saying so is what separates a correct answer from an overstated one. Article 8bis creates a structured route to consent; it does not create a right of interdiction. Nothing in it affects the jurisdiction, rights or obligations of States under the general law of the sea.

Read as a whole, then, the Convention gives a Party a complete sequence: it must make the conduct criminal and punish it seriously, take jurisdiction on several bases, secure and inquire into a suspect found on its soil, tell the interested States what it intends, and either send them for trial or try them itself - and, since 2005, it may ask a flag State for permission to stop the ship before any of that becomes necessary. The offences give the Convention its reach; the obligations give it its teeth.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The two limbs are printed at 8 and 8, and the single most expensive mistake on this question is to answer the offences twice. Limb (a) asks for objectives and key provisions - that is where the offences live, because the offences ARE the Convention's key provisions. Limb (b) asks for legal obligations and enforcement mechanisms that signatory states must implement - that is articles 5, 6, 7, 8, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14 and 8bis, and if you have spent limb (b) re-describing article 3 you have written half a paper.

Give limb (a) an opening line about the gap piracy leaves. It costs two sentences and it makes everything after it read as purposive rather than descriptive. Then number the article 3 offences - all seven, not a selection, because a partial list reads as partial knowledge on a question where the examiner asked for the key provisions. Then close limb (a) with the ISPS contrast, which is where the word 'how' in the printed stem is pointing.

Limb (b) should be written as six numbered duties plus the boarding procedure. Numbering matters here more than usual: the examiner is looking for coverage across the obligation articles, and a flowing paragraph that mentions extradition and jurisdiction without separating them will be marked as one point, not two.

The idea underneath the Convention

A treaty of this kind is doing something quite specific and rather elegant. It does not create an international court, it does not create an international police force, and it does not give any State a right to act on another's ships. What it does is make national legal systems interoperable for one class of conduct. Every Party writes the same offences into its own criminal law; every Party takes jurisdiction on the same agreed bases; every Party agrees in advance to the same procedure for custody, inquiry and notification; and every Party accepts that if the offender turns up on its territory it will either hand him over or try him.

The result is that the offender can no longer arbitrage the differences between legal systems. Before 1988 the practical question after an incident was 'is there anywhere that both can and must deal with this?' and the answer was often no. After 1988 the answer is always yes, and the only remaining question is which Party. That is a smaller-sounding achievement than it is: the removal of the safe haven is the whole point, and everything in the Convention is either defining the conduct that triggers it or building the machinery that delivers it.

The 2005 extension follows the same logic applied to a changed threat. Once you accept that a ship can be a weapon or a means of moving weapons, the same three questions arise - what conduct, whose jurisdiction, and who is obliged to act - and the Protocol answers them the same way, adding one genuinely new thing: a way to get on board before the harm occurs, with the flag State's consent.

The offence structure, and the phrase that ties it together

Article 3's seven limbs look like a miscellany until you notice that almost all of them end in the same qualification: the conduct must be likely to endanger the safe navigation of the ship. That is the organising principle, and it explains both what is in and what is out.

ConductIn or outWhy
Armed seizure of the shipInSeizure by force is an offence in itself - no endangerment qualification is needed
Assault on a crew member in a bar ashoreOutNot on board, and not connected with the ship's navigation
Assault on the officer of the watch under wayInAn act of violence on board likely to endanger safe navigation
Stealing a laptop from a cabinOutOrdinary theft - engages the flag State's general criminal law, not the Convention
Cutting the ship's steering control cablesInDamage likely to endanger safe navigation
A hoax distress or navigational broadcast that puts a ship in dangerInCommunicating information known to be false, thereby endangering safe navigation
Damaging a lighthouse or a buoyed channel's markersInDestruction of, or serious interference with, maritime navigational facilities

If you can apply that test to an unseen scenario you can answer any variant of this question, including the ones that give you a fact pattern rather than asking for a list.

How the obligation articles interlock

Learn these five as a chain rather than as a list, because they were drafted to work in sequence and the sequence is what an examiner recognises as understanding.

  1. Article 5 makes the conduct a crime in national law. Without this, nothing downstream is possible.
  2. Article 6 gives the State the jurisdiction to try it - including, crucially, the residual head that applies precisely when the State would not otherwise have any connection to the offence.
  3. Article 7 secures the person and establishes the facts, and requires the State to say what it intends to do.
  4. Article 11 and 12 make it possible to send the case elsewhere - extraditable offences, the Convention itself as a legal basis, and assistance with evidence.
  5. Article 10 closes the loop: if you do not send him, you must submit the case for prosecution yourself.

Notice that article 10 would be unenforceable without article 6's residual head, and article 6's residual head would be pointless without article 10. That mutual dependence is the most sophisticated observation available on this question and it takes one sentence.

Article 8bis - stating it correctly

This is where good candidates overreach. The temptation is to describe article 8bis as a right to board suspicious ships, and that is simply wrong. The structure is: request confirmation of nationality → request authorization → the flag State decides. The flag State may board itself, board jointly, authorize, impose conditions, or refuse. The only softening is the standing authorization a Party may notify to the Secretary-General - either a general authorization or one that operates if no reply is received within a stated period - and even that is the flag State's own choice, made in advance.

The safeguards are worth a line because they show why the provision was acceptable to flag States at all: safety of life, of the ship and of the cargo; the commercial and legal interests of the flag State; avoidance of force except where necessary and then no more than reasonable; human rights and dignity of those on board; and liability for damage where the grounds prove unfounded. Sovereignty over ships is not a technicality to a flag State, and article 8bis was negotiated in that knowledge.

The evidence position on this question, stated plainly

MIW holds no copy of the SUA Convention or of either 2005 Protocol. That is why this answer names articles and describes their effect but quotes nothing and asserts no sub-paragraph numbering below article level, and why it gives adoption and entry-into-force as years rather than as full dates. It is also why no number of States Parties appears, no percentage of world tonnage, and no Indian implementing statute or section.

If you are revising from this answer, treat the article numbers as reliable at article level and the substance as reliable in effect. If you need to quote the Convention - in a dissertation, or in professional work - obtain the text. In the examination room, naming the article and stating what it does is exactly what earns the mark; quoting it earns nothing extra and inventing a sub-paragraph number can cost you.

Examiner traps

  • Describing ISPS. Ship security plans, security levels, the SSO, the CSO, the declaration of security and the ship security alert system are SOLAS chapter XI-2 and the ISPS Code. None of them is in the SUA Convention. Mentioning ISPS by way of contrast is excellent; mentioning it by way of answer is fatal.
  • Saying SUA lets warships stop suspect vessels. It does not. Article 8bis is a consent procedure and the flag State decides.
  • Writing limb (b) as the offences again. The commonest structural failure on this question.
  • Inventing article sub-paragraphs. 'Article 3(1)(a)(ii)' looks authoritative and is exactly the kind of detail that is wrong more often than not. Name the article, describe the conduct.
  • Quoting a number of States Parties. It changes constantly, you cannot check it, and it adds nothing to the answer.
  • Reaching for the Merchant Shipping Act 2025. It commenced on 15 March 2026, nineteen months after this sitting. For an August 2024 paper the Indian statute is the Merchant Shipping Act 1958 - and in any event the stem does not ask for national law.

If you only have ten minutes for this question

Limb (a), six lines: piracy needs two ships and private ends, so the Achille Lauro fell through the gap and nobody was obliged to act; SUA 1988, in force 1992, extended by the 2005 Protocol in force 2010; its objectives are to criminalize the conduct everywhere, to remove the safe haven, to provide a cooperation framework and to deter; article 3 makes it an offence to seize the ship by force, to commit violence on board likely to endanger safe navigation, to damage or destroy her, to plant a device, to interfere with navigational facilities, to communicate false information endangering navigation, or to injure or kill in connection with any of these, plus attempt, abetting and threat; 2005 added the ship as a weapon and the carriage of weapons of mass destruction, transporting fugitives, and a boarding procedure; and it enhances security by removing impunity and enabling interdiction, as distinct from ISPS, which prevents the incident rather than punishing it.

Limb (b), six lines: make the offences crimes with penalties reflecting their grave nature; establish jurisdiction on the flag, territorial and nationality bases, plus the residual head where the offender is present and is not extradited; take custody, hold a preliminary inquiry, allow consular access, notify the interested States and say whether you will exercise jurisdiction; extradite or prosecute, without exception whatsoever; treat the offences as extraditable, use the Convention itself as a legal basis if you need one, and give the greatest measure of mutual legal assistance; cooperate in prevention and report to the interested States and to the IMO Secretary-General; and note that a master may deliver a suspected offender to another Party's authorities, and that article 8bis permits boarding a foreign ship only with the flag State's authorization, with safeguards.

Common mistakes

These are the places this particular question goes wrong, taken from the ambiguities identified before drafting and from the single error most likely to cost marks.

The one that costs most: Answering as though SUA were a shipboard security regime. It is not - it creates no ship security plan, no ship security officer, no security levels and no declaration of security. Those are ISPS. SUA is a criminal-law and jurisdiction treaty that operates through national courts. The second trap is writing limb (b) as a second pass over the offences: the stem asks what STATES MUST IMPLEMENT, which is articles 5, 6, 7, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14 and the article 8bis boarding procedure.

The wording of the stem invites four specific misreadings, and each is resolved in the answer rather than left to chance:

  • 'Signatory states' is loose. Strictly, signature and ratification are different acts and the obligations bind States PARTIES rather than signatories. The answer uses 'Party' where precision matters and does not make an issue of the stem's wording, because the examiner plainly means States bound by the Convention.
  • 'Its 2005 Protocol' is printed in the singular. There are in fact TWO 2005 Protocols, one to the Convention and one to the Fixed Platforms Protocol. The answer treats the Convention Protocol as the subject, which is plainly what is meant, and mentions the fixed-platform companion once so that the completeness is visible without derailing the answer.
  • 'Key provisions' in limb (a) overlaps 'legal obligations' in limb (b), and a careless answer will write the same material twice. The division taken is that limb (a) carries the OFFENCE-CREATING provisions - what the Convention makes criminal - and limb (b) carries the OBLIGATION-IMPOSING provisions - what a Party must do about them. Article 8bis sits in limb (b) because it is enforcement, and the stem's second limb names enforcement expressly.
  • The boarding regime is not an obligation in the same sense as article 10; it is a permission with safeguards. It is placed in limb (b) as the enforcement mechanism, and its consent requirement is stated plainly so that it is not mistaken for a right of interdiction.

Likely oral follow-up

The retrieval questions below are the ones this answer is built to survive. They are the same questions an examiner reaches for when testing whether a written answer was understood or reproduced.

  • Give the route for the SUA objectives, provisions, obligations and enforcement question. — 8 + 8. The offences belong in limb (a) as 'key provisions'; limb (b) is what a Party must DO. Writing the offences twice is the commonest way to lose half of limb (b).
  • Why was SUA needed when piracy was already an international crime? — The single best opening for this question. The objectives are unintelligible until the gap is named, and it costs two lines.
  • What conduct does article 3 of the 1988 Convention make an offence? — Given by identity and effect. MIW holds no text of the Convention, so nothing here is a quotation and no sub-paragraph number is asserted.
  • What did the 2005 Protocol add? — The shift is from THE SHIP AS TARGET to THE SHIP AS WEAPON OR DELIVERY VEHICLE. Say that sentence and the analytical marks follow.
  • What must a State Party actually do under the Convention? — This IS limb (b). Six numbered duties, one article each, and the examiner can tick them.
  • Does the SUA Convention allow one State to board another State's ship? — The commonest overstatement on this question. Saying 'with flag State consent' turns a wrong answer into a right one.
  • SUA or ISPS - which does what? — The stem asks how SUA enhances maritime security. The honest answer is deterrence and the removal of impunity, and that only becomes clear against the regime it is NOT.
  • What is aut dedere aut judicare and where is it in the Convention? — If you write one Latin phrase in this paper, write this one - and then explain it in English, because the explanation is what carries the mark.

Flashcards 8

Limb (a): 1 the gap piracy leaves. 2 the instruments and dates. 3 objectives. 4 where it applies. 5 the 1988 offences. 6 the 2005 additions. 7 how it enhances security, with the ISPS contrast. Limb (b): 8 criminalize and penalize. 9 jurisdiction. 10 custody, inquiry, notification. 11 extradite or prosecute. 12 extradition and mutual assistance. 13 prevention and IMO reporting. 14 boarding under article 8bis.

8 + 8. The offences belong in limb (a) as 'key provisions'; limb (b) is what a Party must DO. Writing the offences twice is the commonest way to lose half of limb (b).

Piracy is built around one ship attacking another for private ends on the high seas. The Achille Lauro seizure in 1985 fitted none of it: the offenders were already on board as passengers, so there was no second ship; their motive was political, so it was not for private ends; and no treaty then obliged the State where they surfaced either to try them or to hand them over. SUA abandons the two-ship and private-ends requirements and replaces them with conduct-based offences plus a duty to extradite or prosecute.

The single best opening for this question. The objectives are unintelligible until the gap is named, and it costs two lines.

Unlawfully and intentionally: seizing or exercising control over a ship by force, threat or intimidation; performing an act of violence against a person on board if it is likely to endanger the safe navigation of the ship; destroying a ship or causing damage to her or her cargo likely to endanger safe navigation; placing or causing to be placed on a ship a device or substance likely to destroy or damage her; destroying, seriously damaging or seriously interfering with maritime navigational facilities where that is likely to endanger safe navigation; communicating information known to be false and thereby endangering safe navigation; and injuring or killing a person in connection with any of these. Attempt, abetting and certain threats are also offences.

Given by identity and effect. MIW holds no text of the Convention, so nothing here is a quotation and no sub-paragraph number is asserted.

Three new offence articles and one procedural article. Article 3bis covers using a ship itself as a weapon - to cause death, serious injury or damage for the purpose of intimidating a population or compelling a government - and the transport of explosive or radioactive material, biological, chemical or nuclear weapons and certain dual-use material. Article 3ter covers transporting a person who has committed a listed offence in order to help them evade prosecution. Article 3quater covers attempt, participation and threat in relation to the new offences. Article 8bis creates a boarding procedure.

The shift is from THE SHIP AS TARGET to THE SHIP AS WEAPON OR DELIVERY VEHICLE. Say that sentence and the analytical marks follow.

Six things. Make the offences crimes under its own law and punish them with penalties reflecting their grave nature (article 5). Establish jurisdiction over them - mandatorily where the ship is flying its flag, where the offence occurs in its territory, and where the offender is its national, with optional further bases (article 6). Take an alleged offender present in its territory into custody or secure their presence, conduct a preliminary inquiry, and notify the interested States (article 7). Either extradite or submit the case to its own prosecuting authorities (article 10). Treat the offences as extraditable and afford mutual legal assistance (articles 11 and 12). Cooperate in prevention and report to the IMO Secretary-General (articles 13 and 14).

This IS limb (b). Six numbered duties, one article each, and the examiner can tick them.

Not unilaterally. Article 8bis permits a Party that suspects a listed offence to request confirmation of nationality and, if confirmed, to request AUTHORIZATION FROM THE FLAG STATE to board, search and question. The flag State may grant it, may board itself, may do so jointly, or may decline. A Party may notify the Secretary-General of standing authorizations, and safeguards govern the use of force, the safety of the ship and cargo, and the treatment of those on board. The flag State's primacy is preserved.

The commonest overstatement on this question. Saying 'with flag State consent' turns a wrong answer into a right one.

ISPS, given force by SOLAS chapter XI-2, is PREVENTIVE and operates before and during the voyage: ship and port facility security assessments and plans, a ship security officer and a company security officer, security levels, access control, the ship security alert system and the declaration of security. SUA is CRIMINAL and JURISDICTIONAL and operates after the act and across borders: it defines offences, requires them to be crimes in national law, allocates jurisdiction, and compels prosecution or extradition. Neither substitutes for the other - ISPS aims to stop the incident, SUA ensures it can be punished.

The stem asks how SUA enhances maritime security. The honest answer is deterrence and the removal of impunity, and that only becomes clear against the regime it is NOT.

Article 10 - the extradite-or-prosecute obligation. A Party in whose territory the alleged offender is found must, if it does not extradite them, submit the case without exception whatsoever and without undue delay to its competent authorities for the purpose of prosecution, whether or not the offence was committed in its territory. It is the provision that removes the safe haven, and it is the mechanism on which the whole Convention turns.

If you write one Latin phrase in this paper, write this one - and then explain it in English, because the explanation is what carries the mark.

Also on the platform: QP2411 Q4 - SUA purpose, offences and the 2005 Protocol (donor) · QP2408 Q3 - the flag State's casualty investigation duty · QP2408 Q7 - salvage, the other treaty question on this paper

First in set First set here. The same task returns at November 2024 Q4.

Understand this first

When something goes badly wrong on a ship, two completely different questions arise and they pull in opposite directions. One is who is to blame - which is a matter for courts, insurers, prosecutors and disciplinary bodies, and it is settled by evidence tested adversarially. The other is why did this happen and how do we stop it happening again - which needs the opposite conditions entirely, because the people who know most about what went wrong are usually the people most exposed if blame is being allocated, and they will not talk freely if their words will be used against them. If you run only the first kind of inquiry, you get someone punished and you learn nothing transferable. So the international system deliberately separates them. It creates an inquiry whose only purpose is prevention, which expressly does not apportion blame or determine liability, which is independent of any other proceedings, and which is protected from outside direction - and it obliges States to run one whenever the worst class of accident occurs, and to send the report to the Organization so that everyone else can learn from it too. The obligation itself is old and sits in the conventions - the flag State undertakes to investigate and to share what it finds. What is newer is the standard method: a code that says who must be told, who investigates when several countries are involved, what powers investigators need, how seafarers must be treated when their evidence is taken, and what the report has to contain. The threshold that switches the whole thing from discretionary to compulsory is the worst category - the ship lost, a life lost, or the environment badly damaged - and that is the category the third limb is asking you to define.

Knowledge map
Casualty Investigation - Obligation, Code and Very Serious Marine Casualty
  1. (a)
    1. 1SOLAS regulation I/21 - Casualties
      • Each Administration undertakes to investigate any casualty to its ships
      • Where it judges the investigation may assist in determining desirable changes to the regulations
      • And to supply IMO with pertinent information on the findings
    2. 2SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 - Additional requirements for the investigation of marine casualties and incidents
      • Additional requirements for the investigation of marine casualties and incidents
      • Taking into account regulation I/21, investigations shall be conducted in accordance with the Casualty Investigation Code
      • Parts I and II shall be fully complied with; Part III should be taken into account to the greatest possible extent
    3. 3MARPOL article 12
      • The corresponding duty to investigate casualties and report findings to the Organization
      • Load Lines 1966 article 23 carries the same duty for load line casualties
      • Together these are the conventional sources the Code recites
  2. (b)
    1. 4What the Code is, and how it is built
      • MSC.255(84), adopted 16 May 2008, took effect 1 January 2010
      • Three parts: I general provisions, II mandatory standards, III recommended practices
      • Replaces the practice established by A.849(20) as amended by A.884(21)
    2. 5The purpose - and what a marine safety investigation is not
      • A common approach, to prevent future casualties and incidents
      • It does NOT seek to apportion blame or determine liability
      • Separate from and independent of any other form of investigation
    3. 6Mandatory standards - the Authority, and notification
      • High seas or EEZ: the flag State notifies other substantially interested States
      • In territorial waters: flag and coastal State notify each other and then the others
      • Not delayed for want of complete information; eight-item content list
    4. 7Mandatory standards - the duty to investigate, and agreement between States
      • A marine safety investigation SHALL be conducted into every very serious marine casualty
      • The flag State is responsible, subject to agreement under chapter 7
      • States consult to agree which will be the investigating State
    5. 8Mandatory standards - powers, parallel investigations, cooperation, independence
      • National law must let investigators board, interview and acquire evidence
      • Another substantially interested State may investigate in parallel; timing to be coordinated
      • Investigators shall be impartial and objective and free from external direction
    6. 9Mandatory standards - obtaining evidence from seafarers
      • Taken at the earliest practical opportunity; the seafarer returned or repatriated as soon as possible
      • Human rights upheld at all times
      • Informed of self-incrimination risk, the right to silence and available protections, with access to legal advice
    7. 10Mandatory standards - draft and final reports
      • Draft sent to substantially interested States on request, on a confidentiality guarantee
      • 30 days or another agreed period for comments; comments considered before finalising
      • Final report on every VSMC submitted to IMO, and made available to the public
    8. 11Part III - the recommended practices
      • Resources and qualified personnel; investigators appointed on the A.996(25) skills
      • Principles of investigation, coordination, evidence collection, confidentiality, witness protection
      • Investigation of casualties other than VSMC, and re-opening an investigation
  3. (c)
    1. 12The definition
      • A marine casualty involving the total loss of the ship
      • or a death
      • or severe damage to the environment
    2. 13Reading the definition against its neighbours
      • Marine casualty - seven limbs, deliberate acts excluded
      • Marine incident - endangered, or would endanger if not corrected
      • Severe damage to the environment - a major deleterious effect, as evaluated by the State affected or the flag State
    3. 14Why the classification matters
      • VSMC triggers the only MANDATORY duty to investigate, at chapter 6
      • and the mandatory submission of the report to IMO, at chapter 14.1
      • Below that threshold investigation is judgement, and reporting is conditional
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

(a)
  1. SOLAS regulation I/21 - Casualties
  2. SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 - Additional requirements for the investigation of marine casualties and incidents
  3. MARPOL article 12
(b)
  1. What the Code is, and how it is built
  2. The purpose - and what a marine safety investigation is not
  3. Mandatory standards - the Authority, and notification
  4. Mandatory standards - the duty to investigate, and agreement between States
  5. Mandatory standards - powers, parallel investigations, cooperation, independence
  6. Mandatory standards - obtaining evidence from seafarers
  7. Mandatory standards - draft and final reports
  8. Part III - the recommended practices
(c)
  1. The definition
  2. Reading the definition against its neighbours
