Memory cue. Limb (a) is TWO SOLAS PLUS ONE MARPOL: I/21 general (1), XI-1/6 the Code (2), MARPOL art 12 (3). Limb (b) is WHAT (4), WHY-NOT-BLAME (5), then the Code's own order - TELL (6), MUST INVESTIGATE (7), POWERS AND INDEPENDENCE (8), SEAFARERS (9), REPORTS (10), PART III (11). Limb (c) is DEFINE (12), COMPARE (13), CONSEQUENCE (14).

Remember14 route headings
Cover42 core points beneath them

Memorise the 14 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 42 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. SOLAS regulation I/21 - Casualties
  • Each Administration undertakes to investigate any casualty to its ships
  • Where it judges the investigation may assist in determining desirable changes to the regulations
  • And to supply IMO with pertinent information on the findings
2. SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 - Additional requirements for the investigation of marine casualties and incidents
  • Additional requirements for the investigation of marine casualties and incidents
  • Taking into account regulation I/21, investigations shall be conducted in accordance with the Casualty Investigation Code
  • Parts I and II shall be fully complied with; Part III should be taken into account to the greatest possible extent
3. MARPOL article 12
  • The corresponding duty to investigate casualties and report findings to the Organization
  • Load Lines 1966 article 23 carries the same duty for load line casualties
  • Together these are the conventional sources the Code recites
4. What the Code is, and how it is built
  • MSC.255(84), adopted 16 May 2008, took effect 1 January 2010
  • Three parts: I general provisions, II mandatory standards, III recommended practices
  • Replaces the practice established by A.849(20) as amended by A.884(21)
5. The purpose - and what a marine safety investigation is not
  • A common approach, to prevent future casualties and incidents
  • It does NOT seek to apportion blame or determine liability
  • Separate from and independent of any other form of investigation
6. Mandatory standards - the Authority, and notification
  • High seas or EEZ: the flag State notifies other substantially interested States
  • In territorial waters: flag and coastal State notify each other and then the others
  • Not delayed for want of complete information; eight-item content list
7. Mandatory standards - the duty to investigate, and agreement between States
  • A marine safety investigation SHALL be conducted into every very serious marine casualty
  • The flag State is responsible, subject to agreement under chapter 7
  • States consult to agree which will be the investigating State
8. Mandatory standards - powers, parallel investigations, cooperation, independence
  • National law must let investigators board, interview and acquire evidence
  • Another substantially interested State may investigate in parallel; timing to be coordinated
  • Investigators shall be impartial and objective and free from external direction
9. Mandatory standards - obtaining evidence from seafarers
  • Taken at the earliest practical opportunity; the seafarer returned or repatriated as soon as possible
  • Human rights upheld at all times
  • Informed of self-incrimination risk, the right to silence and available protections, with access to legal advice
10. Mandatory standards - draft and final reports
  • Draft sent to substantially interested States on request, on a confidentiality guarantee
  • 30 days or another agreed period for comments; comments considered before finalising
  • Final report on every VSMC submitted to IMO, and made available to the public
11. Part III - the recommended practices
  • Resources and qualified personnel; investigators appointed on the A.996(25) skills
  • Principles of investigation, coordination, evidence collection, confidentiality, witness protection
  • Investigation of casualties other than VSMC, and re-opening an investigation
12. The definition
  • A marine casualty involving the total loss of the ship
  • or a death
  • or severe damage to the environment
13. Reading the definition against its neighbours
  • Marine casualty - seven limbs, deliberate acts excluded
  • Marine incident - endangered, or would endanger if not corrected
  • Severe damage to the environment - a major deleterious effect, as evaluated by the State affected or the flag State
14. Why the classification matters
  • VSMC triggers the only MANDATORY duty to investigate, at chapter 6
  • and the mandatory submission of the report to IMO, at chapter 14.1
  • Below that threshold investigation is judgement, and reporting is conditional
Model written answer

(a) The applicable SOLAS and MARPOL provisions (4 marks)

Answer this limb in three short paragraphs and move on. Four marks will not pay for an essay, and the marks are for naming the right provisions and saying what each does.

1. SOLAS regulation I/21 - Casualties

The general undertaking. Each Administration undertakes to conduct an investigation of any casualty occurring to any of its ships subject to the provisions of the Convention when it judges that such an investigation may assist in determining what changes in the present regulations might be desirable. Each Contracting Government further undertakes to supply the Organization with pertinent information concerning the findings of such investigations, and the Convention provides that no report or recommendation of the Organization based on that information shall disclose the identity or nationality of the ships concerned or in any manner fix or imply responsibility on any ship or person.

Two features of I/21 are worth a clause each. It is expressed as an undertaking and it is qualified by the Administration's own judgement - when it judges that the investigation may assist. On its own, therefore, it does not compel an investigation into any particular casualty. And the anti-attribution rule in its second paragraph is the earliest expression of the no-blame principle that the Code later develops.

2. SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 - Additional requirements for the investigation of marine casualties and incidents

This is the provision that turns the undertaking into a method, and it is the one the rest of the question depends on. Taking into account regulation I/21, each Administration shall conduct investigations of marine casualties and incidents in accordance with the Convention as supplemented by the Casualty Investigation Code adopted by resolution MSC.255(84), and:

  1. the provisions of parts I and II of the Code shall be fully complied with;
  2. the related guidance and explanatory material in part III should be taken into account to the greatest possible extent, in order to achieve a more uniform implementation of the Code;
  3. amendments to parts I and II shall be adopted, brought into force and take effect in accordance with article VIII of the Convention - the amendment procedure applicable to the annex other than chapter I; and
  4. part III shall be amended by the Maritime Safety Committee in accordance with its rules of procedure.

Sub-paragraphs .3 and .4 are worth noticing because they are the mechanism by which the Code's two halves have different legal characters: the mandatory half moves by treaty amendment, the recommended half by committee decision. That is the structural reason Part III is guidance.

Note also that regulation I/21 carries a footnote directing the reader to regulation XI-1/6. The two are meant to be read together, and an answer that gives only one of them has given half of limb (a).

3. MARPOL article 12

MARPOL carries the corresponding duty for pollution casualties at article 12: the obligation on a Party to conduct an investigation of a casualty occurring to any of its ships and to supply the Organization with the relevant findings. The Casualty Investigation Code's own preamble recites SOLAS regulation I/21, MARPOL article 12 and article 23 of the International Convention on Load Lines, 1966 together, as the conventional provisions requiring casualty investigation and the supply of findings - which is why an answer that names all three, rather than only SOLAS, is a complete one.

MIW holds no text-bearing copy of MARPOL, so article 12 is cited here by identity and effect and is not quoted.

(b) The salient points of the Code, and the recommended practices (8 marks)

4. What the Code is, and how it is built

The full title is the Code of the International Standards and Recommended Practices for a Safety Investigation into a Marine Casualty or Marine Incident, known as the Casualty Investigation Code. It was adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) on 16 May 2008 and took effect on 1 January 2010, on the entry into force of the amendments to regulation XI-1/6 that were adopted by the companion resolution MSC.257(84).

It replaced the practice established by the Code for the Investigation of Marine Casualties and Incidents, resolution A.849(20) of 1997, as enhanced by A.884(21) of 1999, which had itself consolidated a chain of Assembly resolutions running back to A.173(ES.IV) in 1968. The essential change in 2008 was of legal character rather than of technique: what had been a recommendation became, in its first two parts, mandatory.

PartChaptersContentStatus
Part I1 to 3General provisions - purpose, definitions, application of chapters in Parts II and IIIMandatory (with Part II under XI-1/6)
Part II4 to 14Mandatory standards - the investigation Authority, notification, the duty to investigate, agreement between States, powers, parallel investigations, cooperation, independence, evidence from seafarers, draft reports, final reportsShall be fully complied with
Part III15 to 26Recommended practices - administrative responsibilities, principles of investigation, lesser casualties, agreement factors, unlawful interference, notification and commencement, coordination, collection of evidence, confidentiality, protection of witnesses, draft and final report, re-openingShould be taken into account to the greatest possible extent

5. The purpose - and what a marine safety investigation is not

Chapter 1 states the objective: to provide a common approach for States to adopt in the conduct of marine safety investigations into marine casualties and marine incidents. It then says the thing that defines the whole regime:

  • Marine safety investigations do not seek to apportion blame or determine liability. A marine safety investigation is conducted with the objective of preventing marine casualties and marine incidents in the future.
  • The aim is achieved through States applying a consistent methodology and approach, to enable and encourage a broad-ranging investigation in the interests of uncovering causal factors and other safety risks; and providing reports to the Organization so that information is widely disseminated to assist the industry in addressing safety issues.
  • A marine safety investigation should be separate from, and independent of, any other form of investigation. But the Code does not preclude other investigations - civil, criminal or administrative - and, importantly, it is not the intent of the Code that a State should refrain from fully reporting on the causal factors because blame or liability may be inferred from the findings.

That last clause is the sophisticated part and it is worth a sentence in the exam. The Code does not promise that nobody will be blamed as a result of what an investigation finds; it promises that blame is not the investigation's purpose, and it forbids an investigating State from softening its findings out of concern for the consequences.

Chapter 1 also records the jurisdictional starting point: each flag State has a duty to investigate any casualty to a ship flying its flag where it judges the investigation may assist in determining desirable regulatory changes or where the casualty has produced a major deleterious effect on the environment; a flag State shall cause an inquiry into certain casualties and incidents of navigation on the high seas; and a State in whose territory, including territorial sea, a casualty occurs has a right to investigate where it might pose a risk to life or the environment, involve its search and rescue authorities, or otherwise affect it as coastal State.

6. Mandatory standards - the Authority, and notification

Chapter 4 is a single obligation: the Government of each State shall provide the Organization with detailed contact information of the marine safety investigation Authority or Authorities carrying out investigations within their State. Simple, and the precondition for everything else - other States must know whom to contact.

Chapter 5 - notification.

Where the casualty occursWho notifies whom
On the high seas or in an exclusive economic zoneThe flag State of the ship or ships involved notifies other substantially interested States, as soon as is reasonably practicable
Within the territory, including the territorial sea, of a coastal StateThe flag State and the coastal State notify each other, and between them notify the other substantially interested States, as soon as is reasonably practicable

Notification shall not be delayed due to the lack of complete information, and it contains as much of the following as is readily available: the name of the ship and its flag State; the IMO ship identification number; the nature of the casualty; its location; the time and date; the number of any seriously injured or killed persons; the consequences to individuals, property and the environment; and the identification of any other ship involved.

7. Mandatory standards - the duty to investigate, and agreement between States

Chapter 6 is the operative obligation of the whole Code and it is short enough to know exactly:

  • A marine safety investigation shall be conducted into every very serious marine casualty.
  • Subject to any agreement under chapter 7, the flag State of a ship involved in a very serious marine casualty is responsible for ensuring that a marine safety investigation is conducted and completed in accordance with the Code.

Chapter 7 deals with the practical problem that several States may be entitled or obliged to investigate the same event. Where a casualty occurs in a State's territory, the flag State or States and the coastal State shall consult to seek agreement on which State or States will be the marine safety investigating State. The same duty to consult applies where a casualty on the high seas or in an EEZ involves more than one flag State. Agreement may also be reached with another substantially interested State for that State to investigate. Until agreement is reached - or if it is not reached - the existing obligations and rights of each State remain, and each may conduct its own investigation.

Chapter 7.5 supplies the reason a flag State is willing to hand the work over: by fully participating in a marine safety investigation conducted by another substantially interested State, the flag State shall be considered to fulfil its obligations under the Code, SOLAS regulation I/21 and article 94(7) of UNCLOS. That is the provision which prevents duplicated investigation from being the only safe course.

8. Mandatory standards - powers, parallel investigations, cooperation, independence

  • Chapter 8 - powers. All States shall ensure that their national laws provide investigators with the ability to board a ship, interview the master and crew and any other person involved, and acquire evidential material. Without this the Code would be unenforceable in practice, because an investigator with no statutory power of access cannot investigate.
  • Chapter 9 - parallel investigations. Nothing prejudices the right of another substantially interested State to conduct its own separate investigation; but the States concerned shall seek to co-ordinate the timing of their investigations, to avoid conflicting demands upon witnesses and access to evidence where possible.
  • Chapter 10 - co-operation. All substantially interested States shall co-operate with the investigating State to the extent practicable, and the investigating State shall provide for their participation to the extent practicable.
  • Chapter 11 - investigation not to be subject to external direction. Investigating States shall ensure that investigators are impartial and objective, and that the investigation is able to report its results without direction or interference from any persons or organizations that may be affected by its outcome.

Chapter 11 is the structural guarantee of the whole regime. An investigation that can be leaned on by the flag State's own ministry, by the owner, by the class society or by the insurer produces findings nobody can rely on, and the Code therefore makes freedom from direction a mandatory standard rather than an aspiration.

9. Mandatory standards - obtaining evidence from seafarers

Chapter 12 is the human-element limb and it is the one a serving engineer is most likely to encounter personally.

  • Evidence shall be taken at the earliest practical opportunity, and the seafarer shall be allowed to return to his or her ship, or be repatriated, at the earliest possible opportunity. The seafarer's human rights shall at all times be upheld.
  • All seafarers from whom evidence is sought shall be informed of the nature and basis of the investigation.
  • Further, a seafarer from whom evidence is sought shall be informed of, and allowed access to legal advice regarding: any potential risk of self-incrimination in proceedings subsequent to the investigation; any right not to self-incriminate or to remain silent; and any protections afforded to prevent the evidence being used against them.

This chapter sits alongside the Guidelines on Fair Treatment of Seafarers in the Event of a Maritime Accident, resolution A.987(24), adopted in December 2005 and promulgated jointly by IMO and ILO on 1 July 2006. Together they are the answer to an obvious objection: if a marine safety investigation is genuinely no-blame but the same facts may later be used in a criminal prosecution, a seafarer needs to know precisely where they stand before they speak.

10. Mandatory standards - draft and final reports

Chapter 13 - draft reports. Where requested, the investigating State shall send a copy of the draft report to a substantially interested State so that it may comment. Two conditions protect the draft: the receiving State must guarantee not to circulate, publish or give access to it without express consent; and the investigating State is not bound to send the draft if it asks the receiving State to affirm that the evidence in it will not be admitted in civil or criminal proceedings against the person who gave it and that affirmation is refused.

Substantially interested States are invited to comment within 30 days or another mutually agreed period. The investigating State shall consider the comments before preparing the final report, and where acceptance or rejection of a comment will directly affect the interests of the State that made it, shall notify that State how the comment was addressed. If no comments arrive within the period, it may proceed to finalise. It shall also seek to fully verify the accuracy and completeness of the draft by the most practical means.

Chapter 14 - final reports.

  • The final version shall be submitted to the Organization for every marine safety investigation conducted into a very serious marine casualty.
  • Where an investigation into a casualty or incident other than a very serious marine casualty produces a report containing information which may prevent or lessen the seriousness of future casualties or incidents, the final version shall also be submitted.
  • The report shall utilise all the information obtained, taking account of the investigation's scope, so that all relevant safety issues are included and understood and safety action can be taken.
  • The final report shall be made available to the public and the shipping industry by the investigating State, or that State shall undertake to assist the public and the industry with the details necessary to access it where it is published by another State or by the Organization.

What a report must contain is fixed by the definition in Part I: a summary of the basic facts stating whether deaths, injuries or pollution occurred; the identity of the flag State, owners, operators, the company as identified in the safety management certificate and the classification society, subject to national privacy laws; the ship's dimensions and engines and a description of the crew, work routine and time served; a narrative of the circumstances; analysis and comment on the causal factors including mechanical, human and organizational factors; a discussion of the findings, the safety issues identified and the conclusions; and where appropriate, recommendations with a view to preventing future casualties.

11. Part III - the recommended practices

The stem asks for these expressly, and they are where the marks are that most candidates leave on the table. Part III runs from chapter 15 to chapter 26 and covers:

ChapterRecommended practice
15 Administrative responsibilitiesStates should ensure investigating Authorities have sufficient material and financial resources and suitably qualified personnel. Investigators should be appointed on the basis of the skills outlined in resolution A.996(25), without precluding specialists appointed temporarily or consultants for expert advice
16 Principles of investigationThe principles on which an investigation should be conducted - independence, systematic method, and the pursuit of causal factors rather than of fault
17 Investigation of other casualties and incidentsInvestigation of marine casualties other than very serious, and of marine incidents - the discretionary tier
18 Factors for agreement under chapter 7What States should weigh when deciding which of them should investigate
19 Acts of unlawful interferenceHandling an investigation where the event may be a deliberate act rather than an accident
20 Notification and commencementNotification to the parties involved and the commencement of the investigation
21 Co-ordinating an investigationHow a multi-State investigation should be run in practice
22 Collection of evidencePractical guidance on securing and gathering evidential material
23 Confidentiality of informationProtection of the marine safety record - statements, communications, medical and private information, analyses, and voyage data recorder information
24 Protection for witnesses and involved partiesSafeguards for those who give evidence
25 Draft and final reportRecommended practice supplementing the mandatory chapters 13 and 14
26 Re-opening an investigationWhen new and significant evidence justifies reopening a completed investigation

Two of these deserve a line each in an eight-mark limb. Chapter 17 matters because it is the tier where most real investigations sit - below the mandatory VSMC threshold, where investigation is a matter of judgement, and where the Code nevertheless recommends the same method. And chapter 23 matters because the confidentiality of the marine safety record is what makes candid evidence possible at all; without it, chapter 12's protections would be hollow.

(c) The term “very serious marine casualty” (4 marks)

12. The definition

The Code defines it in a single sentence, and it should be given exactly:

  • A very serious marine casualty means a marine casualty involving the total loss of the ship or a death or severe damage to the environment.

Three points follow immediately from the wording. It is a subset of marine casualty, so the event must first qualify as a marine casualty at all. The three branches are alternatives, not cumulative - any one of them is sufficient. And 'a death' means one; there is no threshold of multiple fatalities.

13. Reading the definition against its neighbours

Four marks are not paid for one sentence, and the way to earn them is to locate the term in the Code's definition set.

TermDefinition in the Code
Marine casualtyAn event, or sequence of events, that has resulted in any of the following directly in connection with the operations of a ship: the death of, or serious injury to, a person; the loss of a person from a ship; the loss, presumed loss or abandonment of a ship; material damage to a ship; the stranding or disabling of a ship, or her involvement in a collision; material damage to marine infrastructure external to a ship that could seriously endanger the safety of the ship, another ship or an individual; or severe damage to the environment, or the potential for it, brought about by the damage of a ship or ships. It does not include a deliberate act or omission intended to cause harm
Marine incidentAn event, or sequence of events, other than a marine casualty, occurring directly in connection with the operations of a ship, which endangered, or if not corrected would endanger, the safety of the ship, its occupants, any other person or the environment. The same exclusion of deliberate acts applies
Very serious marine casualtyA marine casualty involving the total loss of the ship, or a death, or severe damage to the environment
Severe damage to the environmentDamage which, as evaluated by the State or States affected, or the flag State as appropriate, produces a major deleterious effect upon the environment
Serious injuryAn injury resulting in incapacitation where the person is unable to function normally for more than 72 hours, commencing within seven days from the date the injury was suffered
Material damageDamage that significantly affects the structural integrity, performance or operational characteristics of marine infrastructure or a ship and requires major repair or replacement of a major component; or destruction of the infrastructure or ship

Two observations that are worth making because they show the definition has been read rather than remembered. First, serious injury does not make a casualty very serious - a serious injury makes the event a marine casualty; it takes a death to reach the top category. Second, the environmental branch is evaluative rather than objective: it depends on what the affected State or the flag State assesses as a major deleterious effect, so two States could in principle classify the same spill differently.

14. Why the classification matters

The term is not a label. It is the switch that turns discretion into obligation, and this is where the remaining marks are.

Very serious marine casualtyOther marine casualty or marine incident
InvestigationMandatory - chapter 6: an investigation shall be conducted into every VSMC, and the flag State is responsible for ensuring it is conducted and completedDiscretionary - SOLAS I/21 applies where the Administration judges the investigation may assist; Part III chapter 17 recommends the method where one is held
Report to IMOMandatory - chapter 14.1: the final version shall be submitted to the Organization for every VSMCConditional - chapter 14.2: submitted where the report contains information that may prevent or lessen the seriousness of future casualties
NotificationApplies to marine casualties generally under chapter 5Applies to marine casualties generally under chapter 5

So the practical significance is this: the moment an event is classified as a very serious marine casualty, the flag State loses its discretion. It must investigate, it must complete the investigation to the Code's standard, and it must send the report to IMO where it becomes part of the international body of lessons. Below that threshold the same machinery is available and recommended, but whether it is used is a judgement. That is why the definition is examined, and it is why the classification is made carefully at the outset of an incident rather than at the end.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The printed marks are 4 / 8 / 4 and they are the plan. The most common way to mismanage this question is to write limb (a) at length - it is enjoyable, you know the regulations, and it feels like progress - and then arrive at an eight-mark limb (b) with no time. Limb (a) is a naming task. Three short paragraphs: I/21, XI-1/6, MARPOL article 12. Say what each does in one sentence and stop.

Limb (b) is the paper. Eight marks, and the stem contains a signpost most candidates walk past: it asks for the salient points of the Code and the recommended practices. 'Recommended practices' is the title of Part III. If your limb (b) covers only the mandatory standards you have answered half of it, however well. Give Part II in the Code's own chapter order - it is a sensible order and it stops you forgetting a chapter - and then give Part III as a short block, naming three or four of its chapters.

Limb (c) is four marks for a one-line definition, which tells you the definition alone is not the answer. Give it exactly, then place it against marine casualty, marine incident and severe damage to the environment, then say what the classification triggers. That third step - mandatory investigation under chapter 6 and mandatory report to IMO under chapter 14.1 - is where the last two marks are.

The idea underneath the Code

The regime exists because of a conflict that cannot be resolved, only separated. After a casualty, society wants two incompatible things. It wants accountability, which requires adversarial process, rights against self-incrimination, and a standard of proof. And it wants to learn, which requires people to say frankly what they did wrong, including the things that would embarrass or incriminate them.

You cannot get both from one inquiry. If the inquiry can punish, nobody will be candid. If the inquiry cannot punish, some people will feel justice was not done. The international answer is to run the second kind of inquiry properly and to insulate it: a marine safety investigation whose sole statutory purpose is prevention, which does not apportion blame or determine liability, which is separate from and independent of criminal, civil and administrative proceedings, which cannot be directed by anyone affected by its outcome, and which gives the seafarer explicit notice of the risk of self-incrimination and access to legal advice before they speak.

Everything in the Code follows from that design decision. Chapter 11's freedom from external direction protects the finding. Chapter 12's warnings protect the witness. Chapter 23's confidentiality of the marine safety record protects the candour. Chapter 13's condition - that a State receiving a draft may be asked to affirm the evidence will not be used in proceedings against the person who gave it - protects the whole arrangement internationally. And chapter 14's duty to publish and to submit to IMO is what makes the exercise worth doing at all: an investigation whose lessons stay inside one administration has protected the witness and taught nobody.

Why there are two SOLAS regulations, and what changed in 2010

This is worth understanding rather than memorising, because it explains the shape of limb (a).

Regulation I/21 is old. A provision requiring flag States to investigate casualties where the investigation might assist in identifying regulatory shortcomings appeared in the 1948 SOLAS Convention and was retained in 1960 and 1974, and the Load Lines Convention 1966 carried the same idea. But it is an undertaking, qualified by the Administration's own judgement, and it says nothing about how to investigate, whom to tell, who takes the lead when three countries are involved, or what the report must contain. For decades the 'how' lived in Assembly resolutions - A.173(ES.IV), A.322(IX), A.440(XI), A.442(XI), A.637(16), consolidated into A.849(20) in 1997 and enhanced by A.884(21) in 1999 - all of which were recommendations.

Regulation XI-1/6 is the change. Adopted by MSC.257(84) alongside the Code itself, it takes the old undertaking as its starting point - 'taking into account regulation I/21' - and makes the method binding: Parts I and II of the Code shall be fully complied with. That is what happened on 1 January 2010, and it is why an answer that names only I/21 is describing the pre-2010 world.

The chapter map of Part II, in one pass

If you can reconstruct these eleven chapters you can write limb (b) without notes. They fall into four natural groups.

  • Who and when - chapter 4 (tell IMO who your investigation Authority is) and chapter 5 (notification: who tells whom, not delayed for incomplete information, eight content items).
  • Whether and by whom - chapter 6 (a VSMC shall be investigated; the flag State is responsible) and chapter 7 (States consult and agree who investigates; full participation discharges the flag State's obligations under the Code, SOLAS I/21 and UNCLOS article 94(7)).
  • How it is protected - chapter 8 (national law must give investigators power to board, interview and take evidence), chapter 9 (parallel investigations allowed, timing coordinated), chapter 10 (cooperation and participation), chapter 11 (impartial, objective, free from direction) and chapter 12 (seafarers' evidence and rights).
  • What comes out - chapter 13 (draft report, confidentiality guarantee, 30 days for comments, verify accuracy) and chapter 14 (final report to IMO for every VSMC, conditionally for lesser events, made available to the public).

The definitions, and the mistakes people make with them

Common statementCorrect position
'A very serious marine casualty involves loss of life and loss of the ship'Or, not and. Any one of total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment
'Several deaths are needed'A death. One is sufficient
'A serious injury makes it very serious'No. A serious injury makes the event a marine casualty. Serious injury is defined as incapacitation preventing normal function for more than 72 hours, beginning within seven days of the injury
'Any pollution makes it very serious'Only severe damage to the environment, which is damage producing a major deleterious effect as evaluated by the affected State or the flag State
'A deliberate act - sabotage, an attack - is a marine casualty'Expressly excluded. Neither a marine casualty nor a marine incident includes a deliberate act or omission intended to cause harm to the safety of a ship, an individual or the environment. Part III chapter 19 deals separately with acts of unlawful interference
'A near miss is a marine casualty'It is a marine incident - an event other than a casualty which endangered, or if not corrected would endanger, safety

The deliberate-act exclusion is the single detail that most reliably distinguishes a candidate who has read the definitions. It also connects this question to Q2 on the same paper: conduct intended to cause harm is not a marine casualty at all - it is the subject matter of the SUA Convention.

Practical significance for a Chief Engineer

You are unlikely to conduct a marine safety investigation. You are quite likely to be inside one, and three things in the Code are worth carrying to sea.

  • Preserve the evidence. The definition of a marine safety record includes statements, communications relating to the operation of the ship, records of analysis, and voyage data recorder information. Bell logbooks, alarm printouts, engine data, oil record books and standing orders are the engineering equivalent, and they are worth more the earlier they are secured.
  • Know what you are being asked, and by whom. A marine safety investigation is no-blame; a class surveyor, a P&I correspondent, a flag State enforcement officer and a coastal State prosecutor are not conducting one. Chapter 12 entitles you to be told the nature and basis of the investigation, and to be informed of and given access to legal advice about self-incrimination and the right to remain silent.
  • Expect the report to be public. Chapter 14.4 requires the final report to be made available to the public and the shipping industry. The corollary is that published investigation reports are among the best technical training material available, and reading them is how the system's benefit actually reaches the fleet.

Examiner traps

  • Naming one SOLAS regulation. There are two, and they do different jobs.
  • Calling MARPOL article 12 a regulation. MARPOL's articles and its Annexes' regulations are different things; article 12 is an article.
  • Omitting Part III. The stem asks for the recommended practices by name.
  • Saying the Code prevents criminal proceedings. It does not. It keeps the safety investigation separate from them, and it protects the safety record and the witness - but the Code expressly does not preclude civil, criminal or administrative investigation.
  • Saying investigators must avoid findings that imply blame. The opposite: the Code says a State should not refrain from fully reporting causal factors because blame or liability may be inferred.
  • Importing the Merchant Shipping Act. The stem asks for SOLAS and MARPOL. The Indian machinery is a different question, and the Merchant Shipping Act 2025 did not commence until 15 March 2026 - nineteen months after this sitting.
  • Giving A.849(20) as the current Code. It was superseded when MSC.255(84) took effect on 1 January 2010. Mention it as history if you like; do not give it as the answer.

If you only have ten minutes for this question

Limb (a), three lines. SOLAS I/21: each Administration undertakes to investigate any casualty to its ships where it judges the investigation may assist in determining desirable regulatory changes, and to supply IMO with the findings. SOLAS XI-1/6: taking I/21 into account, investigations shall be conducted in accordance with the Casualty Investigation Code, MSC.255(84), with Parts I and II fully complied with and Part III taken into account to the greatest possible extent. MARPOL article 12 carries the corresponding duty for pollution casualties, as does Load Lines article 23.

Limb (b), eight lines. The Code was adopted by MSC.255(84) on 16 May 2008 and took effect 1 January 2010, replacing A.849(20); Part I general, Part II mandatory, Part III recommended. Its purpose is prevention and it does not apportion blame or determine liability. Salient mandatory points: tell IMO who your investigation Authority is; notify the substantially interested States promptly and without waiting for complete information; every very serious marine casualty shall be investigated, with the flag State responsible; States consult and agree which will investigate, and full participation discharges the flag State's obligations; national law must give investigators powers to board, interview and take evidence; parallel investigations are permitted but coordinated; investigators must be impartial, objective and free from external direction; seafarers' evidence is taken early with their human rights upheld and with notice of self-incrimination risk and access to legal advice; draft reports go to substantially interested States with 30 days to comment; and the final report goes to IMO for every VSMC and is made public. Recommended practices in Part III: adequate resources and investigators qualified on the A.996(25) skills, principles of investigation, investigation of lesser casualties, acts of unlawful interference, coordination, collection of evidence, confidentiality of the marine safety record, protection of witnesses, and re-opening an investigation.

Limb (c), four lines. A very serious marine casualty is a marine casualty involving the total loss of the ship, or a death, or severe damage to the environment - three alternatives, any one sufficient. It sits inside 'marine casualty', which has seven limbs and excludes deliberate acts, and above 'marine incident', which is an event that endangered or would endanger safety. Severe damage to the environment means a major deleterious effect as evaluated by the affected State or the flag State. The classification matters because it is the trigger: a VSMC must be investigated under chapter 6 and its report must be submitted to IMO under chapter 14.1, whereas below that threshold both are matters of judgement.

Common mistakes

These are the places this particular question goes wrong, taken from the ambiguities identified before drafting and from the single error most likely to cost marks.

The one that costs most: Giving only ONE SOLAS regulation in limb (a). There are two and they do different jobs. The second trap is omitting Part III from limb (b) when the stem expressly asks for 'the recommended practices' - that is Part III's own title and it is a signposted demand. The third is answering limb (c) with a general impression of seriousness instead of the Code's three branches: total loss of the ship, OR a death, OR severe damage to the environment.

The wording of the stem invites four specific misreadings, and each is resolved in the answer rather than left to chance:

  • 'The applicable regulation of SOLAS' is printed in the singular and there are TWO. Regulation I/21 creates the general undertaking; regulation XI-1/6 is what makes the Casualty Investigation Code mandatory and is the one the rest of the question depends on. The answer gives both and explains the relationship, because giving only I/21 leaves limb (b) unanchored and giving only XI-1/6 misses the provision it expressly takes into account.
  • MARPOL's provision is an ARTICLE, not a regulation. The stem's word 'regulation' is loose. The answer says article 12 and does not make an issue of the wording.
  • 'Salient points of casualty investigation code AND the recommended practices' could be read as one demand or two. It is two: the Code's mandatory standards are in Part II, and 'recommended practices' is Part III's own title. An answer that stops at Part II has answered the first half of an eight-mark limb.
  • 'Very serious marine casualty' is defined in the Code in a single short sentence, which cannot by itself be worth four marks. The limb is therefore answered by giving the definition exactly, then locating it against marine casualty, marine incident and severe damage to the environment, then explaining what the classification triggers.
  • The Code's definition of severe damage to the environment is itself evaluative - 'as evaluated by the State(s) affected, or the flag State' - so a VSMC finding on that branch is not purely objective. The answer says so, because it is the honest reading of the text.

Likely oral follow-up

The retrieval questions below are the ones this answer is built to survive. They are the same questions an examiner reaches for when testing whether a written answer was understood or reproduced.

  • Give the route for the casualty investigation question. — Printed 4/8/4. Limb (a) is a naming task and must stay short; limb (b) is eight marks and needs the Code's own chapter order; limb (c) needs three sections because one line of definition cannot be worth four marks.
  • Which SOLAS regulations require a flag State to investigate a casualty, and what does each do? — Four marks for limb (a) and half of them are here. I/21 alone is the pre-2010 answer; XI-1/6 alone leaves the Code unanchored.
  • Define a very serious marine casualty. — The whole of limb (c) rests on getting these three branches right and on knowing they are alternatives, not cumulative.
  • Marine casualty, marine incident, very serious marine casualty - how do they differ? — The three sit inside each other and limb (c) cannot be answered for four marks without showing where the boundaries are. The deliberate-act exclusion is the detail most candidates miss.
  • What is a marine safety investigation NOT for? — The single most distinctive feature of the regime and the easiest mark in limb (b). The last clause is the one that shows real understanding.
  • Who must be told about a marine casualty, and by whom? — A compact, countable list that lifts limb (b) above generalities.
  • When is an investigation compulsory, and when must the report go to IMO? — This is the payoff of limb (c) and it is what makes the classification worth four marks - VSMC is the trigger for both mandatory duties.
  • What protections does the Code give a seafarer whose evidence is taken? — The human-element limb of the Code, and the part a Chief Engineer is most likely to meet in person. It is also the answer to 'why would anyone tell an investigator the truth?'

Uncertainty and evidence limits

What this answer deliberately does not assert, and why. These are declared limits rather than omissions, and a candidate revising from this answer should know where MIW stopped.

  • THE WORDING OF MARPOL ARTICLE 12 IS NOT HELD AND IS NOT QUOTED. The corpus MARPOL consolidated 2022 edition is image-only - 393 pages returning 392 extractable characters. The article's IDENTITY and its EFFECT are carried at primary grade from the MSC.255(84) preamble, which recites it; its text is not reproduced and no paragraph of it is cited.
  • NO AMENDMENT TO THE CASUALTY INVESTIGATION CODE IS HELD OR ASSERTED. The corpus holds MSC.255(84) as adopted. The answer describes the Code as adopted and effective 1 January 2010 and says nothing about whether it has since been amended.
  • NO INDIAN INSTRUMENT IS CITED. The stem asks for SOLAS and MARPOL. The Merchant Shipping Act 1958 governs at this sitting and is not described; the Merchant Shipping Act 2025 commenced 15 March 2026, nineteen months after this sitting, and does not appear. QP2512-Q5 is the MIW question that carries the Indian machinery and it is cross-linked rather than absorbed.
  • NO CASUALTY STATISTIC IS ASSERTED - no number of investigations, no number of reports submitted to IMO, no detention or loss figure. None can be sourced and MIW's evidence-declaration standard requires an unverifiable quantity to be omitted rather than given false precision.
  • NO CASE OR NAMED CASUALTY IS CITED as authority. The Code's own foreword and its historical resolution chain are used to explain how the instrument arose; no individual casualty is described.

The corpus holds MSC.255(84) as adopted and would not contain a later amendment. The answer describes the Code as adopted and effective 1 January 2010 and asserts no amendment either way.

Before you read the answer: can you name all 14 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

Flashcards 8

Limb (a): 1 SOLAS I/21. 2 SOLAS XI-1/6. 3 MARPOL article 12. Limb (b): 4 what the Code is and its three parts. 5 purpose and the no-blame principle. 6 notification. 7 the duty to investigate a VSMC and agreement between States. 8 powers, parallel investigations, cooperation, independence. 9 evidence from seafarers. 10 draft and final reports. 11 Part III recommended practices. Limb (c): 12 the definition. 13 the neighbouring definitions. 14 what the classification triggers.

Printed 4/8/4. Limb (a) is a naming task and must stay short; limb (b) is eight marks and needs the Code's own chapter order; limb (c) needs three sections because one line of definition cannot be worth four marks.

TWO. Regulation I/21 (Casualties) is the general undertaking: each Administration undertakes to conduct an investigation of any casualty occurring to any of its ships subject to the Convention when it judges that the investigation may assist in determining what changes in the regulations might be desirable, and each Contracting Government undertakes to supply the Organization with pertinent information on the findings. Regulation XI-1/6 (Additional requirements for the investigation of marine casualties and incidents) is what makes the method mandatory: taking into account regulation I/21, each Administration shall conduct investigations in accordance with the Convention as supplemented by the Casualty Investigation Code adopted by MSC.255(84), with Parts I and II to be fully complied with and Part III to be taken into account to the greatest possible extent.

Four marks for limb (a) and half of them are here. I/21 alone is the pre-2010 answer; XI-1/6 alone leaves the Code unanchored.

A very serious marine casualty means a marine casualty involving the TOTAL LOSS OF THE SHIP or A DEATH or SEVERE DAMAGE TO THE ENVIRONMENT. Three alternative branches - any one is sufficient. 'Severe damage to the environment' is itself defined as damage which, as evaluated by the State or States affected or the flag State as appropriate, produces a major deleterious effect upon the environment.

The whole of limb (c) rests on getting these three branches right and on knowing they are alternatives, not cumulative.

A MARINE CASUALTY is an event, or sequence of events, resulting in any of seven consequences occurring directly in connection with the operations of a ship: death or serious injury; loss of a person from a ship; loss, presumed loss or abandonment of a ship; material damage to a ship; stranding, disabling or collision; material damage to marine infrastructure external to the ship that could seriously endanger safety; or severe damage to the environment or the potential for it. A MARINE INCIDENT is an event other than a marine casualty which endangered, or if not corrected would endanger, the safety of the ship, its occupants, any other person or the environment. A VERY SERIOUS MARINE CASUALTY is a subset of marine casualty - total loss, a death, or severe damage to the environment. NEITHER a marine casualty nor a marine incident includes a deliberate act or omission intended to cause harm.

The three sit inside each other and limb (c) cannot be answered for four marks without showing where the boundaries are. The deliberate-act exclusion is the detail most candidates miss.

It does not seek to apportion blame or determine liability. It is conducted with the objective of preventing marine casualties and marine incidents in the future, and should be separate from and independent of any other form of investigation, including investigations for civil, criminal and administrative proceedings. The Code is explicit that it is not intended to preclude other forms of investigation, and equally that a State should not refrain from fully reporting causal factors merely because blame or liability may be inferred from the findings.

The single most distinctive feature of the regime and the easiest mark in limb (b). The last clause is the one that shows real understanding.

On the high seas or in an exclusive economic zone, the flag State of the ship or ships involved notifies other substantially interested States as soon as reasonably practicable. Within the territory, including the territorial sea, of a coastal State, the flag State and the coastal State notify EACH OTHER and between them notify the other substantially interested States. Notification is not delayed for lack of complete information, and contains as much as is readily available of: the ship's name and flag State; the IMO ship identification number; the nature, location, time and date of the casualty; the number of seriously injured or killed; the consequences to individuals, property and the environment; and the identification of any other ship involved.

A compact, countable list that lifts limb (b) above generalities.

Chapter 6: a marine safety investigation SHALL be conducted into EVERY very serious marine casualty, and subject to any agreement under chapter 7 the flag State of a ship involved is responsible for ensuring it is conducted and completed in accordance with the Code. Chapter 14.1: the final version of the report SHALL be submitted to the Organization for every marine safety investigation conducted into a very serious marine casualty. Chapter 14.2: where an investigation into a lesser casualty or an incident produces a report containing information that may prevent or lessen the seriousness of future casualties, that final version shall also be submitted. Chapter 14.4: the final report is made available to the public and the shipping industry.

This is the payoff of limb (c) and it is what makes the classification worth four marks - VSMC is the trigger for both mandatory duties.

Chapter 12: evidence is taken at the earliest practical opportunity, and the seafarer is allowed to return to the ship or be repatriated at the earliest possible opportunity; the seafarer's human rights are upheld at all times. Every seafarer from whom evidence is sought is informed of the nature and basis of the investigation, and is informed of and allowed access to legal advice regarding any potential risk of self-incrimination in subsequent proceedings, any right not to self-incriminate or to remain silent, and any protections afforded to prevent the evidence being used against them. The regime sits alongside the Guidelines on Fair Treatment of Seafarers in the Event of a Maritime Accident, resolution A.987(24).

The human-element limb of the Code, and the part a Chief Engineer is most likely to meet in person. It is also the answer to 'why would anyone tell an investigator the truth?'

Understand this first

Every rule the Organization had written about ship emissions up to this point worked the same way: it told you something about the ship. Design her to be efficient. Have a plan for running her efficiently. Report your fuel consumption and be rated on how much carbon you emit per tonne-mile. Those rules do useful work, but they run into a wall. You can make a ship as efficient as engineering allows and she will still burn a fossil fuel, and the reason she burns it is not ignorance or bad design - it is that the alternative costs several times more per unit of energy. No efficiency requirement closes a price gap of that size. So attention turned to a different kind of instrument: instead of telling the shipowner what to build, put a price on the emission itself and let the owner work out the cheapest way to avoid paying it. Do that and two things happen at once. The gap between the dirty fuel and the clean one narrows, because the dirty one now carries a cost it did not carry before. And the money collected becomes a fund that can be used to make the clean fuel cheaper, or to help the countries that are hurt by higher freight costs through no fault of their own. That is the whole idea, and the arguments are not really about whether it would work. They are about how much the price should be, who collects it, who gets the money, and - the hardest one - whether a poor country far from its markets should pay the same rate as a rich one, when one long-standing principle of shipping law says every ship must be treated identically and another principle of climate law says countries should contribute according to their circumstances. At the time of this sitting none of that was settled. The Organization had agreed the shape of the answer - a fuel-intensity rule paired with a pricing mechanism - and had given itself a deadline to agree the substance. It is important to answer the question as it stood then.

Knowledge map
Market-Based Measures at MEPC 81
  1. (a)
    1. 1What a market-based measure is
      • An instrument that puts a price on the emission rather than prescribing the equipment
      • Contrast with technical measures (design) and operational measures (how she is run)
      • The purpose is to make the low-carbon choice the commercially rational one
    2. 2Where the authority comes from
      • The 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, resolution MEPC.377(80), adopted 7 July 2023
      • It revoked the 2018 Initial Strategy and set the current levels of ambition
      • Everything discussed at MEPC 81 is being developed under its work plan
    3. 3The targets the measures have to deliver
      • Net-zero GHG emissions from international shipping by or around 2050
      • At least 20%, striving for 30%, reduction by 2030 against 2008
      • At least 70%, striving for 80%, by 2040 against 2008
    4. 4The main objectives of a market-based measure
      • Close the price gap between conventional and zero or near-zero fuels
      • Raise revenue for the transition and for impacts on States
      • Reward the first mover and penalise the laggard, without prescribing technology
    5. 5The shape the Strategy fixed - a basket of two elements
      • A technical element: a goal-based marine fuel standard on GHG intensity
      • An economic element: a maritime GHG emissions pricing mechanism
      • To be finalized and agreed by the Committee by 2025
    6. 6The types of economic measure under discussion
      • A levy or contribution per tonne of GHG emitted
      • A cap-and-trade emissions trading system
      • A feebate - a fee on high-intensity fuel funding a rebate on low-intensity fuel
    7. 7Why the technical and economic elements are coupled
      • The fuel standard sets the trajectory; the pricing element pays for it
      • Well-to-wake accounting, to prevent a shift of emissions to other sectors
      • Synergies with the existing CII to be considered
    8. 8Where the process had actually reached at this sitting
      • MEPC 81 concluded 22 March 2024, five months before the sitting
      • The mid-term measures were PROPOSALS UNDER DEVELOPMENT, not an agreed instrument
      • MEPC 81 did adopt MEPC.384(81) and MEPC.385(81), which are not MBMs
    9. 9How the measures contribute to reducing GHG emissions
      • Price signal, revenue, and a guaranteed demand for compliant fuel
      • Global and flag-neutral, unlike a regional scheme
      • Technology-neutral, so it does not bet on one fuel
  2. (b)

      • The price level is the whole argument, and it had not been settled
      • Consensus practice at IMO means the least acceptable option constrains the outcome
    2. 11Challenge two - impacts on States, and the equity question

    3. 12Challenge three - fuel, safety and the ship

      • Safety and crew competence for alternative fuels
      • Verification, data quality and enforcement across the world fleet

      • Asset values, newbuilding decisions and the risk of stranded tonnage
      • Competitiveness, trade routes and the risk of regional fragmentation
    5. 14Alignment with global climate goals
      • Explicit reference to the Paris Agreement temperature goal in the Strategy

      • But shipping sits outside national inventories, which is why an IMO measure is needed
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

Memorise the 14 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 42 separate facts to learn individually.
