Model written answer

(a) Objectives and types of market-based measure, and how they cut emissions

1. What a market-based measure is

A market-based measure puts a cost on the greenhouse gas emitted and leaves the shipowner to find the cheapest way of avoiding that cost. It regulates the outcome by price rather than prescribing the means. That is a different kind of instrument from everything MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 already contains:

Kind of measureWhat it regulatesExamples already in force
TechnicalHow the ship is designed and builtEEDI for new ships; EEXI for existing ships
OperationalHow the ship is run and reportedSEEMP; the IMO DCS fuel consumption reporting; the CII rating
Market-basedThe emission itself, through priceNone yet in force at IMO level at this sitting

The reason attention turned to price is that efficiency rules run into a wall. A ship can be designed and operated to the limit of what engineering allows and she will still burn a fossil fuel, because zero and near-zero GHG fuels cost several times more per unit of energy. No efficiency requirement closes a price gap of that size. A market-based measure is intended to close it directly - and, crucially, to do so without the regulator having to pick the winning technology.

2. Where the authority comes from

The work is being done under the 2023 IMO Strategy on Reduction of GHG Emissions from Ships, adopted by resolution MEPC.377(80) on 7 July 2023, which replaced the 2018 Initial Strategy. Everything discussed at MEPC 81 on this subject sits inside that Strategy's work plan, and the Strategy is what an answer should cite - not a committee session, which is an event rather than an instrument.

3. The targets the measures have to deliver

The Strategy sets the level of ambition against which any measure is judged:

  • Net-zero GHG emissions from international shipping by or around 2050, consistent with the long-term temperature goal in Article 2 of the Paris Agreement.
  • Indicative checkpoint 2030 - reduce the total annual GHG emissions from international shipping by at least 20%, striving for 30%, compared to 2008.
  • Indicative checkpoint 2040 - reduce them by at least 70%, striving for 80%, compared to 2008.
  • An ambition on the uptake of zero or near-zero GHG emission technologies, fuels and energy sources.

These are the resolution's own figures and they are the only quantities in this answer. Note that the checkpoints are expressed on total annual emissions, not on intensity - which is precisely why intensity measures alone cannot deliver them, and why an additional instrument was thought necessary.

4. The main objectives of a market-based measure

  1. To close the price gap. By adding a cost to the high-emission fuel, the measure narrows or reverses the commercial disadvantage of the low-emission one, so that the clean choice becomes the rational choice rather than the virtuous one.
  2. To raise revenue for the transition. A pricing mechanism generates funds that can be directed to research and development, to bunkering infrastructure, to rewarding early adopters, and to addressing the impacts on States.
  3. To create a predictable demand signal. Fuel producers will not build production capacity for a fuel nobody is obliged or incentivised to buy; a durable price on carbon makes future demand credible enough to finance the plant.
  4. To reward the first mover and price the laggard. Under efficiency rules a compliant ship gains nothing from going further; under a price on emissions, every tonne avoided is money saved.
  5. To remain technology-neutral. The measure does not specify ammonia, methanol, hydrogen, wind assistance or onboard capture; it prices the emission and lets the market select. This matters because the Organization is in no position to pick a winner two decades out.
  6. To operate globally and flag-neutrally, so that a single rule applies to the whole world fleet rather than the patchwork of regional schemes that otherwise fills the gap.

5. The shape the Strategy fixed - a basket of two elements

This is the load-bearing fact of the question. The Strategy does not leave the mid-term measures open-ended: section 4.5 fixes their architecture. A basket of candidate measures delivering on the reduction targets should be developed and finalized, comprised of both:

  • a technical element, namely a goal-based marine fuel standard regulating the phased reduction of the marine fuel's GHG intensity; and
  • an economic element, on the basis of a maritime GHG emissions pricing mechanism.

The candidate economic elements are to be assessed observing specific criteria considered in the comprehensive impact assessment. The mid-term measures should effectively promote the energy transition of shipping and provide the world fleet with a needed incentive while contributing to a level playing field and a just and equitable transition.

Two further requirements shape the basket. Its development should take into account the well-to-wake GHG emissions of marine fuels as addressed in the LCA guidelines, with the objective of reducing emissions within the boundaries of the energy system of international shipping and preventing a shift of emissions to other sectors. And potential synergies with existing measures such as the CII are to be considered.

Note the vocabulary. The stem calls these market-based measures, which is the term of art from the earlier generation of IMO debate. The 2023 Strategy speaks of an economic element on the basis of a maritime GHG emissions pricing mechanism. They describe the same thing and it is worth using both terms in an answer, but the Strategy's phrasing is the one that appears in the instrument.

6. The types of economic measure under discussion

TypeHow it worksStrengthWeakness
Levy or contributionA fixed charge per tonne of CO2-equivalent emitted, or per tonne of fuel by carbon content, collected on bunkers or on reported consumptionPrice certainty - owners and charterers can plan; simple to administer; generates a large and predictable revenue stream for disbursementEmissions outcome is uncertain - if the rate is set too low, ships pay and carry on. The rate itself becomes the entire negotiation
Emissions trading (cap and trade)A cap on total emissions, divided into tradable allowances that ships must surrender against their emissionsEmissions certainty - the cap determines the outcome; the market discovers the least-cost abatementPrice volatility; needs a credible cap, a registry, a verified reporting system and enforcement against a mobile global fleet
FeebateA fee on fuels above a GHG-intensity benchmark funds a rebate to those below itDirectly and continuously rewards the first mover; can be designed to be revenue-neutral, which lowers political resistanceRaises little external revenue, so it does less for the impacts-on-States problem, and the benchmark must ratchet or the incentive decays

In practice the proposals before the Committee were combinations and hybrids of these - for example, a levy whose revenue is partly returned as a reward for low-intensity fuel, which is a feebate wearing a levy's clothes. The three types above are the honest way to organise the answer, and a candidate who names them and states the trade-off for each has answered 'types' properly.

7. Why the technical and economic elements are coupled

The Strategy deliberately requires both, and it is worth explaining why, because it is the part of the design most candidates miss.

  • The fuel standard sets the trajectory. A goal-based limit on the GHG intensity of the energy used on board, tightening on a published schedule, tells the market exactly what will be required and when. It guarantees the direction of travel regardless of the carbon price.
  • The pricing element pays for it and manages the gap. A standard alone imposes a cost on shipowners and generates no revenue with which to soften the impact on vulnerable States or to accelerate fuel production. A price alone gives no certainty that the trajectory will be met.
  • Together they give both certainty and finance. The standard makes demand for compliant fuel a regulatory fact; the price makes the money available to bring that fuel into existence.

The well-to-wake requirement is what keeps the pair honest. If only emissions from the funnel were counted, a fuel manufactured with heavy fossil energy would appear clean at sea, and the measure would move the emissions ashore rather than removing them. Section 4.7's insistence on life-cycle accounting is what prevents that, and saying so demonstrates real understanding of the design.

8. Where the process had actually reached at this sitting

ItemPosition in August 2024
2023 IMO GHG Strategy, MEPC.377(80)OPERATIVE - adopted 7 July 2023, thirteen months before the sitting
The basket of mid-term measuresNOT AGREED. Its architecture was fixed by the Strategy; its content was a set of competing proposals under development and impact assessment
The Strategy's own deadlineThe basket should be finalized and agreed by the Committee by 2025
MEPC 81PAST - concluded 22 March 2024, five months before the sitting
What MEPC 81 adoptedMEPC.384(81) (MARPOL Protocol I, reporting the loss of containers; in force 1 January 2026) and MEPC.385(81) (MARPOL Annex VI, low-flashpoint fuels and other fuel oil issues, a replacement engine case, and IMO DCS data; in force 1 August 2025). NEITHER IS A MARKET-BASED MEASURE
MEPC 82FUTURE - it had not met
Review of the short-term measuresIn progress; MARPOL Annex VI regulations 25.3 and 28.11 require it to be completed by 1 January 2026

State this position expressly in the examination. On a question tied to a named session, saying where the process had reached is not a caveat - it is a scoring proposition, and it is what distinguishes a candidate who has followed the subject from one who has memorised a summary. The honest answer in August 2024 is: the Organization had agreed the shape of the answer and had given itself until 2025 to agree the substance.

9. How the measures contribute to reducing GHG emissions

  1. Through the price signal on the operator. Every tonne of fuel burned carries a cost that scales with its carbon content, so slow steaming, voyage optimisation, hull and propeller condition, waste-heat recovery and wind assistance all acquire a financial value they did not previously have. These are measures a Chief Engineer can act on immediately, without waiting for a new fuel.
  2. Through the price signal on the investor. Newbuilding and major conversion decisions are taken on a twenty-five year view. A credible, rising carbon price changes the discounted economics of a dual-fuel engine or a shaft generator at the point the order is placed, which is where the largest reductions are actually decided.
  3. Through revenue. Funds raised can subsidise the cost gap for zero and near-zero fuels, finance bunkering infrastructure, support research and development, and reward first movers - which addresses the chicken-and-egg problem that nobody builds the fuel plant until there are ships to buy the fuel and nobody orders the ships until the fuel exists.
  4. Through the guarantee of demand. Paired with the goal-based fuel standard, the measure converts an aspiration into a regulatory requirement on a published schedule, which is what makes production capacity financeable.
  5. Through global uniformity. A single IMO measure applies to the world fleet regardless of flag, which avoids the distortion and evasion risk that arises when only some regions price carbon.
  6. Through life-cycle integrity. Well-to-wake accounting ensures that the reduction is real rather than an accounting transfer to another sector.

(b) Implementation challenges, impacts on the industry, and alignment with climate goals

10. Challenge one - agreeing the mechanism and the price

  • The types are structurally different and produce different winners. A levy suits States that want predictable revenue for disbursement; an ETS suits those that want a guaranteed emissions outcome; a feebate suits those who want the incentive without a large transfer. These are not variations of one idea and a compromise between them is not simple.
  • The rate is the entire argument. A price high enough to close the gap to green fuels is a very large number when applied to the world fleet's bunker bill; a price low enough to be politically acceptable may change nothing. There is no technical answer to this, and at this sitting there was no agreed one.
  • Revenue disbursement is as contested as collection. Who holds the fund, on what basis it is distributed, and how much stays in the sector are all live questions, and they determine whether a State supports the measure at all.
  • The Organization works by consensus in practice. A measure that a significant bloc of States regards as inequitable will not be adopted, however sound the economics, which is why the Strategy built a formal impact-assessment stage into the process rather than proceeding straight to adoption.

11. Challenge two - impacts on States, and the equity question

This is the genuine obstacle and it is not a matter of opinion - the Strategy addresses it directly.

  • Impacts must be assessed before adoption. The impacts on States of a measure or combination of measures should be assessed and taken into account as appropriate before adoption, in accordance with the Revised procedure for assessing impacts on States, with particular attention to the needs of developing countries, in particular LDCs and SIDS.
  • The assessment criteria name the vulnerability. Impacts are considered in terms including geographic remoteness of and connectivity to main markets, cargo value and type, and transport dependency. A small island State that imports nearly everything it consumes over a long ocean leg is, by construction, the most exposed to a rise in freight cost per tonne-mile.
  • Two principles collide, and both are in the Strategy. Its guiding principles expressly include the principle of non-discrimination and the principle of no more favourable treatment, enshrined in MARPOL and other IMO conventions, and also the principle of common but differentiated responsibilities and respective capabilities, in the light of different national circumstances, enshrined in the UNFCCC, its Kyoto Protocol and the Paris Agreement.

Those two principles pull in opposite directions and that is the heart of the difficulty. No more favourable treatment is the rule on which the whole IMO system rests: every ship is treated identically at every port regardless of flag, and without it the conventions could be evaded by re-flagging. Common but differentiated responsibilities says that States should contribute according to their circumstances and capabilities. A uniform global carbon price honours the first and appears to offend the second. The way through - a uniform price on ships combined with a differentiated disbursement of the revenue to States - is the reason the revenue question is inseparable from the measure itself, and it is why this took until 2025 rather than being settled at MEPC 81.

12. Challenge three - fuel, safety and the ship

  • A price cannot create fuel that does not exist. Zero and near-zero GHG fuels were, at this sitting, produced in quantities negligible against the world fleet's demand, and available at very few ports. A carbon price makes the willingness to pay; it does not make the plant, the terminal or the bunker barge.
  • Safety and competence lag the fuels. Ammonia is toxic; hydrogen has extreme flammability and storage demands; methanol has a low flashpoint and poor luminosity in fire. Each requires design provisions under the IGF Code framework and each requires trained crews. Note the connection to MEPC 81's own output: MEPC.385(81), adopted at that session, is largely about low-flashpoint fuels, which shows the Committee working on the enabling framework in parallel with the pricing debate - though those amendments were adopted and not yet in force at this sitting.
  • Measurement and verification must be robust. A price on emissions is only as good as the data it is levied on. It rests on the IMO DCS reporting system, on bunker delivery notes, on verified fuel consumption and on well-to-wake emission factors for every fuel pathway - and a fuel-intensity standard needs a certification chain for the fuel's origin, not merely its properties on delivery.
  • Enforcement is against a mobile fleet. Collection points, non-compliance sanctions and the treatment of ships of non-Parties all need to work in ports worldwide, and port State control is the only realistic enforcement surface.

13. Potential impacts on the shipping industry

AreaImpact
Operating costA material addition to the largest single voyage cost. It falls hardest on tonnage that is old, inefficient or engaged in long-haul low-value trades, and it rewards efficient modern tonnage immediately
Who paysThe charterparty allocation problem. On a time charter the charterer buys the bunkers and directs the employment, but the owner controls the ship's technical condition; on a voyage charter the owner buys them. Existing contracts were not drafted for a carbon cost, so clauses must be written and priced, and disputes about who bears the levy are a predictable consequence
Asset values and newbuildingsA credible carbon price re-rates the fleet immediately - efficient ships gain, inefficient ships lose - and creates a genuine risk of stranded tonnage. It also freezes ordering while the technology choice is uncertain, which is itself a cost to the industry
Operational practiceSlow steaming, weather routeing, just-in-time arrival, hull and propeller husbandry and waste-heat recovery all acquire a direct financial return. Much of the near-term reduction is available here
Freight rates and tradeCosts pass through to freight and ultimately to the cargo. The effect is proportionally largest on low-value, high-volume, long-distance cargoes, which is the same exposure the impacts-on-States assessment measures
Competitiveness and fragmentationThe strongest industry argument for a global measure. While no IMO instrument exists, regions legislate - the EU Emissions Trading System had already brought shipping into scope from 1 January 2024, and FuelEU Maritime was to apply from 1 January 2025. A patchwork of regional schemes creates double regulation, evasion routes and administrative cost, and the industry's own position has consistently been that one global measure is preferable to several regional ones
Crewing and trainingNew fuels require new competences, new procedures and new emergency response. This is a cost and a lead time, and it falls on the same manning market that is already stretched

14. Alignment with global climate goals

  1. The Strategy is explicitly aligned by its own terms. Its ambition of net-zero GHG emissions from international shipping by or around 2050 is stated to be consistent with the long-term temperature goal set out in Article 2 of the Paris Agreement, and its guiding principles refer to the UNFCCC, its Kyoto Protocol and the Paris Agreement.
  2. It closes a structural gap in the Paris architecture. International shipping's emissions are not attributed to any single State's nationally determined contribution, because a voyage between two countries belongs to neither. That is precisely why the Organization, rather than the UNFCCC process, is the body that must act - and it is the strongest answer to the question of why an IMO measure is needed at all.
  3. Well-to-wake accounting aligns the method. By requiring life-cycle emissions to be counted, the Strategy prevents shipping from decarbonising on paper by exporting its emissions to the energy sector, which is what climate accounting elsewhere increasingly demands.
  4. The equity provisions align with the Paris framework's own principles, by requiring impacts on developing States, LDCs and SIDS to be assessed before adoption and by naming common but differentiated responsibilities among the guiding principles.
  5. But alignment is not delivery, and an honest answer says so. At this sitting the 2030 checkpoint was six years away, the measures that were to deliver it were not agreed, and the Strategy's own timetable had the basket being finalized in 2025 with adoption and entry into force to follow. The alignment of ambition was clear; the alignment of instruments was still being negotiated.

That closing judgement is the right note on which to end limb (b). The examiner has asked how the measures align with global climate goals, and the defensible answer at August 2024 is: in ambition, closely and explicitly; in delivery, not yet - which is exactly why the basket was under negotiation.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

This question is unusual on an engineering paper because almost none of the marks are technical. It is a policy question, and the discriminator is judgement - can you explain why a sensible idea has taken years to agree? A candidate who lists three types of carbon pricing and stops has answered a third of it.

The single most valuable thing you can do is state where the process had actually reached. The stem names MEPC 81, which fixes the question to March 2024, and the sitting is August 2024. At that point the Organization had agreed the architecture of the mid-term measures - a fuel-intensity standard paired with a pricing mechanism - and had given itself until 2025 to agree the substance. Saying that clearly, with the Strategy's own deadline, shows you know the difference between a proposal and an instrument, and it protects everything else you write.

In limb (b), notice that the printed stem asks three things: implementation challenges, potential impacts on the industry, and alignment with global climate goals. Answer all three under separate headings. The alignment question is last and is the one candidates run out of time for, and it is worth two marks that are easy to bank if you leave four lines for it.

The idea underneath the whole debate

Strip away the acronyms and the difficulty is simple to state. Shipping burns a fuel that is cheap partly because nobody pays for the damage its combustion does. The clean alternatives are not cheap. Nobody will build the plants to make the clean fuel until there are ships that must buy it; nobody will build the ships until there is fuel to buy. Efficiency rules cannot break that loop, because they act on the ship and the loop is about the fuel.

A price on carbon breaks it from both ends at once. It narrows the gap, so the clean fuel becomes buyable, and it produces money, which can be used to narrow the gap further and to build the infrastructure. Pair it with a rule that says the average GHG intensity of the energy the fleet uses must fall on a published schedule, and you have converted an aspiration into a bankable demand forecast. That pairing is exactly what the 2023 Strategy specified.

So why is it hard? Not because anyone doubts the mechanism. Because a global carbon price is, unavoidably, a transfer of money, and every question about it is a question about who pays and who receives. That is why the argument is conducted in the language of principle - no more favourable treatment against common but differentiated responsibilities - and why an impact assessment on States was built into the process before adoption. If you understand that the obstacle is distributional rather than technical, you can write limb (b) without having read a single submission to the Committee.

The two principles that pull against each other

No more favourable treatmentCommon but differentiated responsibilities
Comes fromMARPOL and the other IMO conventionsUNFCCC, the Kyoto Protocol and the Paris Agreement
SaysEvery ship is treated identically in every port, whatever her flagStates contribute according to their responsibilities and capabilities, in the light of national circumstances
Exists to preventEvasion by re-flagging to a State with weaker rulesPoorer States bearing costs disproportionate to their contribution
Applied to a carbon priceOne uniform rate for the whole world fleetA rate, or a return of revenue, that differentiates by circumstances

Both are named as guiding principles in the same section of the same resolution, which is a remarkably candid piece of drafting - the Strategy states the tension rather than resolving it. The reconciliation the negotiations moved towards is worth understanding: apply the price uniformly to ships, and differentiate on the disbursement of the revenue to States. That keeps the shipping rule flag-neutral while allowing the climate framework's equity principle to operate on the money. It is also why revenue distribution could not be treated as a detail to be settled afterwards.

Levy, ETS or feebate - the trade-off in one line each

  • A levy fixes the price and leaves the emissions uncertain. Easy to administer, generates large predictable revenue, and the whole negotiation collapses into the single question of the rate.
  • An ETS fixes the emissions and leaves the price uncertain. Environmentally the more rigorous instrument, but it needs a credible cap, a registry, verified reporting and enforcement against ships that may never call at the regulating State's ports.
  • A feebate fixes neither but changes behaviour continuously. It rewards every improvement in intensity relative to a benchmark, can be revenue-neutral, and therefore attracts less political resistance - but raises little money for external disbursement, which is a serious weakness given that the disbursement is what buys the agreement.

If you can state those three sentences you have covered 'types' properly. Adding that real proposals were hybrids - a levy with part of the revenue returned as a reward for low-intensity fuel is a feebate in a levy's clothing - shows that you understand the categories are analytical rather than watertight.

What a Chief Engineer should take from this

It is easy to read this as a question about diplomacy. It is not, in the end. If emissions carry a price, then a great deal of what a Chief Engineer already controls acquires a direct financial value:

  • Hull and propeller condition. Fouling costs fuel, and fuel now costs more than fuel.
  • Machinery condition and load management. Turbocharger fouling, economiser performance, air cooler differential, injection condition - all of it moves the specific consumption.
  • Waste heat recovery, shaft generators, and the decision about which machine makes the power.
  • Voyage and speed management, though these are largely commercial decisions in which the engineer supplies the data.
  • Accurate fuel consumption data. Under a pricing mechanism the DCS report and the bunker delivery note stop being administrative returns and become the basis on which money is charged. The quality of engine room record-keeping becomes a financial matter.

That last point is the one worth carrying into an answer. The link between this question and Q6 on the same paper is real: a carbon price is levied on measured fuel, so the bunker delivery note and the consumption record become instruments of financial as well as regulatory consequence.

The temporal discipline on this question, stated plainly

This is the question on the paper most likely to be answered with information that is entirely correct today and entirely wrong for the sitting. At August 2024:

Do NOT writeBecause
'The basket has been approved'Approval came at MEPC 83 in April 2025, eight months later
'The IMO Net-Zero Framework provides…'2025. It did not exist
'The Global Fuel Intensity mechanism requires…'2025. It did not exist
'The price is set at X dollars per tonne'No rate was agreed at this sitting, and none is quoted in this answer at all
'MARPOL Annex VI chapter V provides…'A 2025 proposal. At this sitting Annex VI had no such chapter
'MEPC 82 decided…'MEPC 82 met 30 September to 4 October 2024, two months AFTER the sitting
'MEPC 81 adopted a market-based measure'It did not. It adopted MEPC.384(81) and MEPC.385(81), on container loss reporting and on low-flashpoint fuels and DCS data

The safe formulation, and the accurate one, is the Strategy's own: the basket was to be finalized and agreed by the Committee by 2025, with entry into force to follow adoption. Written that way you are quoting an operative instrument's timetable rather than guessing at an outcome.

Examiner traps

  • Writing the 2025 answer. Comprehensively the biggest risk on this paper. See the table above.
  • Confusing MBMs with EEDI, EEXI, SEEMP and CII. Those are the technical and operational measures already in Annex VI chapter 4. Mention them as the contrast, not as the answer.
  • Describing the EU ETS as the answer. It is a regional scheme. Name it once, as the pressure that makes a global measure urgent - it had applied to shipping from 1 January 2024, which is true at this sitting - and move on.
  • Quoting a levy rate. No rate was agreed and any figure is invented precision.
  • Treating the technical element as optional. The Strategy requires the basket to comprise both a technical and an economic element. An answer that describes only carbon pricing has described half the basket.
  • Forgetting well-to-wake. It is what stops the measure exporting emissions to the fuel producers rather than removing them, and it is a cheap mark.
  • Never answering 'align with global climate goals'. It is printed in the stem and it is worth marks that require four lines.

If you only have ten minutes for this question

Limb (a). A market-based measure prices the emission rather than prescribing the ship, unlike EEDI, EEXI, SEEMP and CII which regulate efficiency. The authority is the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, MEPC.377(80), adopted 7 July 2023, aiming at net zero by or around 2050 with checkpoints of at least 20% striving for 30% by 2030 and at least 70% striving for 80% by 2040 against 2008. Its objectives are to close the price gap between fossil and zero or near-zero fuels, raise revenue for the transition and for impacts on States, create a bankable demand signal, reward the first mover, stay technology-neutral, and apply globally rather than regionally. The Strategy fixes a basket of two elements - a goal-based marine fuel standard on GHG intensity, and a maritime GHG emissions pricing mechanism - to be finalized and agreed by 2025, developed on a well-to-wake basis. The economic element could take the form of a levy, an emissions trading system or a feebate. At August 2024 none of this was agreed: MEPC 81 had concluded on 22 March 2024 and the measures remained proposals under impact assessment.

Limb (b). Implementation challenges: agreeing which instrument and at what rate, and how the revenue is disbursed; the impacts on States, which the Strategy requires to be assessed before adoption with particular attention to LDCs and SIDS, and the clash between no more favourable treatment and common but differentiated responsibilities; and the physical constraints - zero-carbon fuel is barely produced or bunkered, the safety and training framework for ammonia, hydrogen and methanol is still being built, and the whole measure depends on verified consumption data and life-cycle emission factors. Industry impacts: a material addition to the largest voyage cost; a contractual problem about who bears it under time and voyage charters; immediate re-rating of asset values and a risk of stranded tonnage; a strong financial return on efficiency measures the engineer already controls; higher freight on low-value long-haul trades; and the risk of regional fragmentation, which is why the industry prefers one global measure. Alignment: the Strategy's net-zero ambition is expressly tied to the Paris temperature goal; shipping's emissions fall outside national inventories, which is why IMO must act; well-to-wake accounting keeps the reduction real; and the equity provisions echo the Paris framework's own principles - but at this sitting the ambition was aligned and the instruments were not yet agreed.

Common mistakes

These are the places this particular question goes wrong, taken from the ambiguities identified before drafting and from the single error most likely to cost marks.

The one that costs most: WRITING THE 2025 ANSWER. At August 2024 the basket was NOT agreed, NOT approved and NOT adopted. Anything about an approved basket, an IMO Net-Zero Framework, a Global Fuel Intensity mechanism, a settled price per tonne or a new MARPOL Annex VI chapter belongs to 2025 and is wrong for this sitting. The second trap is claiming MEPC 81 adopted a market-based measure - it did not; it adopted two Annex VI and Protocol I amendment resolutions on unrelated subjects.

The wording of the stem invites four specific misreadings, and each is resolved in the answer rather than left to chance:

  • 'Market-Based Measures (MBMs)' is the stem's vocabulary and it belongs to the earlier generation of IMO debate. The 2023 Strategy does not use it: it speaks of an ECONOMIC ELEMENT on the basis of a maritime GHG emissions pricing mechanism. The answer uses both terms and says which is which, rather than silently attributing the stem's phrase to the resolution.
  • 'Discussed during MEPC 81' invites an account of what happened in the room. MIW holds no MEPC 81 report. The answer therefore describes what was ON THE TABLE by reference to the Strategy's own basket and timetable, which were operative and are read at source, and asserts nothing about proceedings.
  • The stem's limb (b) asks about measures 'proposed in MEPC 81'. Strictly, MEPC 81 adopted two resolutions and neither is a market-based measure. The answer says this plainly, because a candidate who claims MEPC 81 adopted an MBM has made a factual error that a well-prepared examiner will catch.
  • 'Types' could mean the two elements of the basket or the varieties of economic instrument. It means the latter, and the answer gives levy, emissions trading and feebate - while placing them inside the basket structure so the relationship to the technical element is visible.

Likely oral follow-up

The retrieval questions below are the ones this answer is built to survive. They are the same questions an examiner reaches for when testing whether a written answer was understood or reproduced.

  • Give the route for the MEPC 81 market-based measures question. — 8 + 8. Section 8 is the one that protects the whole answer - stating where the process had actually got to by August 2024 is a scoring proposition, not a caveat.
  • What is a market-based measure and how does it differ from what Annex VI already does? — The opening distinction. Without it, limb (a) reads as a list of proposals with no explanation of why they were needed at all.
  • What did the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy actually fix about the mid-term measures? — This is the single most important fact in the answer and it is read at source. It also fixes the honest August 2024 position: the SHAPE was agreed, the SUBSTANCE was not.
  • Name the types of economic measure and say what each does. — 'Types' is a printed demand. Three named instruments with their trade-offs is worth far more than a paragraph about carbon pricing in general.
  • What was the position on the mid-term measures at August 2024? — The temporal spine of the question. Everything about an approved basket, a Net-Zero Framework, a GFI mechanism or a settled price belongs to 2025 and is wrong for an August 2024 sitting.
  • The Strategy's levels of ambition and indicative checkpoints. — These are the resolution's own numbers, read at source. They are the only figures in this answer, and everything the measures must deliver is measured against them.
  • Why is agreement on an MBM so difficult - in one sentence? — The single most sophisticated point available in limb (b), and it is sourced - both principles appear in MEPC.377(80) section 3.5.
  • What does the Strategy require before a measure can be adopted? — Turns 'it is politically difficult' into a sourced procedural requirement, and explains why the timetable ran to 2025 rather than being concluded at MEPC 81.

Flashcards 8

Limb (a): 1 what an MBM is. 2 the 2023 Strategy as authority. 3 the targets. 4 the objectives. 5 the basket - technical plus economic. 6 the types: levy, ETS, feebate. 7 how the two elements pair. 8 the position at MEPC 81 and at this sitting. 9 how they cut emissions. Limb (b): 10 agreeing the mechanism and price. 11 impacts on States and equity. 12 fuel availability, safety and enforcement. 13 industry impacts. 14 alignment with global climate goals.

8 + 8. Section 8 is the one that protects the whole answer - stating where the process had actually got to by August 2024 is a scoring proposition, not a caveat.

A market-based measure attaches a cost to the greenhouse gas emitted, rather than prescribing how the ship is built or operated, and leaves the owner to find the cheapest way to avoid the cost. MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 already contains TECHNICAL measures (EEDI for new ships, EEXI for existing ships) and OPERATIONAL measures (SEEMP and the CII rating), both of which regulate efficiency. An MBM instead regulates the emission itself through price. Efficiency rules cannot close the price gap between fossil and zero or near-zero fuels; a price on carbon is intended to.

The opening distinction. Without it, limb (a) reads as a list of proposals with no explanation of why they were needed at all.

Resolution MEPC.377(80) section 4.5: a basket of candidate measures delivering on the reduction targets should be developed and finalized, comprised of BOTH a technical element - namely a goal-based marine fuel standard regulating the phased reduction of the marine fuel's GHG intensity - AND an economic element, on the basis of a maritime GHG emissions pricing mechanism. Section 4.1.2 requires the basket to be finalized and agreed by the Committee BY 2025. Section 4.7 requires the development to take into account well-to-wake emissions as addressed in the LCA guidelines, so as to prevent a shift of emissions to other sectors.

This is the single most important fact in the answer and it is read at source. It also fixes the honest August 2024 position: the SHAPE was agreed, the SUBSTANCE was not.

A LEVY or contribution - a fixed charge per tonne of CO2 equivalent emitted or per tonne of fuel by carbon content. Predictable price, uncertain emissions outcome, and it raises revenue directly. An EMISSIONS TRADING SYSTEM - a cap on total emissions with tradable allowances. Certain emissions outcome, uncertain price, and it requires a credible cap and registry. A FEEBATE - a fee on fuels above a GHG-intensity benchmark funding a rebate to those below it. Can be designed to be revenue-neutral and directly rewards the first mover, but generates less money for external distribution.

'Types' is a printed demand. Three named instruments with their trade-offs is worth far more than a paragraph about carbon pricing in general.

NOT AGREED. The 2023 Strategy had fixed the shape of the basket and set a deadline of 2025 to finalize and agree it. MEPC 81 had concluded on 22 March 2024, five months before the sitting, and the candidate measures remained a set of competing proposals under development and impact assessment. MEPC 81 adopted MEPC.384(81) and MEPC.385(81) - amendments on container loss reporting and on low-flashpoint fuels and DCS granularity - and NEITHER is a market-based measure. MEPC 82 had not yet met.

The temporal spine of the question. Everything about an approved basket, a Net-Zero Framework, a GFI mechanism or a settled price belongs to 2025 and is wrong for an August 2024 sitting.

Net-zero GHG emissions from international shipping by or around 2050, consistent with the long-term temperature goal in Article 2 of the Paris Agreement. Indicative checkpoints, compared to 2008: reduce total annual GHG emissions by at least 20%, striving for 30%, by 2030; and by at least 70%, striving for 80%, by 2040. The Strategy also addresses the uptake of zero or near-zero GHG emission technologies, fuels and energy sources.

These are the resolution's own numbers, read at source. They are the only figures in this answer, and everything the measures must deliver is measured against them.

Because the argument is not technical but distributional: a global carbon price collides with a genuine conflict of principle between NO MORE FAVOURABLE TREATMENT, on which the whole IMO system rests and which requires every ship to be treated identically regardless of flag, and COMMON BUT DIFFERENTIATED RESPONSIBILITIES AND RESPECTIVE CAPABILITIES, from the UNFCCC and Paris framework, under which States contribute according to their circumstances. The 2023 Strategy expressly names BOTH as guiding principles.

The single most sophisticated point available in limb (b), and it is sourced - both principles appear in MEPC.377(80) section 3.5.

Sections 4.10 to 4.12: the impacts on States of a measure or combination of measures should be ASSESSED AND TAKEN INTO ACCOUNT BEFORE ADOPTION, in accordance with the Revised procedure for assessing impacts on States, with particular attention to developing countries and in particular LDCs and SIDS. The Committee should consider the comprehensive impact assessment in order to inform further consideration. Impacts are considered in terms including geographic remoteness of and connectivity to main markets, cargo value and type, and transport dependency.

Turns 'it is politically difficult' into a sourced procedural requirement, and explains why the timetable ran to 2025 rather than being concluded at MEPC 81.

Understand this first

The word 'efficiency' is doing something specific here and if you take it in the ordinary sense the whole question goes wrong. A very large ship burns far more fuel per day than a small one, so by any everyday measure she is the less efficient of the two. But nobody buys a ship to burn fuel; they buy her to move cargo. So the measure that matters is how much energy it takes to move one tonne of cargo one mile, and by that measure the large ship is usually far better - because as a hull gets bigger its carrying volume grows faster than the surface the water has to drag against, and because the crew, the lighting, the pumps and the accommodation cost roughly the same whether you are carrying twenty thousand tonnes or two hundred thousand. That is the first idea. The second is about speed, and it is brutal: the resistance a hull meets climbs steeply as she goes faster, and the power needed to overcome it climbs faster still - roughly as the cube of the speed over the normal range. Take a little off the speed and the fuel falls away disproportionately. The third is about shape. For a given amount of cargo you can build a long thin ship or a short fat one. The long thin one slips through the water making smaller waves, but she has more surface to drag and costs more to build; the short fat one carries beautifully but pushes a hill of water in front of her. The designer chooses where to sit between them. And the reason all this is worth an exam question is that these three choices are made once, on paper, years before the ship floats - and having been made they set the fuel bill for the next twenty-five years. Everything the engineer does afterwards is working inside a box that the naval architect drew.

Knowledge map
Ship Dimension Optimisation for Energy Efficiency
  1. intro
    1. 1The unit the question is really about
      • The unit is energy per unit of transport work - per tonne-mile - not per hour
      • This is why a bigger, slower ship can be efficient while being a large consumer
      • EEDI and CII both measure in this form
    2. 2Why dimensions are the largest lever, and when it can be pulled
      • They fix resistance, installed power and transport work for the whole life of the ship
      • They can only be chosen once, at design; everything later is mitigation
      • Hence the regulatory focus on new-ship design
  2. (a)
    1. 3Where the economy of scale comes from
      • Displacement grows with volume, wetted surface with area
      • Machinery, hotel and auxiliary loads do not scale with cargo
      • Crew, stores and overheads are near-constant per ship
    2. 4The limits, and the condition the whole saving depends on
      • Canal, port, berth, draught and terminal constraints
      • Cargo availability, utilisation and ballast legs
      • A large ship part-loaded is worse per tonne-mile than a smaller full one
    3. 5The regulatory consequence of scale
      • EEDI's capacity term rewards larger ships on the same reference line
      • Larger ships also carry more absolute emissions, which is what the CII rating exposes
      • Scale improves the index; it does not by itself reduce total emissions
  3. (b)
    1. 6The power-speed relationship and what follows from it
      • Resistance rises steeply with speed and power rises faster still
      • Approximately with the cube of speed over the normal range
      • Fuel per mile therefore rises approximately with the square of speed
    2. 7What the design service speed fixes permanently
      • Installed power, engine selection, propeller design and the hull lines
      • The bulbous bow and lines are tuned to a design speed and draught
      • Off-design operation carries a penalty the ship cannot design away
    3. 8The operational and commercial trade-off
      • Slower means less fuel per mile but longer voyages and more ships for the same annual capacity
      • Cargo value, freight rate and fuel price set the economic speed
      • Slow steaming has real machinery consequences
  4. (c)
    1. 9The resistance components the dimensions act on
      • Frictional resistance follows wetted surface
      • Residuary resistance follows form and speed-length ratio
      • Air and appendage resistance are smaller but not negligible
    2. 10Length
      • Greater length for a given displacement lowers the speed-length ratio and wave-making
      • But increases wetted surface, structural weight and cost
      • Longer parallel middle body is cheap to build and carries cargo well
    3. 11Beam, draught and fullness
      • Beam gives stability and deck area but adds wave-making and demands power
      • Draught is limited by ports and by the load line; deeper is generally more efficient
      • Fullness carries cargo but adds residuary resistance and complicates the wake
    4. 12The afterbody, the aperture and the propeller
      • Afterbody form governs wake quality and hull efficiency
      • The aperture must allow the largest, slowest-turning propeller that will fit and stay immersed
      • Rudder, bilge keels, bossings and openings all cost something
  5. all
    1. 13What stops optimisation being a free gain
      • Stability, subdivision and damage stability; strength and structural weight
      • Seakeeping, added resistance in waves, and manoeuvrability
      • Tonnage measurement, port dues and canal tolls; building cost and yard capability

      • Speed is decided first because it sizes the powering problem
      • Capacity is decided by the trade and the route constraints
      • Dimensions are then optimised within those two, which is why the order matters

Remember14 route headings

Memorise the 14 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 42 separate facts to learn individually.
