Model written answer

1. The unit the question is really about

Everything in this answer depends on one distinction. A ship's efficiency is not measured in fuel per day. It is measured in energy per unit of transport work - the fuel required to move one tonne of cargo one mile. Measured per day, a large fast ship is always the worst on the water; measured per tonne-mile she is frequently the best, and every regulatory index the Organization has written - EEDI for new ships, EEXI for existing ships and the operational CII - is expressed in that per-transport-work form.

State this in the first two lines of the answer. Without it, 'a bigger ship is more efficient' reads as a contradiction; with it, all three limbs become one argument.

2. Why dimensions are the largest lever, and when it can be pulled

The principal parameters - size, speed and the principal dimensions - fix the ship's resistance, her installed power and her transport work simultaneously, and they do so for the whole of her service life. Once the hull is built, the naval architect's box is closed: the engineer can keep the hull clean, keep the machinery in condition, recover waste heat, optimise the voyage and slow down, and all of that matters - but it is mitigation within a box drawn on paper years earlier. That is why the regulatory effort on new ships is directed at design, and it is why the stem calls dimensional optimisation a recognised means of improving efficiency.

(a) Ship size and capacity (6 marks)

3. Where the economy of scale comes from

The saving is physical, not merely commercial, and it has three independent sources.

  1. Geometry - volume against area. Scale a hull up and its displacement, and with it the cargo it can carry, grows with volume, while the wetted surface that generates frictional resistance grows with area. Volume grows faster. The larger ship therefore carries disproportionately more cargo for the skin friction she must overcome, and since frictional resistance dominates the resistance of a full-form ship at moderate speed, that is the largest single component of the effect.
  2. Loads that do not scale with cargo. Accommodation, lighting, ventilation, domestic services, most auxiliary machinery and the steering gear are sized by the ship and the crew rather than by the cargo. A doubling of cargo capacity does not double the hotel load. Spread across far more tonne-miles, these become a much smaller share of the energy bill.
  3. Fixed costs per ship. The crew, the stores, the certification, the surveys, the port entry and the voyage management are near-constant per ship rather than per tonne. This is a commercial rather than an energy saving, but it is what makes the large ship attractive to the owner in the first place, and the stem asks for scope and impact rather than for energy alone.

There is a fourth, subtler effect worth a clause. A larger main engine is generally a more thermally efficient one: a large-bore, long-stroke, slow-running two-stroke turning a large slow propeller achieves a better specific fuel consumption and a better propulsive coefficient than the smaller, faster-running machinery a small ship must use. Scale improves the propulsion plant as well as the hull.

4. The limits, and the condition the whole saving depends on

This is where the marks in a six-mark limb are, because the benefit is easy and the constraints are not.

ConstraintWhat it bounds
Canal and lock dimensionsLength, beam and draught for any trade that must transit. A ship built one metre too wide for a canal loses that routeing permanently
Port, berth and channelBerth length, channel and approach depth, turning basin, air draught under bridges, and tidal windows. A deeper ship may be restricted to part cargo or to tidal sailing
Terminal infrastructureCrane outreach and height, loading and discharge rates, shore tank capacity, and the storage the terminal has behind the berth
Cargo availabilityA larger parcel needs a larger buyer, a deeper trade and a longer stem. This is the binding constraint far more often than the physical ones
Frequency of serviceFewer, larger sailings mean longer intervals between them, which some trades will not accept whatever the freight saving
Flexibility and riskA large ship can serve fewer ports and fewer trades, so she is more exposed to a downturn in one route and harder to redeploy

And then the condition on which everything rests: the saving is per tonne CARRIED. A large ship sailing part-loaded, or making long ballast legs because her size restricts the cargoes and ports open to her, can be worse per tonne-mile than a smaller ship that is consistently full. Utilisation, not size, is what actually delivers the efficiency, and an answer that says 'bigger is better' without this qualification has stated half a truth.

One further distinction sharpens limb (a). The economy of scale behaves differently in weight-limited and volume-limited trades. A crude tanker or an ore carrier is limited by draught and by deadweight, so scale is bounded by the depth of water available. A car carrier or a container ship is limited by the volume and stacking she can offer, so scale is bounded by air draught, by stability and by crane reach. Saying which constraint binds a given trade is the mark of a candidate who has thought about the question rather than recited it.

5. The regulatory consequence of scale

Because EEDI expresses grams of CO2 per tonne-mile, and its required value is set against a reference line that is itself a function of capacity, a larger ship of a given type generally attains a better index than a smaller one of the same design generation. Scale therefore helps a designer meet the required EEDI, and that is a real, intended part of the design response.

But two honest qualifications belong here. First, the index improves while total emissions rise: a larger ship emits more in absolute terms, and moving the same trade in fewer, larger ships only reduces total emissions if the tonne-miles do not grow to fill the capacity. Second, the operational CII rating is sensitive to how the ship is actually run - to utilisation, to ballast legs and to time at anchor - so a large ship that trades badly can rate poorly despite an excellent design index. Design scale is rewarded by the design index; it is not a substitute for operating the ship well, which is the point at which this question meets the SEEMP.

(b) Service speed (5 marks)

6. The power-speed relationship and what follows from it

Resistance rises steeply and non-linearly with speed, and the power required to overcome it rises faster still. Over a ship's normal operating range the standard first-principles approximation is that propulsive power varies approximately with the cube of speed.

The consequence that matters is one step further on. Time on passage falls in inverse proportion to speed, so fuel consumed per mile rises approximately with the square of speed. A modest reduction in speed therefore produces a disproportionately large reduction in fuel per mile - which is why speed is the single most powerful lever available anywhere in this question, and the only one that can be pulled after the ship is built.

State the qualification, because it is the difference between engineering and arithmetic. The cube is an approximation and an idealisation. The true exponent depends on the hull form, on the loading condition, on the speed-length ratio, and it rises sharply once significant wave-making sets in - which is why a ship pushed above her design speed pays a penalty far worse than the cube suggests. At the other end, the relationship becomes unreliable at very low load, where the machinery, not the hull, becomes the limiting consideration.

7. What the design service speed fixes permanently

The choice of design service speed is not a setting. It is a commitment, and it determines:

  • The installed propulsive power, and therefore the main engine's bore, stroke, cylinder number and rated output.
  • The propeller - diameter, pitch, blade area and design shaft speed - and with it the gearing or direct-drive arrangement.
  • The hull lines, including the fineness of the entrance and run and the size, shape and position of the bulbous bow, which is tuned to a particular speed and draught combination.
  • The auxiliary architecture - whether a shaft generator is worthwhile, and how much exhaust energy is available for waste-heat recovery, both of which depend on the power level and on the intended operating point.
  • The attained EEDI at delivery, since the index is computed at a reference speed and power condition.

The consequence is that off-design operation carries a penalty the ship cannot design away. A bulbous bow optimised for the loaded design draught and speed can actually add resistance when the ship runs in ballast at a much reduced speed. A main engine selected for a high service speed and then run permanently at low load suffers poor combustion, cold-end corrosion, turbocharger mismatch and fouling of the economiser and the gas ways. This is the direct link between a naval architect's decision and the engineer's daily problems, and it is worth one sentence in the exam.

8. The operational and commercial trade-off

Speed reduction is not free, and 'discuss' requires the other side to be argued.

Reducing service speedGainsCosts
EnergyFuel per mile falls disproportionately; emissions fall with it; CII rating improvesBelow a point, machinery suffers - low-load combustion, cold corrosion, fouling, and the need for derating or engine modification
CommercialLower voyage cost; the largest single saving available on an existing shipLonger voyages, so more ships are needed to carry the same annual volume. The capital, crewing and maintenance of the extra tonnage offset part of the fuel saving
Service-Slower delivery, more cargo tied up in transit, higher inventory cost for the shipper, and a service some trades will not buy
DesignA lower design speed permits a fuller, cheaper hull with less installed power and a smaller engineThe ship is then permanently slow - she cannot make up time, and her value in a strong freight market is lower

The economic speed is therefore set by the interaction of fuel price, freight rate and cargo value. High fuel and weak freight push the fleet slow; cheap fuel and a strong market push it fast. The design decision must be taken years before any of those are known, which is why designers increasingly specify a hull and a plant that perform acceptably across a range of speeds rather than optimally at a single point - and why engine derating, adjustable propeller design margins and dual-rating arrangements have become common.

(c) Principal dimensions (5 marks)

9. The resistance components the dimensions act on

Limb (c) becomes straightforward once the resistance is decomposed, because each dimension acts on a different component and often in opposite directions.

ComponentWhat it depends onWhich dimensions drive it
Frictional resistanceWetted surface area, and the roughness and condition of the surfaceIncreased by greater length, greater beam and deeper draught - anything that adds skin
Residuary resistance (chiefly wave-making, with eddy and form drag)Hull form and the speed-length ratioReduced by greater length for a given displacement, finer ends, lower fullness, and a bulb tuned to the operating point
Appendage resistanceRudder, bilge keels, bossings, shaft brackets, sea chests and openingsGoverned by the stern arrangement and by how many appendages the design needs
Air resistanceAbove-water form and projected frontal areaSuperstructure, container stacks, deck cargo - modest but not negligible on a high-sided ship
Added resistance in wavesSeakeeping behaviour in the actual service environmentSlenderness, bow form, freeboard and mass distribution

The central tension of the whole limb is that length acts on the first two components in opposite directions. Every hull-form decision is a position taken on that trade-off.

10. Length

  • The gain. For a given displacement and speed, a longer hull has a lower speed-length ratio and therefore makes smaller waves. Wave-making resistance falls, sometimes substantially, and the ship can be driven at her service speed on less power.
  • The cost. A longer hull has more wetted surface, so frictional resistance rises. It is also structurally more demanding - the longitudinal bending moment rises steeply with length, so more steel is needed, which costs money and eats into deadweight. Length is expensive in yard terms, may exceed available building dock or berth dimensions, and attracts higher port dues and canal tolls.
  • The compromise in practice. Where a trade allows it, length is added as parallel middle body - the cheapest part of a hull to build, the most efficient part in which to carry cargo, and hydrodynamically almost neutral because it generates no additional wave system. The expensive hydrodynamics is at the ends.

11. Beam, draught and fullness

DimensionIncrease it and you gainIncrease it and you pay
BeamTransverse stability - metacentric height; deck area and container rows; cargo volume without adding lengthWave-making and form resistance rise significantly. Beam is hydrodynamically expensive, more so than length. Also constrained by canal, lock and berth dimensions
DraughtDisplacement and deadweight without adding length or beam; better propeller immersion, which improves propulsive efficiency and reduces the risk of racing and cavitation in ballastLimited by the assigned freeboard under the Load Line Convention and by the depth of channels, berths and terminals. A ship too deep for her ports sails part loaded
Fullness (block or prismatic coefficient)More cargo in the same envelope; cheaper construction; more parallel middle bodyResiduary resistance rises, especially as speed increases. A very full afterbody produces a poor, non-uniform wake, which costs propulsive efficiency and causes cavitation and vibration

Two general propositions follow and are worth stating. Deeper is generally more efficient - for a given deadweight, taking it in draught rather than in beam is hydrodynamically cheaper, which is why efficiency-driven designs push draught to whatever the trade's ports will permit. And fullness is a decision about speed: a slow ship can afford to be full because her residuary resistance is small at low speed-length ratios; a fast ship cannot. That is the link between limbs (b) and (c) and it is the single most useful sentence in this part of the answer.

12. The afterbody, the aperture and the propeller

Optimising the forebody for resistance is only half the problem; the afterbody determines how efficiently the power reaches the water.

  • Wake quality. The afterbody shape governs the flow into the propeller disc. A well-designed run gives a more uniform inflow, which raises propulsive efficiency, reduces cavitation and lowers hull-pressure excitation and vibration. A very full run produces a strongly non-uniform wake and the propeller pays for it in every revolution.
  • Propeller size and speed. A large, slow-turning propeller is the more efficient one, because it accelerates a larger mass of water by a smaller amount. The aperture and stern arrangement must therefore accommodate the largest diameter that will fit while remaining fully immersed in the ballast condition - immersion, not just clearance, is the binding constraint.
  • The handover point. Where the hull form cannot be improved further, the wake is addressed by appendages - wake-equalising ducts, pre-swirl stators and post-swirl devices - which recover part of what the form gives away. Those are a separate subject and a separate design decision, but naming the handover shows where hull-form optimisation stops.

13. What stops optimisation being a free gain

The stem says discuss, and a discussion that only lists benefits has not discussed anything. Every dimensional change is resisted by something.

  • Stability. Beam supplies transverse stability. Reducing it to cut resistance can compromise the intact and damage stability requirements, and for many ship types the subdivision and damage stability requirements of SOLAS chapter II-1 are what actually fix the minimum beam and the compartment arrangement.
  • Strength and weight. Longer and finer hulls need more structure for the same section modulus. Steel weight is deadweight forgone, so a hydrodynamic gain can be given straight back in cargo capacity.
  • Seakeeping. Calm-water optimisation is not service optimisation. A fine, slender hull may suffer greater motions, more slamming and greater added resistance in a seaway, so the ship that is best in the tank may not be best on the route she actually trades.
  • Manoeuvrability. Very long, slender hulls with small rudders handle poorly in confined water, and course-keeping in a following sea can suffer.
  • Regulatory measurement. Gross and net tonnage are functions of enclosed volume under the Tonnage Convention, and they drive port dues, canal tolls, manning and fee scales. A design change that adds enclosed volume adds cost for the ship's whole life.
  • Buildability and cost. Yard dock dimensions, crane capacity, plate curvature and the labour cost of complex shell forming all bear on whether a hydrodynamic optimum can actually be built at a price the owner will pay.

14. How the three interact, and the order in which they are decided

The three limbs are printed separately but they are one decision, and the sequence matters.

  1. The trade fixes the capacity and the route. What cargo, in what parcel size, between which ports, through which canals and over which channel depths. This bounds size, draught, beam and often length before any hydrodynamics is done.
  2. The service speed is then chosen, because it sizes the powering problem. It sets the installed power, and through the speed-length ratio it determines how full the hull is permitted to be.
  3. The principal dimensions are optimised within those two - length against wetted surface, beam against stability and resistance, draught against port limits, fullness against speed - and finally the afterbody and aperture are shaped around the largest efficient propeller the arrangement will accept.
  4. The result is checked against the required EEDI, against the stability and subdivision requirements and against the structural rules, and the loop is closed until it converges.

The proposition to close on is the one that unifies the whole question: size determines how much transport work the ship can do, speed determines how hard she must be pushed to do it, and the principal dimensions determine how much of the power actually turns into transport work rather than into waves and friction. Optimising any one in isolation is why so many efficient-looking designs disappoint in service; the gains the stem refers to come from taking all three together, against the trade the ship will actually serve.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The marks are printed 6 / 5 / 5 and the first limb carries the extra one, so give it the extra depth. The single most important thing you can write is the opening frame - efficiency here means energy per tonne-mile, not per day - because without it, limb (a) reads as a claim that burning more fuel is more efficient, and the examiner cannot give you credit for an argument you have not made.

The command verb is discuss, which means the trade-off must be argued. On every limb, give the gain and then give what resists it: scale against port and cargo limits, speed against voyage time and fleet size, fine lines against stability, strength, seakeeping and cargo capacity. A candidate who writes only the benefits has written a brochure, not an answer.

Keep the preamble to two short paragraphs. It is not a printed limb and it earns its place only by making the three that are printed coherent.

The idea underneath the question

A ship is a machine for converting fuel into transport work, and there are only three places the conversion can be improved. You can make the machine do more work per trip - that is size. You can make it work less hard - that is speed, because resistance climbs so steeply that a small reduction buys a large saving. Or you can make the conversion itself less wasteful - that is hull form, which decides how much of the engine's power ends up moving cargo rather than making waves and dragging skin.

What makes it a genuine engineering problem rather than a list is that the three are coupled and every one of them is bounded by something outside hydrodynamics. Size is bounded by canals, berths and, most often, by whether anyone has that much cargo. Speed is bounded by what the trade will pay for and by what the machinery tolerates at low load. Hull form is bounded by stability, by structure, by seakeeping and by the cost of bending steel. The optimum is not the hydrodynamic optimum; it is the best compromise available inside all of those fences.

Hold onto one more thing. All three are decided once, before the ship exists, on assumptions about fuel price, freight rate and trade pattern twenty-five years into the future - assumptions that will be wrong. That is why modern designs deliberately trade a little peak efficiency for robustness across a range of speeds and draughts, and why derating, adjustable margins and retrofittable devices exist at all.

The one relationship worth knowing cold

Propulsive power rises approximately with the cube of speed; fuel per mile therefore rises approximately with the square of speed. Everything about speed in this question follows from those two sentences.

But say approximately, and know why. The exponent is not a constant - it depends on hull form, on loading condition and on the speed-length ratio, and it climbs steeply once the ship begins to make significant waves. That is why pushing a ship above her design speed is punished so severely, and why the last knot always costs far more than the first. In the other direction the relationship breaks down at very low load, where the constraint stops being the hull and becomes the engine: incomplete combustion, cold-end corrosion, turbocharger mismatch, fouled gas ways and economiser. The hull would like you to go slower than the machinery will tolerate, and reconciling that is a real engineering task, not a rounding error.

Scale - the part candidates get half right

Everyone can say that a bigger ship is more efficient per tonne-mile. Fewer can say why, and fewer still can say when it stops being true. The why has three parts - volume grows faster than wetted surface; hotel and auxiliary loads do not scale with cargo; crew and overheads are per ship - plus the frequently forgotten fourth, that a larger, slower-running engine driving a larger, slower-turning propeller is itself a more efficient machine.

The when-it-stops is the discriminator, and it is almost always commercial rather than physical. The canal will take her, the berth will take her, and there simply is not enough cargo moving between those two ports at once to fill her. A ship that sails eighty per cent full, or that must ballast half her voyages because only a handful of ports can accept her, has given the whole saving back. Utilisation, not tonnage, is what converts scale into efficiency, and one sentence to that effect is worth more than a paragraph on economies of scale.

Reading a hull form in your head

You do not need to remember any coefficient values to answer limb (c) well. You need one distinction and one rule of thumb.

The distinction: friction follows surface, wave-making follows form and speed. Anything that adds skin costs friction; anything that makes the ship blunter or shorter for her speed costs waves.

The rule of thumb: a slow ship can afford to be full; a fast ship cannot. At low speed-length ratios wave-making is small, so the designer takes cargo capacity and cheap construction by filling the hull out. As speed rises, residuary resistance grows rapidly and the hull must be finer, longer and more slender to contain it. That is why a bulk carrier and a container ship of similar length look nothing like each other, and it is the sentence that ties limb (c) back to limb (b).

Add one asymmetry that catches people out: beam is hydrodynamically more expensive than length. Adding displacement by getting wider costs more resistance than adding it by getting longer or deeper - which is why efficiency-led designs push draught as far as the ports allow and add length as parallel middle body, and treat beam as something set by stability, by subdivision and by canal limits rather than as a free variable.

Where this meets the engine room

It is easy to read this as a naval architect's question with nothing in it for the operator. There are at least four direct consequences a Chief Engineer lives with.

  • The main engine you have was chosen for a design speed you may never run at. If the ship is being traded slow, you are operating a plant outside its design point, with all the low-load consequences that follow, and part of your job is managing that rather than assuming it is a fault.
  • The bulbous bow was tuned for a draught and a speed. In ballast at reduced speed it may be adding resistance. That is a design consequence, not a hull-fouling problem, and knowing the difference stops you chasing the wrong cause when the consumption looks wrong.
  • Propeller immersion in ballast is a design constraint you inherit. Racing, cavitation and vibration in light condition are largely decided by the aperture and the ballast draught the designer assumed.
  • Hull and propeller condition act directly on the frictional component, which is the largest single component on a full-form ship at moderate speed. Roughness and fouling are the one part of the resistance the operator genuinely controls, and under a carbon price - the subject of Q4 on this paper - they acquire a direct financial value.

Examiner traps

  • Answering in fuel per day. The question becomes unanswerable. Always per tonne-mile.
  • Saying bigger is always better. Only if she is full, and only within the canal, berth, draught and cargo limits.
  • Treating the cube law as exact. Say approximately, and say it degrades above the design speed and at very low load.
  • Turning it into an EEDI question. The stem is naval architecture. Refer to EEDI, EEXI, SEEMP and CII once each to show where design meets regulation, and quote no formula, reduction factor or phase date.
  • Confusing this with propulsion-improving devices. Wake-equalising ducts, pre-swirl stators and post-swirl fins are appendages fitted to a hull whose form is already fixed. Mention the handover; do not answer the wrong question.
  • Forgetting stability and subdivision. For many ship types the minimum beam is set by damage stability, not by hydrodynamics, and an answer that treats beam as freely reducible is wrong.
  • Quoting a block coefficient, an L/B ratio or a percentage saving. These are ship-type and design specific. The mechanisms carry the marks; invented numbers carry risk.

If you only have ten minutes for this question

Frame, two lines. Efficiency means energy per tonne-mile, not per day; the principal parameters are chosen once and fix the fuel bill for the ship's life.

(a) Size and capacity, six lines. Displacement grows with volume while wetted surface grows with area, so a bigger hull carries disproportionately more for the friction it creates; hotel, auxiliary and crew loads do not scale with cargo; and a larger, slower-running engine and propeller are themselves more efficient. Bounded by canals, locks, berth length, channel and terminal depth, air draught and crane reach - and above all by cargo availability, service frequency and flexibility. The saving is per tonne carried, so it depends entirely on utilisation: a part-loaded large ship or one with long ballast legs can be worse than a smaller ship that is always full. Scale improves the attained EEDI because the index is per unit of transport work, but it raises absolute emissions and does not protect a badly traded ship's CII rating.

(b) Service speed, five lines. Power rises approximately with the cube of speed, so fuel per mile rises approximately with the square - the most powerful lever there is, and the only one available after building. The exponent worsens above the design speed once wave-making sets in. The design speed permanently fixes installed power, engine selection, propeller design, the hull lines and the bulb, so off-design running carries a penalty that cannot be designed away and brings low-load machinery problems. Against the fuel saving: longer voyages, more ships needed for the same annual volume, slower delivery and higher inventory cost for the shipper. The economic speed is set by fuel price, freight rate and cargo value, none of which is known at design, which is why modern designs favour robustness across a range and use derating.

(c) Principal dimensions, five lines. Friction follows wetted surface; residuary resistance follows form and speed-length ratio, and length acts on the two in opposite directions. Greater length lowers wave-making but adds skin, steel weight and cost - so length is added as parallel middle body, which is cheap, carries cargo and is hydrodynamically almost neutral. Beam buys stability and capacity but is hydrodynamically expensive and is often fixed by subdivision and by canal limits. Deeper draught is generally efficient and improves propeller immersion, but is bounded by the assigned freeboard and by port depth. Fullness buys cargo and cheap construction but costs residuary resistance and spoils the wake - so a slow ship can afford to be full and a fast one cannot. Finally the afterbody and aperture must give a uniform wake and accept the largest, slowest-turning propeller that stays immersed in ballast. All of it is resisted by stability, subdivision, strength, seakeeping, manoeuvrability, tonnage measurement and building cost.

Common mistakes

These are the places this particular question goes wrong, taken from the ambiguities identified before drafting and from the single error most likely to cost marks.

The one that costs most: Answering in fuel per day instead of fuel per tonne-mile. In fuel per day a bigger, faster ship is always worse and the question becomes unanswerable. The second trap is asserting that bigger is always better - it is better ONLY IF SHE IS FULL, and the cargo availability, utilisation and ballast-leg limits are where the marks in limb (a) actually are. The third is treating the question as an EEDI question; the stem asks about naval architecture and mentions efficiency as the purpose.

The wording of the stem invites four specific misreadings, and each is resolved in the answer rather than left to chance:

  • 'Optimization of ship dimensions' is the stem's frame, but limb (a) is about SIZE AND CAPACITY and limb (b) is about SPEED, neither of which is a dimension. The stem is really about the principal design parameters, of which dimensions are one. The answer takes the three limbs as printed and does not quarrel with the framing, but it does say in the preamble that speed and capacity are chosen alongside dimensions rather than being dimensions themselves - which is what makes section 14 on the order of decisions possible.
  • 'Ships size and capacity' printed as one limb conflates two related but separable things: the physical size of the hull and the cargo capacity it provides. A ship can be large and carry little - a car carrier is voluminous and light; a small tanker is dense and deep. The answer separates volume-limited from weight-limited trades where it matters, because the economy of scale behaves differently in each.
  • 'Service speed' could be read as the operational speed on a given voyage or as the design service speed. The impact on energy efficiency differs: the first is an operational decision, the second is a design decision that fixes installed power for the ship's life. The answer takes it primarily as the DESIGN service speed, since the stem is about optimisation of the ship, and deals with operational speed reduction as the consequence.
  • The cubic power-speed relationship is an approximation. Its exponent varies with hull form, loading, Froude number and the onset of significant wave-making, and it is not reliable at very low load. It is presented as an approximation with those qualifications, and is not used to compute a numerical saving.

Likely oral follow-up

The retrieval questions below are the ones this answer is built to survive. They are the same questions an examiner reaches for when testing whether a written answer was understood or reproduced.

  • Give the route for the ship dimension optimisation question. — Printed 6/5/5. The preamble is not a printed limb so keep it to two short paragraphs, and give limb (a) the extra depth its extra mark buys.
  • Why is a very large ship 'efficient' when she burns far more fuel? — The frame for the entire question. Get this wrong and every limb reads as a contradiction.
  • What is the condition on which the economy of scale entirely depends? — The discriminating point in a six-mark limb. Anyone can say bigger is better; the marks are in the conditions.
  • State the speed-power relationship and what follows from it. — The one quantitative idea in the question, and it must be stated with its qualification - it is a first-principles approximation, not a law.
  • Which dimension acts on which resistance component? — This single distinction organises the whole of limb (c) and lets you reason about a dimension you have not memorised.
  • What does the choice of design service speed permanently fix? — This is why the question is about DESIGN. Operational speed reduction is a mitigation within a box that the design speed drew.
  • What pushes back against making a hull longer, finer and deeper? — 'Discuss' requires the trade-off. An answer that only lists benefits has not discussed anything.
  • How does hull form affect the propeller, and why does it matter? — Connects limb (c) to the machinery the candidate actually operates, and to the adjacent MIW question on wake-equalising and pre-swirl devices.

Memory framework

The route, chunked: PREAMBLE: per tonne-mile (1), chosen once (2). (a) SIZE: WHY IT WORKS (3), WHAT LIMITS IT (4), WHAT THE RULES DO (5). (b) SPEED: THE CUBE (6), FIXED FOR LIFE (7), THE TRADE (8). (c) DIMENSIONS: RESISTANCE (9), LENGTH (10), BEAM-DRAUGHT-FULLNESS (11), STERN AND PROPELLER (12). CLOSE: PUSHBACK (13), ORDER (14).

Fifteen-second recall: Efficiency = energy per tonne-mile, not per hour. SIZE: volume grows faster than wetted surface, and hotel, auxiliary and crew loads barely scale - so bigger is better per tonne-mile, BUT bounded by canals, ports, draught, berths and above all by cargo availability; a part-loaded big ship is worse than a full small one. SPEED: power rises roughly as V cubed, so fuel per mile roughly as V squared - the single biggest operational lever; design speed permanently fixes installed power, propeller and hull lines. DIMENSIONS: length lowers wave-making but adds wetted surface, weight and cost; beam adds stability and capacity but costs power; deeper draught is generally efficient but is limited by ports and the load line; fullness carries cargo but adds residuary resistance and spoils the wake; the afterbody and aperture must allow a large slow-turning well-immersed propeller. PUSHBACK: stability, subdivision, strength, seakeeping, tonnage and building cost.

The numbers and thresholds worth carrying exactly:

  • NO NUMERICAL VALUE IS ASSERTED ON THIS QUESTION - this is deliberate and is recorded in the answer object
  • The only relationship used is qualitative: propulsive power rises approximately with the CUBE of speed over a ship's normal operating range, so fuel per mile rises approximately with the SQUARE of speed
  • That relationship is an APPROXIMATION whose exponent varies with hull form, loading, Froude number and the onset of wave-making, and it degrades at very low load
  • Efficiency in this question is measured PER UNIT OF TRANSPORT WORK - per tonne-mile - which is the form EEDI, EEXI and the CII all take

Keywords that unlock the rest: energy efficiency, tonne-mile, transport work, economy of scale, economies of scale, wetted surface, frictional resistance, residuary resistance, wave-making resistance, block coefficient, prismatic coefficient, length to beam ratio, beam to draught ratio, slenderness, speed-length ratio, Froude number, parallel middle body, bulbous bow, design draught, service speed, design speed, propeller law, cube law, slow steaming, derating, engine margin, sea margin, wake, hull efficiency, propeller immersion, EEDI, EEXI, SEEMP, CII, load line, subdivision, seakeeping, added resistance in waves, tonnage measurement, deadweight, lightship

Regulation and source map

Critical regulation: MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 - EEDI for new ships, EEXI for existing ships, the SEEMP and the operational CII rating; the design-side of which is what makes dimensional optimisation a regulatory as well as a commercial matter. Named by function; no formula term or reduction factor is quoted.

Every instrument this answer relies on, in the order the answer uses them:

  • MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 - regulations on energy efficiency for ships
  • MARPOL Annex VI - the Energy Efficiency Design Index (EEDI) for new ships, and its required and attained values
  • MARPOL Annex VI - the Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index (EEXI)
  • MARPOL Annex VI - the Ship Energy Efficiency Management Plan (SEEMP)
  • MARPOL Annex VI - the operational carbon intensity indicator (CII) and its rating
  • Resolution MEPC.377(80) - 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, the policy driver behind design optimisation
  • International Convention on Load Lines, 1966 - the assigned freeboard and its effect on draught and deadweight
  • SOLAS chapter II-1 - subdivision and damage stability, a constraint on beam, length and compartment arrangement
  • International Convention on Tonnage Measurement of Ships, 1969 - gross and net tonnage as a function of enclosed volume

Evidence grades behind those instruments on this question: 1 read at source this session (P1), 5 authoritative secondary (P2), 3 carried from the verified MIW built set, and 30 engineering or legal judgement declared as such. 1 claims are stated at a lower evidence grade.

The sources themselves, with what was read and at what grade:

  • ENGINEERING_JUDGEMENT - naval architecture and marine engineering reasoned from first principles and from standard practice. The resistance decomposition into frictional and residuary components, the effect of length, beam, draught and fullness on each, the approximately cubic relationship between speed and propulsive power over a ship's normal operating range, the economy of scale in displacement-to-wetted-surface, and the consequences for installed power and engine selection are all standard, non-proprietary engineering and are presented as such.
  • P2 AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY - the structure of MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 and the existence and purpose of EEDI, EEXI, SEEMP and the CII. Named by identity and function. No EEDI reference-line constant, reduction-factor percentage, correction factor or formula term is quoted, because MIW's Annex VI copy was read this session for regulations 14 and 18 and appendix V and chapter 4 was not re-read for this question.
  • INTERNAL_REUSE_VERIFIED - the MIW house treatment of hull-form parameters as QUALITATIVE. Established across QP2410, QP2409, QP2404, QP2506 and QP2411, all of which discuss block coefficient without asserting a value. That treatment is followed here.
  • INTERNAL_REUSE_VERIFIED - QP2502-Q8 (propeller propulsion efficiency devices), read this session to establish the boundary between hull-form optimisation, which is this question, and appendage retrofitting, which is not, so that the two answers do not overlap or contradict.
  • P1 PRIMARY (context only) - MEPC.377(80), read at source this session for the levels of ambition that make design optimisation commercially and regulatorily worth doing at this sitting.

Uncertainty and evidence limits

What this answer deliberately does not assert, and why. These are declared limits rather than omissions, and a candidate revising from this answer should know where MIW stopped.

  • NO QUANTITY IS ASSERTED ANYWHERE ON THIS QUESTION. No block coefficient value, no length-to-beam or beam-to-draught ratio, no Froude number, no admiralty coefficient, no percentage fuel saving, no percentage power reduction, no speed in knots, no deadweight figure and no EEDI value or reduction factor appears. Every one of these is ship-type, size and design specific; MIW holds no naval-architecture text, no model-test data and no builder's project guidance; and MIW's evidence-declaration standard requires an unverifiable quantity to be omitted rather than given false precision. THE MECHANISMS ARE EXPLAINED QUALITATIVELY AND COMPLETELY, WHICH IS WHAT THE STEM ASKS FOR AND WHAT EARNS THE MARKS.
  • THE CUBIC POWER-SPEED RELATIONSHIP IS PRESENTED AS AN APPROXIMATION AND AN IDEALISATION, not as a law. It is stated as the standard first-principles relationship over a ship's normal operating range, with the qualification that the true exponent varies with hull form, loading condition, Froude number and the onset of wave-making, and that it degrades badly at very low load. No numerical exponent is used to compute a saving.
  • NO EEDI FORMULA TERM, REFERENCE LINE CONSTANT OR REDUCTION FACTOR IS QUOTED. The Index is named and its structure described in words - a measure of CO2 emitted per unit of transport work - because that is what the question needs. A session wishing to quote the formula must read Annex VI chapter 4 at source.
  • NO SHIP TYPE IS USED AS A WORKED NUMERICAL EXAMPLE. Container, tanker and bulk carrier practice is contrasted qualitatively where the contrast is instructive, without asserting dimensions, speeds or capacities for any of them.
  • NO MODEL TEST, CFD RESULT OR TOWING TANK DATA IS CITED. MIW holds none.

Claims stated at a lower evidence grade:

  • The naval-architecture reasoning throughout - the resistance decomposition, the dimensional effects, the economy of scale and the speed-power relationship. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION) — DECLARED AS ENGINEERING JUDGEMENT reasoned from first principles and standard practice. MIW holds no naval-architecture text, no model-test data and no builder's project guidance, so nothing here is attributed to a source and NO QUANTITY IS ASSERTED. The mechanisms are standard and non-proprietary; a session with a hydrodynamics text could add figures, and should do so only with attribution.
  • The cubic relationship between speed and propulsive power. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION) — An APPROXIMATION over the normal operating range, stated as such with its limitations - the exponent varies with hull form, loading, Froude number and the onset of wave-making, and the relationship is unreliable at very low load. It is used to explain the shape of the trade-off and is NOT used to compute a numerical saving.
  • The description of EEDI, EEXI, SEEMP and the CII by identity and function. (B_CURRENCY_CHECK) — P2. MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 was NOT re-read for this question - the Annex VI copy was read this session for regulations 14 and 18 and appendix V. No formula term, reference-line constant, reduction factor or phase date is quoted. The regulatory frame around design efficiency does tighten over time, so a reviewer moving this content to another sitting should re-check the phase position if any is ever added.

Flashcards 8

Preamble: 1 efficiency means energy per tonne-mile. 2 dimensions are fixed once and govern the ship's life. (a) 3 where the economy of scale comes from. 4 the limits and the utilisation condition. 5 the regulatory consequence. (b) 6 power rises roughly with the cube of speed. 7 the design speed fixes installed power, propeller and lines permanently. 8 the commercial trade-off. (c) 9 the resistance components. 10 length. 11 beam, draught and fullness. 12 afterbody and propeller. Close: 13 counter-pressures. 14 the order of decisions.

Printed 6/5/5. The preamble is not a printed limb so keep it to two short paragraphs, and give limb (a) the extra depth its extra mark buys.

Because efficiency here is energy per unit of TRANSPORT WORK - per tonne-mile - not per hour. Displacement grows with volume, roughly as the cube of linear size, while the wetted surface that generates frictional resistance grows as the square, so a larger hull carries disproportionately more cargo for the drag it creates. On top of that, the loads that do not scale with cargo - accommodation, lighting, ventilation, most auxiliary machinery, and the crew and stores - are spread across far more cargo. EEDI, EEXI and the CII are all expressed in this per-transport-work form.

The frame for the entire question. Get this wrong and every limb reads as a contradiction.

UTILISATION. The saving is per tonne CARRIED, so it is only realised if the ship is full. A large ship sailing part-loaded, or making long ballast legs because her size restricts the cargoes and ports available to her, can be WORSE per tonne-mile than a smaller ship that is consistently full. Scale is also bounded physically - canal and lock dimensions, port and berth length, air draught, channel depth, terminal crane reach and shore infrastructure - and commercially, because a larger parcel needs a larger buyer and a deeper trade.

The discriminating point in a six-mark limb. Anyone can say bigger is better; the marks are in the conditions.

Over a ship's normal operating range, propulsive power required rises approximately with the CUBE of speed. Since time on passage falls inversely with speed, fuel consumed PER MILE rises approximately with the SQUARE of speed. A modest reduction in speed therefore produces a disproportionately large reduction in fuel per mile, which is why speed is the single most powerful operational lever available. The relationship is an APPROXIMATION: the true exponent varies with hull form, loading condition and Froude number, rises sharply once significant wave-making begins, and the relationship is unreliable at very low load.

The one quantitative idea in the question, and it must be stated with its qualification - it is a first-principles approximation, not a law.

FRICTIONAL resistance follows WETTED SURFACE AREA, so it is increased by anything that adds skin - greater length, greater beam, deeper draught, more appendages. RESIDUARY resistance, chiefly WAVE-MAKING, follows the HULL FORM and the speed-length ratio, so it is reduced by greater length for a given displacement, by finer ends, by a lower block or prismatic coefficient, and by a bulbous bow tuned to the design speed and draught. The two pull in opposite directions on length: a longer hull makes smaller waves and drags more skin. That opposition is the central trade-off of hull-form design.

This single distinction organises the whole of limb (c) and lets you reason about a dimension you have not memorised.

The installed propulsive power and therefore the main engine selection - its bore, stroke, cylinder number and rated output; the propeller design, diameter and pitch, and with it the shaft speed; the hull lines, including the fineness of the ends and the size, shape and position of the bulbous bow, which are tuned to a particular speed AND draught; the shaft generator and waste-heat recovery arrangement, which depend on the power level; and the ship's EEDI at delivery. Off-design operation - notably a bulb tuned for a loaded design draught, running in ballast at reduced speed - carries a penalty the ship cannot design away afterwards.

This is why the question is about DESIGN. Operational speed reduction is a mitigation within a box that the design speed drew.

STABILITY and SUBDIVISION - beam gives metacentric height, and reducing it to cut resistance may compromise intact and damage stability under SOLAS chapter II-1. STRENGTH AND WEIGHT - longitudinal bending moment rises steeply with length, so a longer hull needs more steel, which costs money and eats deadweight. SEAKEEPING - a fine, slender hull can suffer greater motions and added resistance in a seaway, so the calm-water optimum is not the service optimum. DRAUGHT LIMITS - the assigned freeboard under the Load Line Convention, and the depth of ports, channels and berths. COMMERCIAL - building cost, yard berth and dock dimensions, tonnage measurement affecting dues and canal tolls, and cargo capacity lost to fine ends.

'Discuss' requires the trade-off. An answer that only lists benefits has not discussed anything.

The afterbody shape determines the WAKE the propeller works in. A fine, well-designed run gives a more uniform inflow, which raises propulsive efficiency, reduces cavitation, and lowers vibration and hull-pressure excitation. A very full afterbody produces a strongly non-uniform wake, which costs efficiency and can force a smaller or more heavily loaded propeller. The aperture and stern arrangement must allow the LARGEST, SLOWEST-TURNING propeller that will fit while remaining fully immersed in the ballast condition, because a large slow propeller is the more efficient one. This is where hull-form optimisation hands over to the propulsion-improving devices that address the wake directly.

Connects limb (c) to the machinery the candidate actually operates, and to the adjacent MIW question on wake-equalising and pre-swirl devices.

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

Fuel arrives on board as a promise and a liquid, and the two do not always match. The promise is a commercial specification - the buyer told the seller which grade was wanted, and that grade is a published list of properties with limits against each one. The liquid is whatever came out of the barge. So there are three separate questions to keep apart, and most confusion on this topic comes from running them together. First, is it what we ordered? That is the commercial specification, and if it is not, there is a claim to be made against the supplier. Second, is it lawful? A short list of things is forbidden by international law regardless of what anyone contracted for - it must essentially be a petroleum product, it must not contain acid, and it must not contain anything that damages the ship, harms the crew or makes the air worse - and the sulphur content is separately controlled because that is what the ship is certified against. Third, and this is the one that keeps you awake, can we safely burn it in this ship with this plant? That is not answered by the specification at all: a fuel can be perfectly on-spec and still be difficult for a particular installation, and a fuel slightly off-spec on one parameter may be entirely manageable while being off-spec on another makes it unusable. The laboratory report is what lets you answer all three, and the sensible order is to take the sample, hold the fuel, test it, and only then decide. And the reason such care is taken over one piece of paper at the end - who delivered what, when, of what sulphur content, with a sealed sample signed by both sides - is that when any of these three questions is disputed months later, in a port State inspection or in a claim, that document and that sample are the only evidence anyone has.

Knowledge map
ISO 8217 Marine Fuel Standards and the Bunker Delivery Note
  1. (a)
    1. 1The standard, named and placed
      • ISO 8217 is the international specification for marine fuels
      • It is a commercial specification, not a regulation
      • MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.3 is the mandatory floor beneath it
    2. 2How the specification is organised
      • Residual grades - the RM family
      • Distillate grades - the DM family
      • Grades distinguished by viscosity and by the parameter set applied
    3. 3The salient features of the specification
      • A table of characteristics with limits, each tied to a test method
      • Parameters split into those affecting handling, combustion, machinery wear and safety
      • General requirements: free of used lubricating oil and of material that harms the ship, the crew or the air
    4. 4The mandatory floor beneath the specification - MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.3
      • Blends of hydrocarbons from petroleum refining; small amounts of performance additives permitted
      • Free from inorganic acid
      • No added substance or chemical waste jeopardizing safety, harming machinery or personnel, or adding to air pollution
    5. 5How a fuel oil analysis report is read
      • Sample first, then test, then decide - before the fuel is used if possible
      • Compare against the ordered grade and against the ship's plant capability
      • Group the observations - handling, combustion, wear, safety, statutory
    6. 6Corrective action - density, viscosity and ignition quality
      • Density outside the separator's capability - check the plant type and settings
      • Viscosity - correct the preheating to reach injection viscosity, not a temperature
      • Poor calculated ignition quality - protect combustion timing and load changes
    7. 7Corrective action - catalytic fines
      • Aluminium plus silicon - abrasive, and they survive combustion
      • Optimise settling, heating and separation; slow the throughput
      • Sample after the separator, not only on delivery
    8. 8Corrective action - water, sediment, stability and compatibility
      • Water - drain, settle, purify; suspect the tank and the bunkering if it is high
      • Total sediment - a stability warning, not just dirt
      • Never blend incompatible fuels; segregate first, test compatibility before mixing
    9. 9Corrective action - sulphur and flashpoint
      • Sulphur is a statutory parameter, not merely a commercial one
      • Off-spec sulphur triggers reporting and record obligations
      • Low flashpoint is a safety matter and may require the fuel to be rejected outright
    10. 10Corrective action - ash, metals and micro-carbon residue
      • Vanadium and sodium - hot corrosion of exhaust valves and turbine components
      • Used lubricating oil markers - calcium, zinc, phosphorus - a ground for rejection
      • High carbon residue - deposits, fouling and poorer combustion
    11. 11The decision that sits above all the corrective actions
      • Use with precautions, use with restrictions, segregate, or reject and debunker
      • Notify owners, charterers, supplier and P&I early, in writing
      • The MARPOL sample is the evidence on which any claim will turn
  2. (b)
    1. 12What it is, and where it comes from
      • Required by MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.5 for ships subject to regulations 5 and 6
      • It must contain at least the information in appendix V
      • It is a statutory document, not a commercial receipt
    2. 13Its contents, retention and inspection
      • The ten appendix V items, including the signed and certified supplier's declaration
      • Kept readily available for inspection and retained three years
      • Port State may inspect, copy and require the master to certify the copy
    3. 14The representative sample, and why the BDN matters
      • Sealed and signed by the supplier's representative and the master or officer in charge
      • Retained under the ship's control until the fuel is substantially consumed
      • It is the evidence for both statutory defence and commercial claim
