  • Sample first, then test, then decide - before the fuel is used if possible
  • Compare against the ordered grade and against the ship's plant capability
  • Group the observations - handling, combustion, wear, safety, statutory
6. Corrective action - density, viscosity and ignition quality
  • Density outside the separator's capability - check the plant type and settings
  • Viscosity - correct the preheating to reach injection viscosity, not a temperature
  • Poor calculated ignition quality - protect combustion timing and load changes
7. Corrective action - catalytic fines
  • Aluminium plus silicon - abrasive, and they survive combustion
  • Optimise settling, heating and separation; slow the throughput
  • Sample after the separator, not only on delivery
8. Corrective action - water, sediment, stability and compatibility
  • Water - drain, settle, purify; suspect the tank and the bunkering if it is high
  • Total sediment - a stability warning, not just dirt
  • Never blend incompatible fuels; segregate first, test compatibility before mixing
9. Corrective action - sulphur and flashpoint
  • Sulphur is a statutory parameter, not merely a commercial one
  • Off-spec sulphur triggers reporting and record obligations
  • Low flashpoint is a safety matter and may require the fuel to be rejected outright
10. Corrective action - ash, metals and micro-carbon residue
  • Vanadium and sodium - hot corrosion of exhaust valves and turbine components
  • Used lubricating oil markers - calcium, zinc, phosphorus - a ground for rejection
  • High carbon residue - deposits, fouling and poorer combustion
11. The decision that sits above all the corrective actions
  • Use with precautions, use with restrictions, segregate, or reject and debunker
  • Notify owners, charterers, supplier and P&I early, in writing
  • The MARPOL sample is the evidence on which any claim will turn
12. What it is, and where it comes from
  • Required by MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.5 for ships subject to regulations 5 and 6
  • It must contain at least the information in appendix V
  • It is a statutory document, not a commercial receipt
13. Its contents, retention and inspection
  • The ten appendix V items, including the signed and certified supplier's declaration
  • Kept readily available for inspection and retained three years
  • Port State may inspect, copy and require the master to certify the copy
14. The representative sample, and why the BDN matters
  • Sealed and signed by the supplier's representative and the master or officer in charge
  • Retained under the ship's control until the fuel is substantially consumed
  • It is the evidence for both statutory defence and commercial claim
Model written answer

(a) The ISO marine fuel standard, its salient features, and corrective action on an analysis report (12 marks)

1. The standard, named and placed

The international specification for marine fuels is ISO 8217, Petroleum products - Fuels (class F) - Specifications of marine fuels. It is supported by a family of separate test-method standards, each defining how one characteristic is measured, and by a companion standard governing the precision of test results and how a disputed result is interpreted - which matters commercially, because two laboratories testing the same fuel will not return identical numbers and the specification has to say when a difference is real.

Two things must be said about this standard before anything else, because everything downstream depends on them.

  • It is a commercial specification, not a regulation. ISO has no power to legislate. ISO 8217 binds because a bunker contract incorporates it by reference: the buyer orders a named grade, and the seller warrants delivery to that grade. A fuel that fails it gives rise to a claim against the supplier, not to a breach of international law.
  • It is periodically revised. Successive editions have adjusted the parameter set - for example, in response to the arrival of very low sulphur fuel oils after the 2020 sulphur limit, and to concerns about used lubricating oil and about hydrogen sulphide. MIW holds no copy of ISO 8217 in any edition and therefore does not state which numbered edition was current at this sitting, quote any table, or assert any limit value. That is a declared limitation. It costs the answer nothing, because the marks below are for what the specification does and for what to do when a fuel fails it - neither of which turns on the edition year.

2. How the specification is organised

ISO 8217 divides marine fuels into two families and specifies each by grade:

FamilyDesignationCharacterTypical use
ResidualRM gradesThe heavy fraction remaining after refining, blended to a required viscosity. Contains asphaltenes, ash, metals and catalyst residuesMain propulsion and larger auxiliaries, after heating and centrifugal treatment
DistillateDM gradesLighter, cleaner fractions condensed during refining. Little or no ash and metalsAuxiliary engines, emergency machinery, boilers, and residual-engine operation where required

Grades within each family are distinguished chiefly by viscosity and by the parameter set applied to them, and the specification tabulates a list of characteristics against each grade, giving for every characteristic a limit - a maximum, a minimum, or a range - and the test method by which it is to be determined. That structure is the single most important thing to say about it: the specification is not a description of fuel, it is a table of measurable properties with limits and the methods for measuring them.

3. The salient features of the specification

  1. Characteristics with limits, each tied to a named test method. Without the method the limit is meaningless, because different methods give different answers for the same property.
  2. Parameters chosen for what they threaten, not for chemical interest. They fall into recognisable groups: handling and treatment (density, viscosity, pour point, water); combustion behaviour (ignition quality, carbon residue); wear and corrosion (catalytic fines, ash, vanadium, sodium); and safety and legality (flashpoint, sulphur, hydrogen sulphide, acid number).
  3. General requirements applying to all grades, above and beyond the table - most importantly that the fuel shall be free from used lubricating oil, and free from any material that renders it unacceptable for use, harms the ship's machinery, endangers personnel or contributes to additional air pollution.
  4. A clause on the interpretation of test results, so that a marginal difference between the supplier's and the receiver's laboratory does not automatically become a dispute.
  5. The specification is a floor for quality, not a guarantee of suitability. A fuel can meet its grade in every respect and still be difficult for a particular installation - because suitability depends on the ship's heating, settling and separation capacity, on her engine, and on how she is traded. Saying this shows judgement and it is true.

4. The mandatory floor beneath the specification - MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.3

Underneath the commercial specification sits a short list of requirements that are mandatory international law, and an answer that names them separately from ISO 8217 has understood the structure. Fuel oil for combustion purposes delivered to and used on board ships to which the Annex applies shall meet the following requirements:

  • the fuel oil shall be blends of hydrocarbons derived from petroleum refining - which does not preclude the incorporation of small amounts of additives intended to improve some aspects of performance;
  • the fuel oil shall be free from inorganic acid; and
  • the fuel oil shall not include any added substance or chemical waste that jeopardizes the safety of ships or adversely affects the performance of the machinery, or is harmful to personnel, or contributes overall to additional air pollution.

For fuel derived by methods other than petroleum refining, the equivalent requirements are that it shall not exceed the applicable sulphur content under regulation 14, shall not cause an engine to exceed the applicable NOx limits under regulation 13, shall not contain inorganic acid, and shall not jeopardize safety, adversely affect machinery performance, harm personnel or contribute to additional air pollution.

Regulation 18.4 records that the regulation does not apply to coal in its solid form or nuclear fuels, and that paragraphs 5, 6, 7.1, 7.2, 8.1, 8.2, 9.2, 9.3 and 9.4 do not apply to gas fuels such as LNG, CNG or LPG, whose sulphur content is instead documented by the supplier.

Status note - and it is a scoring point
MEPC.385(81), adopted 22 March 2024 at MEPC 81, amends regulation 18 substantially - among other things replacing the definition of fuel oil, inserting a definition of gas fuel, and splitting paragraph 5 into 5.1 and 5.2 to create a separate bunker delivery note regime for low-flashpoint and gas fuels.Those amendments enter into force on 1 August 2025. At this sitting they are ADOPTED AND NOT IN FORCE, and the text set out above and in limb (b) is the operative one. Saying this correctly - adopted, not yet in force - is worth a mark; describing the amendment's content as the requirement is an error

5. How a fuel oil analysis report is read

Before any corrective action, the method. Sample, hold, test, then decide. The sample is drawn continuously at the ship's manifold throughout the delivery, sealed and signed by both sides; a portion goes to the laboratory and the MARPOL sample is retained unopened. Where the arrangement allows it, the new bunker is segregated and not brought into use until the result is back - which is the single most valuable habit in bunker management, because almost every corrective action below is easier before the fuel enters the service system.

The report is then read against two references, not one: against the grade ordered, which tells you whether there is a commercial claim; and against the ship's own capability - her heating, settling, separation and engine - which tells you whether the fuel can be used. And the observations are best grouped by consequence rather than taken line by line, because that is how the corrective actions group too.

6. Corrective action - density, viscosity and ignition quality

Adverse observationWhat it threatensCorrective action
Density high, approaching or beyond the separator's capabilityConventional gravity-disc separation depends on a density difference between oil and water. Too high a density and water will not separate reliablyConfirm the separator type and its rated capability; where the installation has a modern separator without gravity discs, set it for the fuel; where it does not, reduce throughput to increase residence time and drain the settling tank rigorously. Where the fuel exceeds the plant's capability, treat it as a suitability failure and consider segregation or rejection
Viscosity outside the ordered gradeToo high and the fuel cannot be brought to injection viscosity; too low and the fuel pump loses lubrication and leaks internallyCorrect to a viscosity, not to a temperature. Adjust the viscotherm or heater control to achieve the injection viscosity the engine builder requires, and check the heater has the capacity to do so. Where viscosity is far below grade, watch fuel pump and injector wear and consider whether a cooler is needed
Poor ignition and combustion quality (indicated by a calculated index from density and viscosity, or by a direct ignition test)Ignition delay, rapid pressure rise, high thermal and mechanical loading, difficult starting and manoeuvring, and afterburning with high exhaust temperatures and foulingRaise the fuel temperature to the top of the acceptable injection range; maintain injection equipment in good condition and verify timing; avoid rapid load changes and prolonged low-load running; keep charge air temperature and pressure up; and where the engine is electronically controlled, consider adjusting the injection profile within the builder's permitted range. Monitor cylinder condition more closely

7. Corrective action - catalytic fines

Aluminium plus silicon reported together are catalytic fines: particles of the alumino-silicate catalyst used in refinery cracking. They matter more than almost any other parameter because they are extremely hard, extremely abrasive, and not consumed by combustion. They score liners, piston rings, fuel pump plungers and injector nozzles, and the damage is cumulative and permanent.

  1. Maximise the treatment chain. Adequate settling time with proper draining; correct treatment temperature at the separator, since separation efficiency improves as viscosity falls; and reduce separator throughput so that residence time in the bowl increases. Run separators in series where the installation permits it rather than in parallel.
  2. Drain the tanks. Fines are denser than the fuel and concentrate at the bottom of settling and service tanks. Regular, disciplined draining removes a great deal of what has already separated.
  3. Sample after the separator, not only at the manifold. The delivered figure tells you what you were sold; the figure at the engine inlet tells you what your plant is actually letting through, and that is the number that damages the machinery. This is the single most useful piece of practice in the whole answer.
  4. Operate more conservatively while the fuel is in use - avoid rapid load changes, and increase the frequency of scavenge space inspections and of liner and ring inspection, watching for the fine vertical scoring that fines produce.
  5. Segregate the bunker if it can be isolated, and if the delivered level is beyond what the plant can handle, treat it as a rejection case.
  6. Notify and preserve. Written notice to owners, charterers and supplier; MARPOL sample retained unopened as the evidence.

8. Corrective action - water, sediment, stability and compatibility

  • Water high. Free water is drained; emulsified water is removed by settling and purification. High water on delivery is also a quantity question - the ship has been charged for it - so it belongs in the note of protest as well as in the engine room. Persistent water later in the voyage points at the ship rather than the supplier: a leaking heating coil, a tank top defect or condensation, and the source must be found rather than merely purified away.
  • Total sediment high. This is not simply dirt. It is the principal warning that the fuel's asphaltenes are not securely held in suspension. The consequence is sludge, blocked filters and purifiers, an overwhelmed separation plant, and in the worst case loss of fuel supply to the engine.
  • Stability and compatibility - the most important procedural point in this limb. Stability is a fuel's ability to hold its own asphaltenes in suspension. Compatibility is whether two fuels, each perfectly stable alone, remain stable when mixed - and a paraffinic fuel blended with an aromatic one frequently will not. The corrective action here is not mechanical, it is stowage and procedure: segregate new bunkers from existing ones; do not commingle in a tank without a compatibility test; run a tank down before changing over where the arrangement allows; change over gradually and watch the filter differential pressure as you do it; and keep the purifier and filter manning appropriate to the risk.

A sludging event from an incompatible blend can immobilise a ship faster than any single off-specification parameter, and it is entirely avoidable by planning. That is worth saying explicitly.

9. Corrective action - sulphur and flashpoint

These two are different in kind from everything above, because they are statutory rather than merely commercial.

  • Sulphur above the applicable limit. This is not simply an off-spec fuel; it is a potential breach of MARPOL Annex VI regulation 14 and of the ship's certification. The corrective action is immediate and largely administrative: do not burn it in a controlled area; segregate it; enter the facts in the oil record book and the engine log; notify the flag State and the port State authority as required, and the supplier, owners and charterers in writing; retain the MARPOL sample unopened, since it is the sample on which any enforcement or defence will turn; and where compliant fuel genuinely cannot be obtained, use the fuel oil non-availability procedure in regulation 18.2 - presenting a record of the actions taken and evidence of the attempt to purchase compliant fuel in accordance with the voyage plan, notifying the Administration and the competent authority of the port of destination.
  • Flashpoint low. This is the one adverse observation on which the correct answer is frequently outright rejection. A flashpoint below the required minimum for fuel oil used on board creates an explosion risk in the bunker and settling tanks that no treatment can remove and no operating precaution reliably contains. It engages both the ship's safety construction requirements and the appendix V declaration on the bunker delivery note. Debunker.

10. Corrective action - ash, metals and micro-carbon residue

Adverse observationWhat it threatensCorrective action
Vanadium and sodium high, particularly with an adverse ratio between themHot corrosion. Vanadium and sodium form low-melting-point compounds that deposit on and attack exhaust valve seats, valve spindles and turbocharger nozzle rings and blades. This is the classic burnt-valve mechanismReduce the sodium load by removing salt water - efficient settling, draining and purification, since much sodium arrives as seawater contamination. Keep exhaust valve temperatures under control; increase inspection frequency of valves and of the turbocharger gas side; ensure valve rotators are working; and consider a fuel treatment additive only where the builder accepts it, since additives are themselves controlled by regulation 18.3
Ash highAbrasive and deposit-forming; carries the metals and the catalytic finesImprove settling and separation; the same treatment chain as for fines
Micro-carbon residue highA tendency to form carbon deposits - fouling of injector tips, ring grooves, exhaust ports, the turbocharger gas side and the economiser, with poorer combustion followingEnsure good atomisation - injector condition and correct injection viscosity - and adequate combustion air; monitor exhaust temperatures and turbocharger performance; increase the frequency of scavenge and gas-side inspection; and be alert to economiser fouling, which carries a fire risk of its own
Used lubricating oil markers - calcium with zinc, or calcium with phosphorusUsed lubricating oil in the fuel. It brings additive metals and wear debris and it is expressly excluded by the specification; depending on what else it carries it may also engage MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.3This is a rejection case. Segregate immediately, do not use, notify all parties in writing without delay, preserve the sample, and debunker. Do not treat this as a parameter to be managed by better purification

11. The decision that sits above all the corrective actions

The report is not an end in itself. It exists so that one of four decisions can be taken, and an answer that reaches this point has answered 'corrective action which can be initiated' properly.

  1. Use as normal - the deviation is marginal and within what the plant and the engine tolerate.
  2. Use with precautions - enhanced treatment, closer monitoring of filter differential pressures, exhaust temperatures, liner and ring condition and lubricating oil condition, and more frequent scavenge inspections.
  3. Use with restrictions - segregated to particular tanks, avoided for manoeuvring, for pilotage and for critical passages, or with engine load or rate of load change limited.
  4. Reject and debunker - the right answer where the fuel is unsafe or unlawful: a low flashpoint, contamination with used lubricating oil or chemical waste, or a breach of regulation 18.3.

Alongside whichever is chosen, three things happen every time. Notify in writing and early - owners, charterers, the supplier, and where appropriate the P&I club - because bunker claims are time-sensitive and a late notice is often a lost claim. Preserve the MARPOL sample unopened, because it is the evidence on which both the statutory position and the commercial claim will be decided. And record everything - the note of protest, the log entries, the oil record book, the analysis report and the correspondence - since months later the paperwork is all that will remain.

(b) The Bunker Delivery Note - salient features and importance (4 marks)

12. What it is, and where it comes from

The Bunker Delivery Note is a statutory document required by MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.5, not a commercial delivery receipt. For each ship subject to regulations 5 and 6 of the Annex - that is, the ships subject to survey and certification under it - the details of fuel oil for combustion purposes delivered to and used on board shall be recorded by means of a bunker delivery note that shall contain at least the information specified in appendix V.

The words at least matter: appendix V is a minimum, and a supplier or a charterparty may require more. The document is the point at which the fuel's identity, quantity, key properties and legal conformity are fixed in writing by the party that supplied it.

13. Its contents, retention and inspection

Appendix V - Information to be included in the bunker delivery note (regulation 18.5):

  1. Name and IMO number of receiving ship
  2. Port
  3. Date of commencement of delivery
  4. Name, address and telephone number of marine fuel oil supplier
  5. Product name(s)
  6. Quantity in metric tonnes
  7. Density at 15 degrees Celsius
  8. Sulphur content (% m/m)
  9. The flashpoint, specified in accordance with standards acceptable to the Organization, or a statement that the flashpoint has been measured at or above 70 degrees Celsius
  10. A declaration signed and certified by the fuel oil supplier's representative that the fuel oil supplied is in conformity with regulation 18.3 and that its sulphur content does not exceed the limit given by regulation 14.1, or the limit given by regulation 14.4, or the purchaser's specified limit value - the applicable box being marked with a cross by the supplier's representative, the last option being available where the fuel is to be used with an equivalent means of compliance under regulation 4 or under a regulation 3.2 trials exemption

Retention and inspection:

  • The BDN shall be kept on board in such a place as to be readily available for inspection at all reasonable times, and shall be retained for a period of three years after the fuel oil has been delivered on board.
  • The competent authority of a Party may inspect the bunker delivery notes on board any ship to which the Annex applies while she is in its port or offshore terminal, may make a copy of each delivery note, and may require the master or person in charge to certify that each copy is a true copy. It may also verify the contents through consultation with the port where the note was issued.
  • That inspection shall be performed as expeditiously as possible without causing the ship to be unduly delayed - a protection for the ship that is worth knowing.

14. The representative sample, and why the BDN matters

The sample. The bunker delivery note shall be accompanied by a representative sample of the fuel oil delivered, taking into account the guidelines developed by the Organization. The sample is to be sealed and signed by the supplier's representative and the master or officer in charge of the bunkering operation on completion of bunkering operations, and retained under the ship's control until the fuel oil is substantially consumed. The note and the sample are a pair, and neither is worth much without the other.

Why it matters - four reasons, and this is what the four marks are for.

  1. It is the statutory record of compliance. The declared sulphur content and the supplier's signed conformity declaration are how the ship demonstrates, to a port State control officer months later and in another hemisphere, that the fuel she burned was lawful.
  2. It is the primary evidence in any dispute. Quantity short, quality off, sulphur exceeded, the wrong grade delivered - every one of those arguments is conducted on the note and the sealed sample. A note accepted without protest, or a sample improperly drawn or unsigned, weakens the ship's position irrecoverably.
  3. It is a technical input, not merely paperwork. Product name, density, sulphur and flashpoint tell the Chief Engineer immediately what the treatment plant must do with this fuel, how it must be segregated, and whether it can go into the tank that is already part full.
  4. It is a port State control target. The BDNs and the samples are among the first documents inspected under Annex VI, and a missing note, a note outside the three-year retention or a note without the appendix V minimum content is a deficiency in its own right - before anyone has tested a drop of the fuel.

A closing observation ties the two limbs together. The analysis report tells you what you actually got; the bunker delivery note and its sealed sample are what let you prove it. Every corrective action in limb (a) that involves a claim, a defence or a notification depends on the document in limb (b) having been completed properly at the manifold - which is why four marks are attached to what looks, at first glance, like a receipt.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The marks are 12 and 4, and that is an unusual split which tells you exactly how to spend your time. Limb (a) is three quarters of the question and it contains three chained demands: name the standard, explain its salient features, and then explain the corrective action against adverse observations in an analysis report. The third of those is the biggest and it is where most of the twelve marks sit, so do not spend six minutes writing an essay about ISO 8217 and then list four parameters.

The way to cover the corrective action efficiently is to group by consequence rather than working down the report line by line. Five groups - density/viscosity/ignition, catalytic fines, water/sediment/stability, sulphur/flashpoint, ash/metals/carbon residue - cover essentially every parameter a report contains, and each group has a coherent set of actions. A candidate working alphabetically through fifteen parameters will still be on aluminium when the time runs out.

Limb (b) is four marks and it is nearly free if you know appendix V. Give the list, give the three-year retention, give the sealed sample, give one sentence on why it matters. Do not write a page - there are only four marks there and there are eight waiting in limb (a)'s corrective action.

The idea underneath the question

There are three separate questions to ask about a delivered bunker and almost all confusion on this topic comes from running them together.

QuestionGoverned byIf the answer is no
Is it what we ordered?The bunker contract, incorporating ISO 8217A commercial claim against the supplier
Is it lawful?MARPOL Annex VI regulations 14 and 18.3A statutory problem - port State control, flag State, the ship's certification
Can we safely burn it in THIS ship?The ship's heating, settling and separation plant, and her engineAn engineering problem - treat, restrict, segregate or reject

The three overlap but they are not the same, and the same analysis report answers all three. A fuel can be off-spec without being unlawful. A fuel can be entirely on-spec and still be unsuitable for a ship whose separator cannot handle its density. And a fuel can be lawful and on-spec and still be dangerous to blend with what is already in the tank. If you can keep those three apart, you can answer any version of this question, including the ones that give you a fact pattern.

The single most valuable habit in bunker management

Sample, hold, test, then decide. Almost every corrective action in this answer is easier, cheaper and more effective before the fuel enters the service system. Once a doubtful bunker has been commingled with a good one in a service tank, the options collapse: you cannot segregate it, you cannot reject it, you have contaminated a known-good parcel, and if the two are incompatible you may have created sludge throughout the system.

The corollary is about stowage. Bunker planning is not just about where the weight goes; it is about keeping parcels separable until they are proved. Where the tank arrangement permits it, this is the cheapest risk control on the ship.

Catalytic fines - why they get their own section

If you have room for only one worked example of corrective action, make it this one, because it demonstrates every layer of response in a single parameter.

  • The threat is physical and permanent. Alumino-silicate catalyst particles are harder than the surfaces they meet and they are not burned. Every hour they are in the fuel they are removing metal from liners, rings, pump plungers and nozzles, and nothing you do later puts it back.
  • The treatment response is about residence time, not about equipment. Settling with proper draining, correct separator treatment temperature, and above all reduced throughput. Slowing the purifier down is counter-intuitive and it is the right answer.
  • The measurement response is the insight. The delivered figure tells you what you bought; the figure after the separator tells you what your engine is eating. Sampling at the engine inlet is what turns a laboratory result into plant management, and very few candidates say it.
  • The operational response - avoid rapid load changes, inspect scavenge spaces and liners more often, look for fine vertical scoring.
  • The commercial response - written notice, sealed sample, claim.

Notice how much of that is available without any special equipment. That is the shape of a good corrective-action answer: what you can actually do, in the order you would actually do it.

Stability and compatibility - the failure that stops ships

These two words are used loosely and the distinction is worth being precise about. Stability is whether a fuel holds its own asphaltenes in suspension. Compatibility is whether two fuels, each stable on its own, stay stable when mixed. Residual fuels vary widely in how paraffinic or aromatic they are, and mixing across that divide can drop the asphaltenes out of solution as sludge.

The consequence is not gradual. Filters block, the purifier is overwhelmed, sludge tanks fill, and fuel supply to the main engine can be lost - and it happens in hours, not weeks. The defence is entirely procedural: segregate, test before commingling, run down before changing over, change over gradually, and watch the filter differentials while you do it. That is why total sediment in a report deserves more attention than its position in the table suggests: it is the early warning for a failure mode that no amount of purification will fix once it has started.

The temporal point on this question, and why it is worth marks

This is the question on the paper where the law was in motion at the sitting. MEPC.385(81) was adopted at MEPC 81 on 22 March 2024 and entered into force on 1 August 2025. In August 2024 it was adopted and not in force - which means the regulation 18 in force was the older text.

At the sitting (pre-amendment)From 1 August 2025 (post-amendment)
Reg 18.3 chapeau: 'fuel oil for combustion purposes delivered to and used on board ships''fuel oil delivered to and used on board a ship'
One paragraph 5 - one BDN regime, appendix V5.1 and 5.2 - a separate BDN regime for low-flashpoint and gas fuels
Reg 18.4 disapplies paras 5, 6, 7.1, 7.2, 8.1, 8.2, 9.2, 9.3, 9.4 to gas fuelsDisapplies only 5.1, 8.1, 8.2 to low-flashpoint and gas fuel
No definition of gas fuel in regulation 2Gas fuel defined; fuel oil redefined as any fuel delivered to and intended for use on board

There is a real exam lesson here beyond this one paper. Adopted is not in force. An amendment adopted before your sitting and entering into force after it is not the law you should be describing - but naming it and saying it is not yet in force is a mark, because it shows you know the difference. Both halves matter: describe the operative text, then note the amendment and its date.

Examiner traps

  • Calling ISO 8217 a regulation. It is a commercial specification. MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.3 is the mandatory floor.
  • Quoting an ISO 8217 limit value or edition year that you cannot verify. Name the standard, describe what it does, and put your effort into the corrective action - that is where the marks are.
  • Writing the post-2025 regulation 18. See the table above.
  • Treating every adverse observation as a purification problem. Low flashpoint and used lubricating oil are rejection cases, and sulphur is a statutory matter. Purifying harder is the wrong answer to all three.
  • Forgetting the commercial and statutory response. Corrective action includes the note of protest, the written notification, the preserved sample and, where compliant fuel is unobtainable, the regulation 18.2 non-availability procedure.
  • Blending two bunkers because both are on-spec. On-spec says nothing about compatibility.
  • Describing the BDN as a receipt. It is a statutory document with a minimum content, a three-year retention period, an inspection regime and an accompanying sealed sample.
  • Giving a threshold value for cat fines, water or sediment. These are grade, plant and engine specific. The mechanism earns the mark; a wrong number costs one.

If you only have ten minutes for this question

(a), eight lines. The specification is ISO 8217, supported by separate test-method standards - a commercial specification incorporated into the bunker contract, dividing marine fuels into residual RM and distillate DM grades and tabulating characteristics with limits and test methods for each. Its salient features: parameters chosen for what they threaten - handling and treatment, combustion, wear and corrosion, safety and legality; general requirements including freedom from used lubricating oil; a rule for interpreting test results; and the fact that meeting the grade is a floor for quality, not a guarantee of suitability for a particular ship. Beneath it, MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.3 is mandatory: blends of hydrocarbons from petroleum refining, free from inorganic acid, and no added substance or chemical waste jeopardizing safety, harming machinery or personnel, or adding to air pollution. On the report: sample, hold, test, then decide, reading it against both the grade ordered and the ship's own plant. Corrective action by group - density and viscosity: match the separator's capability, reduce throughput, and heat to an injection viscosity not a temperature; poor ignition quality: heat to the top of the range, keep injection equipment right, avoid rapid load change and low load; catalytic fines: settle, drain, treat at the right temperature, slow the purifier, run in series, and sample after the separator; water and sediment: drain, settle, purify, and above all segregate and test before commingling, since incompatible fuels sludge; sulphur: statutory - segregate, log, notify, retain the sample, and use the regulation 18.2 non-availability procedure if compliant fuel is unobtainable; low flashpoint or used lubricating oil markers: reject and debunker; vanadium and sodium: remove salt water, control exhaust valve temperatures, inspect valves and turbocharger more often. Above all of it, choose: use, use with precautions, use with restrictions, or reject - and notify in writing early, preserve the MARPOL sample and record everything.

(b), four lines. The Bunker Delivery Note is required by MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.5 for ships subject to regulations 5 and 6, and must contain at least the ten items in appendix V: receiving ship's name and IMO number; port; date of commencement of delivery; supplier's name, address and telephone number; product name; quantity in metric tonnes; density at 15 degrees Celsius; sulphur content; flashpoint or a statement that it is at or above 70 degrees Celsius; and a signed and certified supplier's declaration of conformity with regulation 18.3 and of the applicable sulphur limit. It is kept readily available for inspection and retained three years; a port State may inspect it, copy it and require the master to certify the copy, without unduly delaying the ship; and it must be accompanied by a representative sample, sealed and signed by the supplier's representative and the master or officer in charge, retained under the ship's control until the fuel is substantially consumed. It matters because it is the statutory record of compliance, the primary evidence in any quantity or quality dispute, an immediate technical input to how the fuel is treated and stowed, and one of the first documents a port State control officer asks for.

Common mistakes

These are the places this particular question goes wrong, taken from the ambiguities identified before drafting and from the single error most likely to cost marks.

The one that costs most: WRITING THE POST-2025 REGULATION 18. MEPC.385(81) split paragraph 5 into 5.1 and 5.2, creating a separate BDN regime for low-flashpoint and gas fuels, redefined 'fuel oil' as any fuel delivered to and intended for use on board, inserted a definition of 'gas fuel', and rewrote paragraph 4. NONE of that was in force in August 2024. The second trap is treating ISO 8217 as a regulation - it is a commercial specification, and MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.3 is the mandatory floor. The third is quoting an ISO 8217 limit value or edition year, neither of which MIW can verify.

The wording of the stem invites four specific misreadings, and each is resolved in the answer rather than left to chance:

  • 'The present ISO standards' is printed in the PLURAL. Strictly there is one specification for marine fuels, ISO 8217, supported by separate test-method and precision standards. The answer names the specification and explains that it is supported by a family of test-method standards, without asserting the number of any of them, because MIW holds none.
  • 'The present' is a date-sensitive word on a question whose answer object must be true at a sitting two years in the past. The answer names the standard and expressly declines to assert which numbered edition was current, stating the reason. This is a declared limitation, not an evasion, and it costs no marks because the marks are for the features and the corrective action.
  • 'Corrective action which can be initiated against various adverse observations' is deliberately open. It could be read as laboratory action, as engine-room action, or as commercial action. It is all three, and the answer says so - treatment plant, operating precautions, and the notification and claim route - because a Chief Engineer who purifies harder and tells nobody has taken half the action available.
  • The relationship between ISO 8217 and MARPOL is frequently confused. ISO 8217 is a COMMERCIAL SPECIFICATION incorporated by reference into a bunker contract; MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.3 is a MANDATORY requirement of international law. A fuel can be off-specification without breaching MARPOL, and can breach MARPOL while meeting most of the specification. The answer makes the distinction explicitly.
  • Regulation 18.5 as it stood at this sitting speaks of 'fuel oil FOR COMBUSTION PURPOSES'. The 2024 amendments remove that phrase and split the paragraph. The pre-amendment wording is used and the amendment's status is stated separately.

Likely oral follow-up

The retrieval questions below are the ones this answer is built to survive. They are the same questions an examiner reaches for when testing whether a written answer was understood or reproduced.

  • Give the route for the marine fuel standard, analysis and BDN question. — Printed 12 + 4. The corrective action is about two thirds of limb (a), so group the parameters by consequence rather than working down the report line by line.
  • ISO 8217 or MARPOL - which one does what? — The single most useful distinction in the question, and the one candidates most often blur.
  • What must a Bunker Delivery Note contain? — Half of limb (b). It is a printed, checkable list, and it is the sort of thing an examiner marks by ticking.
  • What are the BDN retention, inspection and sampling rules? — The three-year retention and the sealed sample are the two most quotable facts in limb (b).
  • The analysis shows high catalytic fines. What do you actually do? — The best single worked example of 'corrective action', because it shows treatment, operation, evidence and commercial response together.
  • What is the difference between stability and compatibility, and why does it matter more than any single parameter? — Sludging from an incompatible blend can immobilise a ship faster than any single off-spec parameter, and the answer to it is stowage planning, not treatment.
  • State the position of the 2024 Annex VI amendments at this sitting. — THE decisive temporal fact on this paper. Naming the resolution and stating that it is adopted and not yet in force is a scoring proposition; describing its content as the requirement is an error.
  • What is the decision that sits above all the individual corrective actions? — Turns a list of parameters into a decision, which is what 'corrective action which can be initiated' is actually asking for.

Memory framework

The route, chunked: (a) is FOUR then FIVE then ONE: NAME (1), GRADES (2), FEATURES (3), THE LEGAL FLOOR (4) - then read the report (5) - then FIVE GROUPS: DENSITY-VISCOSITY-IGNITION (6), CAT FINES (7), WATER-SEDIMENT-STABILITY (8), SULPHUR-FLASHPOINT (9), ASH-METALS-MCR (10) - then THE BIG DECISION (11). (b) is WHAT (12), CONTENTS (13), SAMPLE (14).

Fifteen-second recall: ISO 8217 is the international marine fuel specification - RM residual grades, DM distillate grades - a COMMERCIAL specification incorporated into the bunker contract. Beneath it, MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.3 is MANDATORY: blends of hydrocarbons from petroleum refining, free from inorganic acid, and no added substance or chemical waste that jeopardizes the ship's safety, adversely affects machinery performance, is harmful to personnel or contributes to air pollution. Corrective action groups: density/viscosity/ignition - separator capability and preheating to injection viscosity; cat fines (Al+Si) - settle, heat, slow the purifier, sample AFTER the separator; water and sediment - drain, settle, purify, and beware instability and incompatibility, never blend untested; sulphur and flashpoint - statutory, and low flashpoint may mean outright rejection; vanadium and sodium - hot corrosion, so watch valve and turbine temperatures. Above all: use, restrict, segregate or reject - and notify in writing early. BDN: regulation 18.5, at least the appendix V ten items, kept readily available, retained THREE YEARS, port State may inspect and take certified copies, and a sealed representative sample signed by supplier and ship is retained until the fuel is substantially consumed.

The numbers and thresholds worth carrying exactly:

  • MARPOL Annex VI appendix V - the bunker delivery note contains at least TEN items of information
  • MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.6 - the BDN is retained on board for THREE YEARS after the fuel oil has been delivered on board
  • MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.8.1 - the representative sample is sealed and signed by the supplier's representative and the master or officer in charge, and retained under the ship's control until the fuel is substantially consumed
  • SOLAS regulation II-2/4 - the flashpoint requirement of 60 degrees Celsius for fuel oil used on board (identified; the regulation was not re-read for this question)
  • MEPC.385(81) - adopted 22 March 2024, ENTERED INTO FORCE 1 AUGUST 2025. ADOPTED AND NOT IN FORCE AT THIS SITTING
  • NO ISO 8217 GRADE LIMIT, PARAMETER VALUE OR EDITION YEAR IS ASSERTED - MIW holds no copy of the standard
  • NO THRESHOLD, TEMPERATURE OR PERCENTAGE IS ASSERTED IN THE CORRECTIVE-ACTION SECTIONS - all are fuel, plant and maker specific

Keywords that unlock the rest: ISO 8217, marine fuel specification, residual, distillate, RM grades, DM grades, MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18, regulation 18.3, fuel oil quality, bunker delivery note, BDN, appendix V, MARPOL sample, representative sample, three years, fuel oil analysis, density, viscosity, CCAI, ignition quality, catalytic fines, aluminium plus silicon, cat fines, water content, total sediment, stability, compatibility, sulphur content, flashpoint, vanadium, sodium, hot corrosion, micro carbon residue, ash, used lubricating oil, calcium zinc phosphorus, purifier, settling tank, debunker, fuel oil non-availability report, MEPC.385(81)

Regulation and source map

The sources themselves, with what was read and at what grade:

Uncertainty and evidence limits

What this answer deliberately does not assert, and why. These are declared limits rather than omissions, and a candidate revising from this answer should know where MIW stopped.

  • MIW HOLDS NO LICENSED COPY OF ISO 8217 IN ANY EDITION. This is the controlling limitation on limb (a) and it is stated in the answer itself. The standard is NAMED as the international specification for marine fuels and its structure is described; NO TABLE IS REPRODUCED, NO GRADE LIMIT IS QUOTED AND NO PARAMETER VALUE IS ASSERTED. This is the standing MIW position, recorded previously at QP2411-Q2 and QP2512, and it is followed here without weakening.
  • NO EDITION YEAR OF ISO 8217 IS ASSERTED. The stem asks for 'the present ISO standards', which is edition-sensitive on its face. MIW cannot verify from a primary source which numbered edition was current on the day of the August 2024 sitting, because it holds no copy of the standard in any edition. Under MIW's evidence-declaration standard an unverifiable particular is omitted rather than given false precision. THE ANSWER SAYS SO EXPRESSLY AND EXPLAINS WHY IT COSTS NOTHING - the marks in limb (a) are for the salient features and the corrective action, neither of which turns on the edition year. See the paper anchor at section 6.3.
  • NO TEST-METHOD STANDARD NUMBER IS ASSERTED beyond the one that MARPOL Annex VI appendix V itself cites in its own footnote. MIW holds no ISO test-method standard, so no method number is attributed to any parameter in the analysis report.
  • NO THRESHOLD, LIMIT VALUE, TEMPERATURE, PERCENTAGE, INTERVAL OR TOLERANCE IS ASSERTED ANYWHERE IN THE CORRECTIVE-ACTION SECTIONS. No catalytic fines figure at the inlet or the outlet of the purifier, no maximum water content, no sediment limit, no CCAI value, no preheating temperature, no viscosity at the injector, no settling time and no purifier throughput reduction is given. Every one of these is fuel, plant, engine and maker specific; MIW holds no engine-builder project guidance, no purifier maker's manual and no fuel-testing laboratory's guidance; and the mechanisms are what earn the marks.
  • NO FUEL TESTING LABORATORY, SERVICE PROVIDER OR COMMERCIAL SCHEME IS NAMED. The analysis report is described generically by the parameters it reports.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 14 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________(a) The standard, named and placed
  2. __________(a) How the specification is organised
  3. __________(a) The salient features of the specification
  4. __________(a) The mandatory floor beneath the specification - MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.3
  5. __________(a) How a fuel oil analysis report is read
  6. __________(a) Corrective action - density, viscosity and ignition quality
  7. __________(a) Corrective action - catalytic fines
  8. __________(a) Corrective action - water, sediment, stability and compatibility
  9. __________(a) Corrective action - sulphur and flashpoint
  10. __________(a) Corrective action - ash, metals and micro-carbon residue
  11. __________(a) The decision that sits above all the corrective actions
  12. __________(b) What it is, and where it comes from
  13. __________(b) Its contents, retention and inspection
  14. __________(b) The representative sample, and why the BDN matters
Flashcards 8

(a) 1 name ISO 8217 and place it. 2 the RM and DM grade families. 3 the salient features of the specification. 4 the mandatory floor at MARPOL Annex VI reg 18.3. 5 how the report is read. Then five corrective-action groups: 6 density, viscosity, ignition quality. 7 catalytic fines. 8 water, sediment, stability and compatibility. 9 sulphur and flashpoint. 10 ash, metals and micro-carbon residue. 11 the use / restrict / segregate / reject decision and the notifications. (b) 12 what the BDN is and its legal basis. 13 contents, retention and inspection. 14 the representative sample.

Printed 12 + 4. The corrective action is about two thirds of limb (a), so group the parameters by consequence rather than working down the report line by line.

ISO 8217 is a COMMERCIAL SPECIFICATION. It has no legal force of its own; it binds because the bunker contract incorporates it by reference, and a fuel that fails it gives rise to a CLAIM against the supplier. MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.3 is MANDATORY INTERNATIONAL LAW: the fuel shall be blends of hydrocarbons derived from petroleum refining (small amounts of performance additives permitted), shall be free from inorganic acid, and shall not include any added substance or chemical waste that jeopardizes the safety of ships or adversely affects the performance of the machinery, is harmful to personnel, or contributes overall to additional air pollution. A fuel can be off-specification without breaching MARPOL, and can breach MARPOL while meeting most of the specification.

The single most useful distinction in the question, and the one candidates most often blur.

At least the ten items in MARPOL Annex VI appendix V: name and IMO number of the receiving ship; port; date of commencement of delivery; name, address and telephone number of the marine fuel oil supplier; product name(s); quantity in metric tonnes; density at 15 degrees Celsius; sulphur content (% m/m); the flashpoint, specified in accordance with standards acceptable to the Organization, or a statement that the flashpoint has been measured at or above 70 degrees Celsius; and a declaration signed and certified by the fuel oil supplier's representative that the fuel supplied is in conformity with regulation 18.3 and that the sulphur content does not exceed the limit given by regulation 14.1, or that given by regulation 14.4, or the purchaser's specified limit value, the applicable box being marked with a cross.

Half of limb (b). It is a printed, checkable list, and it is the sort of thing an examiner marks by ticking.

RETENTION - the BDN shall be kept on board in such a place as to be readily available for inspection at all reasonable times, and shall be retained for a period of THREE YEARS after the fuel oil has been delivered on board. INSPECTION - the competent authority of a Party may inspect the BDNs on board any ship to which the Annex applies while she is in its port or offshore terminal, may make a copy of each note, and may require the master or person in charge to certify that each copy is a true copy; it may also verify the contents through consultation with the port where the note was issued, and the inspection shall be performed as expeditiously as possible without causing the ship to be unduly delayed. SAMPLE - the BDN shall be accompanied by a representative sample of the fuel oil delivered, taking into account the guidelines developed by the Organization, sealed and signed by the supplier's representative and the master or officer in charge of the bunkering operation on completion, and retained under the ship's control until the fuel oil is substantially consumed.

The three-year retention and the sealed sample are the two most quotable facts in limb (b).

Understand the threat first: catalytic fines are aluminium and silicon particles from the refinery's cracking process, they are extremely abrasive, and they are not consumed by combustion - so they score liners, rings, fuel pumps and injectors and the damage is cumulative and permanent. Actions: maximise the effectiveness of the settling and separation chain - ensure adequate settling time with proper draining, run the fuel at the separator maker's recommended treatment condition, and REDUCE THROUGHPUT so the residence time in the bowl increases, running separators in series where the installation allows; drain settling and service tanks regularly, since fines concentrate at the bottom; and above all TAKE A SAMPLE AFTER THE SEPARATOR, because what matters to the engine is what reaches it, not what was delivered. Consider restricting engine load and avoiding rapid load changes. Segregate the bunker if it can be isolated, notify owners, charterers and the supplier in writing, and preserve the MARPOL sample for the claim.

The best single worked example of 'corrective action', because it shows treatment, operation, evidence and commercial response together.

STABILITY is a fuel's ability to hold its asphaltenes in suspension on its own. COMPATIBILITY is whether two fuels, each stable alone, remain stable when mixed - and paraffinic and aromatic residual fuels frequently do not. When asphaltenes drop out you get sludge that blocks filters and purifiers, overwhelms the separation plant and can stop the engine. High total sediment in a report is the warning sign. The corrective action is procedural rather than mechanical: SEGREGATE new bunkers from existing ones, do not commingle in a tank without a compatibility test, run tanks down before changing over where the arrangement allows, and change over gradually while watching filter differential pressures.

Sludging from an incompatible blend can immobilise a ship faster than any single off-spec parameter, and the answer to it is stowage planning, not treatment.

Resolution MEPC.385(81) was ADOPTED on 22 March 2024 at MEPC 81 and ENTERS INTO FORCE ON 1 AUGUST 2025. At an August 2024 sitting it is ADOPTED AND NOT IN FORCE, with eleven months still to run. The operative text is therefore the PRE-AMENDMENT regulation 18: a SINGLE paragraph 5 requiring a bunker delivery note containing at least the appendix V information; the chapeau of 18.3 reading 'fuel oil FOR COMBUSTION PURPOSES delivered to and used on board ships'; and paragraph 4 disapplying paragraphs 5, 6, 7.1, 7.2, 8.1, 8.2, 9.2, 9.3 and 9.4 to gas fuels. The amendments split paragraph 5 into 5.1 and 5.2, create a separate BDN regime for low-flashpoint and gas fuels, redefine fuel oil as any fuel delivered to and intended for use on board, and insert a definition of gas fuel - none of which applies at this sitting.

THE decisive temporal fact on this paper. Naming the resolution and stating that it is adopted and not yet in force is a scoring proposition; describing its content as the requirement is an error.

Four options, and the report exists to choose between them. USE AS NORMAL where the deviation is trivial. USE WITH PRECAUTIONS - enhanced treatment, closer monitoring of filters, liner condition, exhaust temperatures and lubricating oil, and more frequent scavenge inspections. USE WITH RESTRICTIONS - segregated to certain tanks, avoided for manoeuvring or for critical passages, or with engine load limited. REJECT AND DEBUNKER, which is the right answer where the fuel is unsafe - notably a low flashpoint, contamination with used lubricating oil or chemical waste, or a breach of regulation 18.3. Alongside whichever is chosen: notify owners, charterers, the supplier and where appropriate P&I IN WRITING AND EARLY, since bunker claims are time-sensitive; preserve the MARPOL sample unopened; and if compliant fuel cannot be obtained, the fuel oil non-availability procedure in regulation 18.2 applies.

Turns a list of parameters into a decision, which is what 'corrective action which can be initiated' is actually asking for.

Major trap
WRITING THE POST-2025 REGULATION 18. MEPC.385(81) split paragraph 5 into 5.1 and 5.2, creating a separate BDN regime for low-flashpoint and gas fuels, redefined 'fuel oil' as any fuel delivered to and intended for use on board, inserted a definition of 'gas fuel', and rewrote paragraph 4. The third is quoting an ISO 8217 limit value or edition year, neither of which MIW can verify.

Also on the platform: QP2408 Q4 - carbon pricing, which is levied on measured fuel · QP2408 Q8 - turbocharger developments and exhaust-side condition

Repeated — reworded Returns reworded from November 2023 Q7.

Understand this first

A ship is aground and there is no time. Somebody with tugs and pumps and people who know what they are doing has to decide, now, whether to sail towards her - and the owner has to decide, now, whether to let them. Neither side can possibly agree a price at that moment: nobody knows how long it will take, what it will cost, whether it will work, or what the ship and her cargo will turn out to be worth when she is safe. So the law does something unusual. It says: go and do the work, and if you succeed you will be paid, and the amount will be worked out afterwards by someone applying settled criteria. If you fail, you get nothing. That single arrangement - reward for success, nothing for failure - is what makes a professional salvage industry possible, because it turns an act of rescue into a business that can own expensive equipment and keep it waiting. The rest follows from it. Because the reward is fixed afterwards, there has to be a list of things the person fixing it must weigh - how dangerous it was, how skilful, how quick, what was risked, and what was saved. Because a rescuer who is paid only for saving property has no reason to care about oil on a beach, the list was expanded to reward protecting the coastline too, and duties to look after the environment were placed on both sides - the salvor and the shipowner. And because none of this can be negotiated line by line on a lee shore, the industry uses a short standard form that the master can sign in minutes, which settles who decides the amount, under what law, and where - while leaving the amount itself to be worked out later on the settled criteria. So the two documents in this question are not rivals. One says what a rescue is worth; the other is how you get started before anyone has time to argue about it.

Knowledge map
Salvage Convention - Award Criteria, Duties and LOF 2000
  1. intro
    1. 1The Convention, and where the stem's facts have got to
      • International Convention on Salvage, 1989, in force 1996, replacing the 1910 Convention
      • The stem's facts - grounded, refloated, towed to a place of safety by a professional salvor
      • A useful result has been achieved, so the right to a reward has accrued
  2. (a)
    1. 2The scheme Article 13 sits inside
      • Article 12 - no payment without a useful result. No cure, no pay is retained
      • Article 13 - the reward, fixed with a view to encouraging salvage operations
      • The reward is what Article 13 quantifies; the entitlement comes from Article 12
    2. 3The Article 13 criteria
      • Salved value; skill and efforts in preventing or minimising environmental damage
      • Measure of success; nature and degree of danger; skill and efforts in salving the property and life
      • Time, expenses and losses; risk of liability; promptness; availability and use of vessels; state of readiness and efficiency of equipment and its value
    3. 4How the criteria are applied
      • They are NOT listed in order of importance - all are taken together
      • The reward shall not exceed the salved value of the vessel and other property
      • Payable by the interests in proportion to their respective salved values
    4. 5Who pays, and what can reduce or extinguish the award
      • Ship and cargo interests, each in proportion to its salved value
      • Article 18 - misconduct, fault or neglect by the salvor may deprive him of all or part
      • Article 19 - services against express and reasonable prohibition are not payable
  3. (b)
    1. 6The salvor's duties
      • Carry out the operations with due care
      • In performing that duty, exercise due care to prevent or minimise damage to the environment
      • Seek help from other salvors when circumstances reasonably require, and accept their intervention when reasonably requested by the owner or master
    2. 7The duties of the owner and the master
      • Co-operate fully with the salvor
      • In doing so, exercise due care to prevent or minimise damage to the environment
      • Accept redelivery when the vessel is brought to a place of safety and reasonably requested to do so
    3. 8The mutual character of the duties, the master's authority, and breach
      • The environmental duty is imposed on BOTH sides - this is the Convention's innovation
      • The master may conclude a salvage contract on behalf of the ship's owner
      • Breach bears on the award and on liability; misconduct engages Article 18
  4. (c)
    1. 9What LOF 2000 is, and what it is not
