Model written answer

1. The Convention, and where the stem's facts have got to

The governing instrument is the International Convention on Salvage, 1989, which entered into force in 1996 and replaced the Brussels Convention of 1910. On the stem's facts a professional salvor has refloated a grounded ship and towed her to a place of safety. A useful result has therefore been achieved, which under article 12 is the condition of any payment at all - no cure, no pay, retained by the 1989 Convention rather than abolished by it. The entitlement has accrued, and the three limbs ask how it is quantified, what duties the parties owed one another while it was being earned, and what happens when the parties have also signed a contract.

MIW holds no licensed text of the Convention and no copy of LOF 2000 or SCOPIC. Articles and forms below are identified and described by effect; nothing is quoted, and no clause number is cited.

(a) The criteria under Article 13 for determining the amount of the award (6 marks)

2. The scheme Article 13 sits inside

Two articles work together and it is worth separating them in one line. Article 12 creates the entitlement: salvage operations which have had a useful result give right to a reward, and no payment is due if they have had no useful result. Article 13 then fixes the amount - and it does so, in the Convention's own words of purpose, with a view to encouraging salvage operations.

That phrase governs everything else in the limb. The reward is not a reimbursement of the salvor's costs, and it is not a quantum meruit for hours worked. It is deliberately set at a level intended to make it worth someone's while to own tugs, pumps and trained crews and keep them idle against the day they are needed. A candidate who explains that has explained why criterion (10) - the salvor's state of readiness and the value of his equipment - is in the list at all.

3. The Article 13 criteria

The Convention lists ten. Give them all, and in this order, because the examiner has named the article:

  1. The salved value of the vessel and other property.
  2. The skill and efforts of the salvors in preventing or minimising damage to the environment.
  3. The measure of success obtained by the salvor.
  4. The nature and degree of the danger.
  5. The skill and efforts of the salvors in salving the vessel, other property and life.
  6. The time used and the expenses and losses incurred by the salvors.
  7. The risk of liability and other risks run by the salvors or their equipment.
  8. The promptness of the services rendered.
  9. The availability and use of vessels or other equipment intended for salvage operations.
  10. The state of readiness and efficiency of the salvor's equipment and the value thereof.

Note where the environmental criterion sits. It is second, immediately after salved value - not appended at the end. That placement is the Convention's most important single reform: under the 1910 regime a salvor who spent days and money keeping oil off a coastline but saved nothing of value earned nothing for it, and the 1989 Convention rewards environmental protection inside the ordinary reward, before ever reaching the separate special compensation provisions.

4. How the criteria are applied

  • They are not in order of importance. The Convention says so expressly. All ten are weighed together in the round, and the relative weight depends on the casualty - danger and skill will dominate one case, promptness and readiness another.
  • The reward is capped. It shall not exceed the salved value of the vessel and other property. The salvor can never recover more than he saved, however heroic the operation or however great his expenditure.
  • The cap is why more was needed. Where the property saved is of little value but the environmental exposure is enormous - a laden tanker in ballast tanks aground on a coast - the cap can leave a salvor with a reward far below his costs. That is the gap the Convention's separate special compensation provisions were written to fill. Those are not the subject of this stem and are not developed here; they are mentioned so that the shape of the scheme is visible.
  • The criteria are applied by whoever fixes the award - by agreement between the parties, or failing that by the arbitrator or tribunal to whom the question has been referred. This is the point at which limb (a) hands over to limb (c).

5. Who pays, and what can reduce or extinguish the award

Payment. The reward is payable by all of the vessel and other property interests in proportion to their respective salved values. Ship and cargo therefore contribute separately and proportionately - a point worth making, because it explains why a salvor takes security from cargo interests as well as from the owner before releasing the property, and why cargo underwriters are as interested in a salvage award as hull underwriters.

What reduces it. The scheme has teeth in both directions:

  • Article 18 - a salvor may be deprived of the whole or part of the payment due to the extent that the salvage operations have become necessary or more difficult because of his own fault or neglect, or if he has been guilty of fraud or other dishonest conduct.
  • Article 19 - services rendered notwithstanding the express and reasonable prohibition of the owner or master of the vessel give rise to no payment under the Convention. A salvor cannot force his services on an unwilling ship and then present a bill.
  • And within article 13 itself, a poor measure of success, a lack of promptness or unimpressive skill and efforts simply produce a smaller award. The criteria cut both ways.

(b) The duties of the salvor and of the shipowner and master (5 marks)

6. The salvor's duties

Article 8 is the duties article and it is expressed as a reciprocal scheme. The salvor owes to the owner of the vessel or other property in danger a duty:

  1. to carry out the salvage operations with due care;
  2. in performing that duty, to exercise due care to prevent or minimise damage to the environment;
  3. whenever circumstances reasonably require, to seek assistance from other salvors; and
  4. to accept the intervention of other salvors when reasonably requested to do so by the owner or master of the vessel - though the amount of his reward is not prejudiced should it be found that such a request was unreasonable.

Duties (3) and (4) are the ones candidates omit, and they are doing real work. A professional salvor who has a casualty under contract has an obvious commercial incentive to keep the job to himself; the Convention removes that incentive where the ship's interests require more resources than he has. The salvor may not hold a casualty hostage to his own capacity - and the closing qualification protects him from being displaced on an unreasonable demand.

7. The duties of the owner and the master

The same article imposes duties the other way. The owner and master of the vessel or the owner of other property in danger owe the salvor a duty:

  1. to co-operate fully with him during the course of the salvage operations;
  2. in doing so, to exercise due care to prevent or minimise damage to the environment; and
  3. when the vessel or other property has been brought to a place of safety, to accept redelivery when reasonably requested by the salvor to do so.

The third duty fits the stem's facts exactly. The examiner has told you the ship has been towed by the salvor to a place of safety - which is precisely the trigger for the owner's obligation to take her back when reasonably asked. It exists because the alternative is a salvor left holding a damaged ship he cannot dispose of and cannot leave, accruing cost, while the owner delays redelivery to improve his negotiating position on security.

8. The mutual character of the duties, the master's authority, and breach

  • The environmental duty is imposed on both sides, and that is the Convention's genuine innovation. The 1910 regime asked only whether property had been saved. The 1989 Convention makes the prevention of environmental damage a duty of the owner and master as well as of the salvor, rewards it in the article 13 criteria, and backs it with separate compensation provisions. An answer that gives the environmental duty only to the salvor has given half of it.
  • The master's authority to contract. Under article 6 the master has authority to conclude contracts for salvage operations on behalf of the owner of the vessel, and the master or the owner has authority to conclude such contracts on behalf of the owner of the property on board. This is the provision that makes limb (c) possible at all: it is why a form can be signed at three in the morning by the only person present with authority, without waiting for owners, charterers, cargo interests and underwriters to be assembled.
  • Breach. The Convention does not create a tariff of penalties for breach of article 8. Its consequences run through the rest of the scheme: a salvor whose want of due care made the operation necessary or more difficult may be deprived of all or part of his reward under article 18; an owner or master who fails to co-operate, or who refuses reasonable redelivery, exposes himself to liability for the loss that follows and weakens his position on the assessment; and failure by either side to exercise due care for the environment bears directly on the article 13 assessment and on the special compensation provisions.

(c) LOF 2000 - when the Convention applies, and when the LOF (5 marks)

9. What LOF 2000 is, and what it is not

Lloyd's Open Form is a standard-form salvage contract. It is commercial contract, not an international instrument, and the single most common error on this limb is to treat it as though it competed with the Convention on the same plane. Its defining characteristics are:

  • It is 'open' - the remuneration is deliberately not fixed at the time of agreement. That is the whole point of the form: at a casualty nobody can price the job, so the parties agree that it will be done and that the price will be settled afterwards.
  • It is no cure, no pay, mirroring the Convention's own condition of payment.
  • It is short and can be agreed in minutes, often verbally and confirmed later, and typically signed by the master under his article 6 authority - which is why work can begin while the ship is still in danger.
  • It fixes the machinery, not the amount - who will determine the sum, under what law, where, and how the property will be secured in the meantime.

SCOPIC is a separate contractual supplement which the parties may incorporate, providing an alternative, tariff-based method of remunerating a salvor in circumstances where the ordinary reward may be inadequate. It too is industry contract, outside the Convention. No clause of either the LOF or SCOPIC is quoted or cited here.

10. When the Convention applies

The Convention applies as the general law of salvage in any State that has given it effect, and it governs by default. Its own article 6 sets out the relationship with contracts:

  • The Convention applies to any salvage operation save to the extent that a contract otherwise provides, expressly or by implication. Parties are therefore free to vary its terms by agreement, and an LOF is exactly such an agreement.
  • But the freedom is not unlimited. The duties in article 8 - including the environmental duties on both sides - cannot be contracted out of.
  • And the Convention supervises the bargain. A contract or any of its terms may be annulled or modified where the contract has been entered into under undue influence or the influence of danger and its terms are inequitable, or where the payment under the contract is in an excessive degree too large or too small for the services actually rendered.

That supervisory power is the answer to the obvious objection to signing anything in an emergency. A master agreeing terms with a ship aground and making water is, by definition, not negotiating freely, and the Convention accordingly reserves the right to reopen a bargain struck in those conditions.

11. When the LOF applies

Where the parties have agreed an LOF, its terms govern the relationship between them to the extent that they provide for the matter in hand. In practice the form governs:

  • the fact and scope of the engagement - that the salvor will use best endeavours to salve the property and take it to an agreed or a place of safety;
  • the forum and the law - that the amount of any award, and any dispute, will be referred to arbitration in London under the terms of the form and the law it specifies, rather than to a court in whatever jurisdiction the casualty happens to lie;
  • security - the obligation on the property interests to provide security for the salvor's claim, and the salvor's corresponding obligation to release the property once it is given; and
  • the procedure - the appointment of the arbitrator, the appeal structure within the form, and the administration of the reference.

What the LOF does not do is create the entitlement or supply the criteria. It records that a reward will be payable if the operation succeeds and provides the machinery for fixing it; the substantive principles the arbitrator applies are the Convention's.

12. How a dispute is actually resolved under an LOF

  1. The ship is salved and delivered to a place of safety. Security is provided by the ship and cargo interests, on the form's terms, and the property is released - which is what allows the ship to trade and the cargo to move while the money is argued about.
  2. The salvor's claim is referred to arbitration in London in accordance with the form. This is the practical significance of the LOF and it is where most of the marks in limb (c) are: the form has removed the question of forum from the dispute entirely. There is no argument about which country's courts have jurisdiction, no race to arrest, and no forum-shopping - a real gain when a casualty may involve a ship of one flag, a salvor of another, cargo owned in a third and a coastline belonging to a fourth.
  3. The arbitrator fixes the amount by applying the Article 13 criteria - salved value, environmental efforts, measure of success, danger, skill, time and expense, risks run, promptness, availability and readiness - and observes the cap at salved value, apportioning the liability between ship and cargo in proportion to their salved values.
  4. An appeal lies within the structure the form itself provides, and the award is then enforceable against the security already held.

13. The relationship, stated as a rule

The ConventionLOF 2000
NatureInternational instrument, given effect in national lawCommercial contract between the parties
SuppliesThe substantive law - entitlement, criteria, duties, cap, apportionment, and the grounds for reopening a bargainThe machinery - engagement, forum, governing law, security, arbitration and appeal
AppliesBy default, to any salvage operation, save to the extent a contract otherwise providesBy agreement, and only between the parties who agreed it
Can be displacedOnly in part. The article 8 duties cannot be contracted out of, and an inequitable or grossly mispriced contract may be annulled or modifiedEntirely - the parties need not use it, and may agree different terms
If the two conflictThe Convention prevails where the contract would defeat its protective provisionsGoverns where it validly provides for the matter

The rule to write down is short: the contract governs procedure and terms; the Convention governs substance; where the contract is silent the Convention fills the gap; and where the contract would defeat the Convention's protective provisions, the Convention prevails.

14. Why the arrangement exists at all

Close by explaining why two documents are needed rather than one, because it makes the whole answer cohere.

A casualty demands a decision in minutes and a valuation that takes months. Nobody at the scene knows how long the work will take, what it will cost, whether it will succeed, or what the property will be worth when it is safe. So the two functions are split. The form lets the work start at once, on terms brief enough for a master to accept while his ship is still aground, and settles in advance the questions that would otherwise be fought over afterwards - who decides, under what law, where, and against what security. The Convention then supplies the principles on which the sum is fixed, guarantees the duties neither side may bargain away, protects the party who agreed terms under the influence of danger, and rewards the salvor for protecting a coastline whose owner is not a party to anything.

Between them they answer the question that makes professional salvage possible: why would anyone sail towards a ship that nobody has yet agreed to pay for? Because success is rewarded on principled criteria that recognise readiness and risk as well as effort; because failure genuinely earns nothing, which keeps the industry honest; and because the terms on which all of that will be decided were settled before anyone had time to argue.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The printed marks are 6 / 5 / 5 and the limbs are three different tasks. Limb (a) says list, so it must be a numbered list - the examiner has named Article 13, which means partial recall of the criteria will be visible as partial recall. Give all ten. Limb (b) is two-sided and the second side is where the marks hide. Limb (c) is the limb most candidates lose, and it is worth as much as limb (b).

Spend your time in proportion. A common failure is a beautiful limb (a) followed by two thin paragraphs - because the criteria are memorable and the duties and the contract are not. If you are short of time, a bare list of the ten criteria takes ninety seconds and then limb (c) deserves the rest.

In limb (c), the sentence that earns the most is the one that says what each document actually does: the contract fixes forum, law, security and machinery; the Convention supplies the criteria the arbitrator applies. Everything else in that limb is elaboration of that sentence.

The idea underneath salvage law

Salvage exists because ordinary contract law cannot do the job. Normally you agree a price and then do the work. At a casualty that is impossible in both directions: the salvor cannot price a job whose duration, difficulty and outcome are unknown, and the owner cannot negotiate freely with a ship aground and making water. If the law required a bargain, either nothing would happen or everything would be extortion.

So the law substitutes a different arrangement: do the work, and if it succeeds you will be paid an amount fixed afterwards on settled criteria. That single move solves both problems. The salvor does not need a price to start. The owner cannot be held to ransom, because the amount will be assessed rather than demanded. And because the assessment expressly aims to encourage salvage operations, it can be generous enough to sustain an industry that must keep expensive equipment idle most of the time - which is why readiness and the value of the salvor's equipment are among the criteria, and why 'you only worked for two days' is not an answer to a large award.

The 1989 Convention then adds the piece the 1910 regime lacked. Under the old law a salvor was rewarded for saving property, so a tanker that could not be saved but whose oil could be kept off a coastline earned him precisely nothing - which meant the law was paying salvors to make exactly the wrong choice about where to put their effort. The 1989 Convention rewards environmental protection inside the ordinary reward criteria, imposes environmental duties on both parties, and provides separately for the case where the property saved is too small to fund the effort. Understand that one reform and most of the Convention's shape follows.

The criteria - how to remember all ten

They fall into four groups, and grouped they are much easier to reproduce under pressure.

GroupCriteria
What was at stakeSalved value; nature and degree of the danger
What the salvor achievedSkill and efforts in preventing or minimising environmental damage; measure of success; skill and efforts in salving vessel, property and life
What it cost himTime used, expenses and losses incurred; risk of liability and other risks run
How well he was placed to do itPromptness; availability and use of vessels and equipment; state of readiness, efficiency and value of the equipment

The last group is the one people forget and it is the most interesting. It rewards the salvor for things that happened before the casualty - for having bought the tugs, maintained them and had them where they were needed. That is the law deliberately paying for standing capacity, and it is the clearest evidence that the award is not a fee for hours worked.

Article 8 - the duties, and the half everyone misses

Almost every candidate can say the salvor must exercise due care. Rather fewer say he must seek assistance from other salvors when circumstances reasonably require and accept their intervention when reasonably requested by the owner or master. Fewer still give the duties on the other side at all.

Learn the reciprocal shape:

The salvor mustThe owner and master must
Carry out the operations with due careCo-operate fully with him during the operations
Exercise due care to prevent or minimise damage to the environmentExercise due care to prevent or minimise damage to the environment
Seek assistance from other salvors when circumstances reasonably require-
Accept the intervention of other salvors when reasonably requested by the owner or masterAccept redelivery when the property is brought to a place of safety and he is reasonably requested to do so

Note the symmetry of the middle row - identical wording, both sides - and note that the two bottom-row duties are each about not obstructing the other party's legitimate interest. That is the design: neither side may use the emergency as leverage.

LOF and the Convention - the mistake to avoid

The commonest error in limb (c) is to write as though the question were 'which of these two applies?', as if they were rival sources of law competing for the same territory. They are not. One is a treaty and one is a contract, and they operate on different questions.

Think of it as a division of labour. The Convention answers: is a reward payable at all? on what criteria? capped at what? paid by whom? what duties did each party owe? and can this bargain be reopened? The LOF answers: are we agreed that you will start work? who will decide the amount? under what law? where? and how is the property secured in the meantime?

Once you see that, the exceptions make sense too. The Convention lets the parties vary its terms by contract - because most of it is default machinery. But it will not let them contract out of the article 8 duties, and it reserves power to annul or modify a contract made under the influence of danger on inequitable terms, or one where the payment is in an excessive degree too large or too small. Those are the protective provisions, and they are the answer to 'what if the master signs something disastrous?'

Why arbitration in London matters more than it sounds

It is easy to read 'arbitration in London under English law' as a piece of parochial detail. It is not. Consider the alternative on a real casualty: a ship of one flag, salvors of another nationality, cargo owned in several countries, insurers in more, and a grounding on the coast of a State with its own interest in the outcome. Without an agreed forum, the first dispute is about where the dispute will be heard, and the salvor's practical remedy is to arrest the property wherever he can reach it.

The form removes all of that in advance, before anyone knows whether there will be an argument. The greatest practical value of the LOF is not what it says about money; it is that it settles the questions that would otherwise be fought over first. That, plus the security provisions that let the ship trade and the cargo move while the amount is assessed, is what makes the form worth signing.

Examiner traps

  • Answering on Article 14 special compensation. This stem does not ask for it. Mention it in one clause to explain why the salved-value cap needed a companion provision, and move on - a full treatment is the answer to a different question.
  • Treating LOF 2000 as an instrument. It is a contract. Say so.
  • Quoting an LOF or SCOPIC clause number. Both forms exist in several editions and clause numbering differs. Describe by function.
  • Giving only the salvor's duties. Article 8 is reciprocal, and the owner's duty to accept redelivery fits the stem's facts exactly.
  • Saying the criteria are in order of importance. The Convention says they are not.
  • Forgetting the cap and the apportionment. The reward cannot exceed the salved value and is paid by ship and cargo in proportion to their salved values - two easy marks.
  • Saying no cure no pay was abolished in 1989. It was retained. What was added was a way of paying for environmental effort when the property saved could not fund it.
  • Reaching for the Merchant Shipping Act 2025. It commenced 15 March 2026, nineteen months after this sitting - and the stem asks about the Convention in any event.

If you only have ten minutes for this question

(a), six lines. Under the Salvage Convention 1989, article 12 gives the right to a reward where the operation has had a useful result - no cure, no pay - and article 13 fixes the amount, with a view to encouraging salvage operations. The criteria are: salved value of the vessel and other property; skill and efforts in preventing or minimising damage to the environment; measure of success; nature and degree of the danger; skill and efforts in salving the vessel, other property and life; time used and expenses and losses incurred; risk of liability and other risks run; promptness of the services; availability and use of vessels or other equipment intended for salvage; and the state of readiness, efficiency and value of the salvor's equipment. They are not in order of importance; the reward cannot exceed the salved value; and it is paid by ship and cargo in proportion to their salved values. Article 18 may deprive a salvor of all or part for his own fault or dishonesty, and article 19 denies payment for services rendered against express and reasonable prohibition.

(b), four lines. Article 8 imposes duties on both sides. The salvor must carry out the operations with due care; in doing so exercise due care to prevent or minimise damage to the environment; seek assistance from other salvors whenever circumstances reasonably require; and accept their intervention when reasonably requested by the owner or master, without prejudice to his reward if the request proves unreasonable. The owner and master must co-operate fully with the salvor; in doing so exercise due care to prevent or minimise damage to the environment; and, when the property has been brought to a place of safety, accept redelivery when reasonably requested. Note that under article 6 the master has authority to conclude a salvage contract on behalf of the owner.

(c), five lines. LOF 2000 is Lloyd's Open Form - a standard-form salvage contract, not an instrument: no cure no pay, remuneration deliberately left open, signable in minutes by the master. The Convention applies as the general law and governs by default; article 6 provides that it applies to any salvage operation save to the extent a contract otherwise provides, but the article 8 duties cannot be contracted out of and a contract made under the influence of danger on inequitable terms, or grossly mispriced, may be annulled or modified. The LOF applies between the parties who agreed it and fixes the machinery: the engagement, security for the claim and release of the property, and reference of the amount and of any dispute to arbitration in London under the form's terms and law, with its own appeal structure. In a dispute, therefore, the LOF determines where, under what law and by whom the question is decided, while the arbitrator applies the Convention's Article 13 criteria to fix the sum. The rule: the contract governs procedure and terms, the Convention governs substance, and where the contract would defeat the Convention's protective provisions the Convention prevails.

Common mistakes

These are the places this particular question goes wrong, taken from the ambiguities identified before drafting and from the single error most likely to cost marks.

The one that costs most: Treating LOF 2000 as though it were a legal instrument that competes with the Convention. It is a standard-form CONTRACT. It fixes the forum, the governing law, the security arrangements and the machinery for fixing the sum; the CONVENTION supplies the substantive principles the arbitrator applies. The second trap is forgetting that Article 8 imposes duties on the SHIPOWNER AND MASTER as well as on the salvor - including the duty to ACCEPT REDELIVERY - which is half of limb (b). The third is answering on Article 14 special compensation, which this stem does not ask for.

The wording of the stem invites four specific misreadings, and each is resolved in the answer rather than left to chance:

  • Limb (c) as printed - 'discuss when the Convention is applicable and when the LOF if any dispute arises' - is grammatically incomplete and could be read two ways: when does each of the two GOVERN, or which forum resolves the dispute. The answer takes it as BOTH, because the two readings are complementary and each carries marks: it explains the substantive division between Convention and contract, and then explains the arbitration machinery. Reproduced as printed and answered on both readings.
  • The stem says the salvor 'has a right to award'. Strictly the right arises under article 12 from the useful result, and article 13 quantifies it. The answer says so in one line without labouring it, because the stem has plainly given success as a fact.
  • 'Duties of the salvor and the shipowner/master' - the printed pairing invites treating owner and master as one. Their duties largely coincide under article 8, but the MASTER additionally has authority to conclude a salvage contract on behalf of the owner. The answer notes that separately because it is the practical link to limb (c).
  • Article 14 special compensation is NOT asked. It is referred to only so far as is needed to explain why the article 13 cap makes some such provision necessary, and is not developed. Answering the donor's question instead of this one would be the most likely way to waste time here.

Likely oral follow-up

The retrieval questions below are the ones this answer is built to survive. They are the same questions an examiner reaches for when testing whether a written answer was understood or reproduced.

  • Give the route for the salvage award, duties and LOF question. — Printed 6/5/5. Limb (a) says LIST, so number the criteria; limb (c) is the limb candidates lose and it needs five short sections, not one paragraph.
  • List the Article 13 criteria for fixing the salvage reward. — Six marks and the examiner named the article, so a selection reads as partial recall. Give all ten in the Convention's own order - and note that the ENVIRONMENTAL criterion sits second, not last.
  • How are the Article 13 criteria applied? — Two of the six marks in limb (a) are here rather than in the list itself, and the 'encouraging salvage operations' point explains the whole design.
  • What are the salvor's duties under the Convention? — Half of limb (b). The duty to accept another salvor's intervention is the one candidates never give, and it is the provision that stops a salvor holding a casualty hostage.
  • What are the duties of the owner and master of the vessel in danger? — The other half of limb (b), and the half most often omitted. Note how directly it fits the stem's facts: the ship HAS been towed to a place of safety, so the redelivery duty is live.
  • LOF 2000 or the Convention - which governs what? — The whole of limb (c) in one card, and the point that separates a good answer from a vague one.
  • When does the Convention apply, and can the parties contract out of it? — 'When is the Convention applicable' is answered here, and the protective provisions are what stop a distressed master signing away everything.
  • What can reduce or extinguish a salvor's award? — Rounds out limb (a) and shows the scheme has teeth on both sides - the salvor is not merely a beneficiary of it.

Fifteen-second recall: Salvage Convention 1989, in force 1996, replaced the 1910 Convention; retains NO CURE NO PAY (article 12 - a useful result). Article 13 criteria, unranked: salved value; skill and efforts in preventing or minimising damage to the environment; measure of success; nature and degree of danger; skill and efforts in salving the vessel, other property and life; time used and expenses and losses incurred; risk of liability and other risks run; promptness; availability and use of vessels or other equipment; and the state of readiness, efficiency and value of the salvor's equipment. Reward CAPPED at salved value; paid by ship and cargo in PROPORTION to their salved values. Article 8 duties, BOTH SIDES: the salvor must use due care, prevent or minimise environmental damage, seek other salvors when reasonably required and accept their intervention when reasonably requested; the owner and master must co-operate fully, exercise due care for the environment, and ACCEPT REDELIVERY when brought to a place of safety and reasonably requested. LOF 2000 is a CONTRACT, not an instrument: no cure no pay, signed in minutes, arbitration in London under English law, security given before release; the Convention supplies the substantive criteria the arbitrator applies.

The numbers and thresholds worth carrying exactly:

  • The reward shall NOT EXCEED the salved value of the vessel and other property

Flashcards 8

Scene: 1 the Convention and the facts. (a) 2 the scheme - no cure no pay under article 12, reward under article 13. 3 the ten criteria. 4 how they are applied - unranked and capped. 5 who pays and what reduces the award. (b) 6 the salvor's duties. 7 the owner's and master's duties. 8 the mutual environmental duty and breach. (c) 9 what LOF 2000 is. 10 when the Convention applies. 11 when the LOF applies. 12 how a dispute is resolved. 13 the relationship stated as a rule. 14 why the arrangement exists.

Printed 6/5/5. Limb (a) says LIST, so number the criteria; limb (c) is the limb candidates lose and it needs five short sections, not one paragraph.

(1) The salved value of the vessel and other property. (2) The skill and efforts of the salvors in preventing or minimising damage to the environment. (3) The measure of success obtained by the salvor. (4) The nature and degree of the danger. (5) The skill and efforts of the salvors in salving the vessel, other property and life. (6) The time used and the expenses and losses incurred by the salvors. (7) The risk of liability and other risks run by the salvors or their equipment. (8) The promptness of the services rendered. (9) The availability and use of vessels or other equipment intended for salvage operations. (10) The state of readiness and efficiency of the salvor's equipment and the value thereof.

Six marks and the examiner named the article, so a selection reads as partial recall. Give all ten in the Convention's own order - and note that the ENVIRONMENTAL criterion sits second, not last.

They are NOT set out in order of importance and are taken together, all of them weighed in the round by whoever fixes the award. The reward is FIXED WITH A VIEW TO ENCOURAGING SALVAGE OPERATIONS - so it is not merely a reimbursement of cost, and a salvor who does an outstanding job on a difficult casualty is meant to do well out of it. The reward SHALL NOT EXCEED the salved value of the vessel and other property, and it is payable by the vessel and other property interests IN PROPORTION to their respective salved values.

Two of the six marks in limb (a) are here rather than in the list itself, and the 'encouraging salvage operations' point explains the whole design.

Article 8. The salvor owes a duty to the owner of the vessel or other property in danger: to carry out the salvage operations WITH DUE CARE; in performing that duty, to exercise DUE CARE TO PREVENT OR MINIMISE DAMAGE TO THE ENVIRONMENT; whenever circumstances reasonably require, to SEEK ASSISTANCE FROM OTHER SALVORS; and to ACCEPT THE INTERVENTION OF OTHER SALVORS when reasonably requested to do so by the owner or master of the vessel - though the amount of his reward is not prejudiced should it be found that such a request was unreasonable.

Half of limb (b). The duty to accept another salvor's intervention is the one candidates never give, and it is the provision that stops a salvor holding a casualty hostage.

Article 8 again, and this is the Convention's innovation. The owner and master owe a duty to the salvor: to CO-OPERATE FULLY with him during the course of the operations; in doing so, to exercise DUE CARE TO PREVENT OR MINIMISE DAMAGE TO THE ENVIRONMENT; and, when the vessel or other property has been brought to a PLACE OF SAFETY, to ACCEPT REDELIVERY when reasonably requested by the salvor to do so.

The other half of limb (b), and the half most often omitted. Note how directly it fits the stem's facts: the ship HAS been towed to a place of safety, so the redelivery duty is live.

THE CONTRACT GOVERNS PROCEDURE AND TERMS; THE CONVENTION GOVERNS THE SUBSTANCE. LOF 2000 is a standard-form salvage CONTRACT - it can be agreed in minutes, often by the master under his article 6 authority, on a no cure no pay basis with the remuneration left to be fixed later. What it fixes is the machinery: that the amount will be determined by ARBITRATION in London under the terms of the form and the law it specifies; how SECURITY is to be provided before the property is released; and the appeal structure. What it does NOT do is create the entitlement or supply the criteria - those come from the Convention, and the arbitrator applies the Article 13 criteria.

The whole of limb (c) in one card, and the point that separates a good answer from a vague one.

The Convention applies as the general law of salvage wherever a State has given it effect, and it governs by default. Article 6 provides that the Convention applies to any salvage operation SAVE TO THE EXTENT THAT A CONTRACT OTHERWISE PROVIDES, EXPRESSLY OR BY IMPLICATION - so parties may vary its terms by agreement, and an LOF is exactly such an agreement. BUT the freedom is not unlimited: the duties in article 8 - including the environmental duties - cannot be contracted out of, and a contract or its terms may be annulled or modified where it was entered into under undue influence or the influence of danger and its terms are inequitable, or where the payment is in an excessive degree too large or too small for the services actually rendered.

'When is the Convention applicable' is answered here, and the protective provisions are what stop a distressed master signing away everything.

Article 18 - a salvor may be DEPRIVED OF THE WHOLE OR PART of the payment due where the salvage operations have become necessary or more difficult BECAUSE OF HIS OWN FAULT OR NEGLECT, or where he has been guilty of fraud or other dishonest conduct. Article 19 - services rendered NOTWITHSTANDING THE EXPRESS AND REASONABLE PROHIBITION of the owner or master of the vessel give rise to no payment under the Convention. And under article 13 itself, a poor measure of success or a lack of promptness simply produces a smaller award.

Rounds out limb (a) and shows the scheme has teeth on both sides - the salvor is not merely a beneficiary of it.

Repeated — reworded Returns reworded from February 2023 Q4. Also set at July 2023 Q1, November 2024 Q5.

Understand this first

Only about a third of the energy in the fuel ends up turning the propeller. Of the rest, the single biggest share goes out of the funnel as hot, fast-moving gas, and for most of the last century the only use anyone made of it was to drive a blower that pushed more air into the engine - which was worth doing, because it let a given cylinder burn more fuel and make more power. But turbochargers have become steadily better at their job, and that produced an unexpected situation: at high load, the turbine can extract more energy than the compressor actually needs. There is a surplus, and it is being thrown away. So the question became what to do with it, and the answer in each of the three devices here is the same in principle - put an electrical machine somewhere in that energy path - with the difference lying in where you put it. You can bleed off some of the gas before it reaches the turbocharger, run it through a second turbine of its own, and use that to drive a generator; now the ship is making electricity out of waste, and an auxiliary engine can be shut down. Or you can put the electrical machine directly onto the turbocharger's own shaft, so that the same machine which is already spinning very fast can be made to generate. And once there is an electrical machine on that shaft, something else becomes possible, and it is the mirror image of everything above: at low load - a ship steaming slowly to save fuel - there is not enough exhaust energy, the turbocharger runs too slowly, the engine is short of air, combustion is poor and it smokes. The same machine can then be run backwards, as a motor, driving the compressor faster than the exhaust alone would. So the family divides neatly in two: at high load you are taking energy out because there is too much, and at low load you are putting energy in because there is too little. Hold that division and the three devices arrange themselves.

Knowledge map
Recent Developments in Marine Turbocharger Application
  1. intro
    1. 1Why the turbocharger is where the gains now are
      • The largest single loss from a diesel engine leaves through the exhaust
      • Modern high-efficiency turbochargers need less of that energy than is available
      • The surplus is what all three devices exploit
    2. 2The common thread, and the division that organises the answer
      • All three put an electrical machine in the exhaust energy path
      • Two of them harvest surplus energy; one of them adds energy where there is a deficit
      • Recognising that division is the key to the whole answer
  2. (a)
    1. 3What it is
      • A separate exhaust gas turbine fed by gas bypassed around the turbocharger
      • It drives a generator, usually through reduction gearing
      • It is an additional machine, not a modification of the turbocharger
    2. 4How it is arranged and controlled
      • Bypass valve, power turbine, gearbox, generator, and the electrical interface
      • Engaged only above the load at which surplus energy exists
      • May be combined with an exhaust gas boiler and steam turbine in a combined plant
    3. 5What it buys, and what it costs
      • Electrical power produced with no additional fuel, so auxiliary engines can be stopped
      • Improves the ship's overall energy efficiency and her design and operational indices
      • Cost, weight, space, complexity, and no benefit at low load
  3. (b)
    1. 6What it is
      • A turbocharger with an electrical machine integrated on its own shaft
      • No separate turbine and no gas bypass to a second machine
      • One machine doing two jobs
    2. 7How it works in both directions
      • As a generator, absorbing surplus turbine power at high load
      • As a motor, driving the compressor when there is not enough exhaust energy
      • Control decides which, according to load and transient demand
    3. 8What it buys, and what it costs
      • Electrical power without fuel, plus low-load and transient support in one installation
      • Compact, with no gearbox and no separate turbine casing
      • The electrical machine runs at turbocharger speed in a hot, dirty environment
  4. (c)
    1. 9What it is
      • A turbocharger with an electric motor assisting the compressor
      • Primarily a motoring device rather than a generating one
      • Draws power from the ship rather than producing it
    2. 10The problem it solves
      • At low load and during acceleration there is not enough exhaust energy
      • The result is low scavenge pressure, poor combustion, smoke and thermal loading
      • Auxiliary blowers do part of this job; the electric motor does it better
    3. 11What it buys, and what it costs
      • Cleaner low-load running, faster response, and better slow steaming behaviour
      • Supports the derating and low-load operation that speed reduction demands
      • It consumes electrical power; the benefit is combustion quality, not direct generation
  5. all
    1. 12Choosing between them
      • Harvest surplus energy - power turbine and hybrid in generating mode
      • Supply a deficit - electro-assist and hybrid in motoring mode
      • Choose by the ship's operating profile, not by the technology
    2. 13What these developments mean for the operating engineer
      • Exhaust-side cleanliness matters more, not less, because energy is being harvested
      • New failure modes: bypass valves, gearing, high-speed electrical machines, power electronics
      • Control interaction with the electrical plant and with the engine's certified configuration
    3. 14Where this fits in the wider picture
      • Efficiency measured per unit of transport work, and now priced
      • These recover part of the largest loss without touching the combustion process
      • The gains are real but incremental, and they are load-profile dependent

Cover42 core points beneath them

Memorise the 14 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 42 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Why the turbocharger is where the gains now are
  • The largest single loss from a diesel engine leaves through the exhaust
  • Modern high-efficiency turbochargers need less of that energy than is available
  • The surplus is what all three devices exploit
