Model written answer

(a) The role of the SMS in implementing the ISM Code (8 marks)

1. What the Code is, and how it binds

The International Management Code for the Safe Operation of Ships and for Pollution Prevention - the ISM Code - was adopted by the Assembly by resolution A.741(18) and is made mandatory through SOLAS chapter IX, Management for the Safe Operation of Ships. Its text has been amended several times, most recently in the corpus chain by resolution MSC.353(92), adopted 21 June 2013, whose amendments have been in force since 1 January 2015. That is the operative text.

What distinguishes it from everything else in SOLAS is its subject. The rest of the Convention regulates the ship - her structure, her subdivision, her machinery, her fire protection, her life-saving appliances. The ISM Code regulates the way the ship is managed, ashore and afloat. Its objectives, stated in element 1.2, are to ensure safety at sea, prevention of human injury or loss of life, and avoidance of damage to the environment, in particular to the marine environment, and to property.

2. What a Safety Management System is, and why the Code works through one

The Code defines a Safety Management System as a structured and documented system enabling Company personnel to implement effectively the Company safety and environmental protection policy.

The reason the Code works through such a system, rather than by prescribing procedures directly, is worth stating because it is the conceptual core of limb (a). Safe operation cannot be specified the way scantlings or lifeboat capacity can. The right procedure for changing over fuel, for entering an enclosed space or for handling a main engine defect depends on the ship, the trade, the machinery, the manning and the company. So the Code sets objectives, requires that a system be developed, implemented and maintained to deliver them, leaves the company to write procedures suited to its own operation - and then holds the company to what it has written, and audits whether it is being done.

That is why the Code's language is 'the Company should establish procedures' rather than 'the procedure shall be as follows'. The SMS is not a by-product of the Code; it is the instrument through which the Code operates.

3. The six functional requirements - what every SMS must contain

Element 1.4 requires every company to develop, implement and maintain an SMS which includes the following functional requirements:

  1. a safety and environmental protection policy;
  2. instructions and procedures to ensure safe operation of ships and protection of the environment in compliance with relevant international and flag State legislation;
  3. defined levels of authority and lines of communication between, and amongst, shore and shipboard personnel;
  4. procedures for reporting accidents and non-conformities with the provisions of the Code;
  5. procedures to prepare for and respond to emergency situations; and
  6. procedures for internal audits and management reviews.

Requirement (3) deserves a clause of its own. Defined levels of authority and lines of communication is the requirement that answers the failure the Code was written to address, and it is the one that is most often absent when something goes badly wrong. Element 1.2.2 adds the safety management objectives of the Company: to provide for safe practices in ship operation and a safe working environment; to assess all identified risks to its ships, personnel and the environment and establish appropriate safeguards; and to continuously improve safety management skills of personnel ashore and aboard, including preparing for emergencies.

4. The components - Part A, elements 1 to 12

The functional requirements are what an SMS must contain; the elements of Part A are how the Code specifies each of them.

ElementWhat it requiresWhich functional requirement it delivers
1 GeneralDefinitions, objectives, application and the functional requirements themselvesAll
2 Safety and Environmental Protection PolicyA policy describing how the objectives will be achieved, implemented and maintained at all levels, ship-based and shore-based(1)
3 Company Responsibilities and AuthorityWhere the operator is not the owner, the owner must report the operator's full details to the Administration. Responsibility, authority and interrelation of all personnel managing, performing and verifying safety and pollution-prevention work must be defined and documented, and adequate resources and shore-based support provided(3)
4 Designated Person(s)A person or persons ashore having direct access to the highest level of management, whose responsibility includes monitoring the safety and pollution-prevention aspects of the operation of each ship and ensuring adequate resources and shore support are applied(3)
5 Master's Responsibility and AuthorityThe master's responsibilities documented; and the SMS must contain a clear statement emphasising that the master has overriding authority and the responsibility to make decisions with respect to safety and pollution prevention and to request the Company's assistance(3)
6 Resources and PersonnelA properly qualified master conversant with the SMS; ships manned with qualified, certificated and medically fit seafarers; familiarisation of new and transferred personnel, with essential pre-sailing instructions identified, documented and given; understanding of relevant rules; identification of training needs; and information in a working language understood by the crew(2), (3)
7 Shipboard OperationsProcedures, plans and instructions, including checklists, for key shipboard operations, with tasks defined and assigned to qualified personnel(2)
8 Emergency PreparednessIdentification of potential emergency situations and procedures to respond; programmes for drills and exercises; and measures ensuring the Company's organisation can respond at any time(5)
9 Reports and Analysis of Non-conformities, Accidents and Hazardous OccurrencesProcedures ensuring these are reported to the Company, investigated and analysed with the object of improving safety and pollution prevention, and procedures for corrective action including measures to prevent recurrence(4)
10 Maintenance of the Ship and EquipmentMaintenance in conformity with rules, regulations and the Company's own additional requirements; inspections at appropriate intervals; non-conformities reported with probable cause; corrective action and records; and identification of equipment and technical systems whose sudden operational failure may result in hazardous situations, with specific measures including regular testing of stand-by arrangements and systems not in continuous use(2)
11 DocumentationControl of all documents and data relevant to the SMS; valid documents at all relevant locations; changes reviewed and approved; obsolete documents promptly removed; the documentation may be called the Safety Management Manual(1), (2)
12 Company Verification, Review and EvaluationInternal safety audits on board and ashore at intervals not exceeding twelve months - exceptionally exceeded by not more than three; periodic verification of those undertaking delegated ISM tasks; periodic evaluation of the effectiveness of the SMS; auditors independent of the areas audited where practicable; results brought to the attention of all personnel responsible; and timely corrective action by the management personnel responsible(6)

5. The certification layer - Part B

Part A describes the system; Part B is how compliance is demonstrated and policed.

  • Document of Compliance (DOC) - issued to the Company by the Administration, by a recognized organization, or at the Administration's request by another Contracting Government, for a period not exceeding five years. It is accepted as evidence that the Company is capable of complying with the Code, and it is valid only for the ship types explicitly indicated in it. Its validity is subject to annual verification within three months before or after the anniversary date, and it is withdrawn where the annual verification is not requested or where there is evidence of major non-conformities.
  • Safety Management Certificate (SMC) - issued to the ship, signifying that the Company and its shipboard management operate in accordance with the approved safety management system. A ship may not hold an SMC unless her Company holds a DOC relevant to that ship type.
  • Interim certification is provided for new companies, new ship types and newly delivered or transferred ships.

The Code's own definitions support this layer and are worth knowing precisely: objective evidence is quantitative or qualitative information, records or statements of fact based on observation, measurement or test which can be verified; an observation is a statement of fact made during a safety management audit and substantiated by objective evidence; a non-conformity is an observed situation where objective evidence indicates the non-fulfilment of a specified requirement; and a major non-conformity is an identifiable deviation that poses a serious threat to the safety of personnel or the ship or a serious risk to the environment requiring immediate corrective action, or the lack of effective and systematic implementation of a requirement of the Code.

That last clause is the one to notice: a system that exists on paper but is not systematically implemented is itself a major non-conformity. The Code anticipated the failure mode of its own compliance.

6. The role the SMS plays in implementing the Code

Pulling the components together, the system performs four distinct functions and it is worth separating them.

  1. It converts objectives into performable acts. 'Ensure safety at sea' cannot be done by anybody; 'carry out this checklist before this operation, and you are the person who does it' can. Elements 7 and 10 are where the Code's objectives become someone's job.
  2. It establishes the shore-to-ship link. Element 3 requires responsibility and authority to be defined and documented; element 4 puts a named person ashore with direct access to the highest level of management; element 5 states the master's overriding authority in the ship's own manual. Between them these create a route by which a concern travels upward and cannot be filtered out by the person it embarrasses - which is precisely what was missing in the casualties that produced the Code.
  3. It generates the evidence on which compliance is judged. Audit under the Code is verification against objective evidence, and the SMS is what produces that evidence - records, reports, audit findings, maintenance data, drill records and reviews. Without the system there is nothing to audit.
  4. It corrects itself. That is section 7, and it is the function that distinguishes an SMS from a filing cabinet.

7. Continuous improvement - the mechanism

There is no clause of the Code headed 'continuous improvement'. It is an outcome produced by three elements working together, and naming them is what earns the marks.

ElementWhat it contributes to the loop
9 Reports and analysisDetection. Non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences are reported to the Company, investigated and analysed with the object of improving safety and pollution prevention - and corrective action must include measures intended to prevent recurrence, which is the difference between fixing a fault and fixing a cause
10 MaintenanceFeeding the loop from the plant. Non-conformity reported with its probable cause, corrective action taken, records maintained, and critical equipment identified with stand-by arrangements regularly tested
12 Company verification, review and evaluationChecking and closing. Internal audits on board and ashore at intervals not exceeding twelve months; verification that those performing delegated ISM tasks conform; periodic evaluation of the effectiveness of the SMS; auditor independence where practicable; results communicated to all responsible personnel; and timely corrective action by the management responsible

Element 1.2.2 completes the picture by making it an objective of the Company's safety management to continuously improve safety management skills of personnel ashore and aboard - so improvement is directed at the people as well as at the procedures.

8. Continuous improvement - why it is the significant part

The printed stem asks about the significance of continuous improvement, and there are four answers.

  1. Without it the system decays into a description of a ship nobody operates. Procedures written for one trade, one manning arrangement and one machinery configuration become progressively less true as all three change. A system that cannot revise itself becomes something the crew works around - and a worked-around procedure is more dangerous than none, because it is still on the shelf being audited against.
  2. It is what makes a goal-based instrument enforceable. Because the Code does not prescribe the procedures, the only meaningful question an auditor can ask is whether the company's own system is working and whether the company knows when it is not. The audit and review machinery is therefore not administrative overhead - it is the mechanism by which a non-prescriptive Code has any teeth at all.
  3. It converts incidents into changed behaviour. Element 9's requirement that corrective action include measures intended to prevent recurrence is the whole point: an investigation that identifies a valve left shut and closes the report has learned nothing; one that asks why the procedure permitted it, and changes the procedure, has.
  4. It is what the Code treats as fundamental. The lack of effective and systematic implementation of a requirement of the Code is itself a major non-conformity. A company whose SMS is complete and unrevised, whose audits find nothing and whose non-conformity file is empty, is not demonstrating excellence - it is demonstrating that the loop is not running.

That is the sentence to end limb (a) on: the Code's real subject is not the procedures, it is the loop that keeps correcting them.

(b) The evolution of the Code in response to emerging challenges (8 marks)

9. Why the Code exists, and the shift it represents

Evolution has to start from the origin. Through the twentieth century, maritime safety was regulated almost entirely by specifying the ship. Yet ships that satisfied every construction, subdivision, fire and life-saving requirement continued to be lost, and when the losses were investigated the fault was found repeatedly in the management of the operation rather than in the hardware: responsibilities ashore undefined, commercial pressure reaching the master unopposed, defects reported before without effective action, and no route by which a concern could travel from the ship to a decision-maker in the office. A major ro-ro passenger casualty and the public inquiry that followed it made that diagnosis unavoidable, and the Organization's response was an instrument of a different kind.

The shift is the point, and it should be stated explicitly. The ISM Code moved regulation from hardware to management, from what can be inspected on a ship to what can be demonstrated about a company, and from prescription to a goal-based system with mandatory audit. Every later adaptation follows from that architecture.

10. Evolution one - from recommendation to mandatory instrument

The Code was adopted as an Assembly resolution, A.741(18), which as an instrument type is a recommendation. The single largest change in its history was its incorporation into SOLAS as chapter IX, which made it mandatory, phased in by ship type and size. That change did three things: it converted 'should' from advice into an obligation whose breach affects certification; it brought the Code within port State control, so that a defective SMS became a detainable matter in any port; and it made the DOC and SMC statutory certificates rather than voluntary attestations.

11. Evolution two - the amendment chain

Since becoming mandatory the Code has been amended through a chain of MSC resolutions - MSC.104(73), MSC.179(79), MSC.195(80), MSC.273(85) and MSC.353(92) - adopted under the SOLAS article VIII procedure by virtue of regulation IX/1.1. The direction of travel across the chain has been consistent: sharper definition of Company responsibility, clearer treatment of non-conformities and their analysis, and firmer requirements for verification, audit and review.

MSC.353(92) was adopted on 21 June 2013 and its amendments entered into force on 1 January 2015. That is the operative text, and it is worth noticing what that fact implies for limb (b): the Code's text has been substantially unchanged for the better part of a decade, through a period in which the industry changed considerably. The evolution the examiner is asking about has therefore happened mostly without amendment - and saying so honestly is a stronger answer than inventing an amendment history.

12. Evolution three - technological advancement

Integrated bridge and engine control systems, remote monitoring and shore-based diagnostics, condition-based maintenance, planned maintenance software, electronic record-keeping and increasing automation have all arrived since the Code was written. The Code has absorbed them without a new element, because its existing requirements are written at a level of generality that reaches them:

  • Element 6 - familiarisation and training. As systems become more capable, competence in them becomes the binding constraint. The requirement that new and transferred personnel be properly familiarised, that essential pre-sailing instructions be identified and given, and that training needs be identified and met, is what the Code actually says about automation.
  • Element 7 - shipboard operations. Procedures, plans, instructions and checklists for key shipboard operations extend naturally to operating and, importantly, to reverting from automated systems.
  • Element 10 - maintenance and critical equipment. The requirement to identify equipment and technical systems whose sudden failure may cause a hazardous situation, and to test stand-by arrangements and systems not in continuous use, is precisely the discipline that automation demands - because the more reliable a system is, the less often the manual fallback is exercised and the less likely it is to work when needed.
  • Element 11 - documentation. Control of documents and data, with obsolete versions promptly removed, applies to software versions and electronic manuals as much as to paper.

There is an honest limitation to record. The Code assumes a Company, a master and shipboard personnel. It manages technology on board a manned ship. It does not, in the text operative at this sitting, address remotely operated or autonomous operation, and an answer that claims otherwise is asserting something the instrument does not say.

13. Evolution four - cyber risk

This is the one named challenge with an instrument-level response, and it should be named precisely.

Resolution MSC.428(98), Maritime cyber risk management in safety management systems, was adopted on 16 June 2017. It recognised the urgent need to raise awareness of cyber risk threats and vulnerabilities to support safe and secure shipping which is operationally resilient to cyber risks, recognised that Administrations, classification societies, shipowners and operators, agents, equipment manufacturers, service providers, ports and port facilities and all other stakeholders should expedite work towards safeguarding shipping from current and emerging cyber threats, and bore in mind MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3, Guidelines on maritime cyber risk management, approved by the Facilitation Committee at its forty-first session and by the Maritime Safety Committee at its ninety-eighth session.

The important structural point is what the Organization did not do. It did not add an element to the Code, and it did not write a cyber code. It directed that cyber risks be addressed within the existing safety management system, using machinery the Code already had:

  • Element 1.2.2 - the objective of assessing all identified risks to ships, personnel and the environment and establishing appropriate safeguards. A cyber risk is an identified risk.
  • Element 7 - procedures for key shipboard operations, extending to the systems those operations depend on.
  • Element 8 - emergency preparedness, extending to response and recovery when a system is compromised or unavailable.
  • Element 9 - reporting, investigation and analysis, extending to cyber incidents and near-misses.
  • Element 10 - identification of equipment and technical systems whose sudden failure may cause a hazardous situation, which now includes the networks and control systems those systems depend on.
  • Elements 6 and 11 - competence and familiarisation, and control of documents, data and software versions.

That is the clearest single illustration of how this Code evolves: a threat that nobody imagined when it was drafted was accommodated without amending it, because it was written in terms of risk, procedure, competence, reporting and audit rather than in terms of any particular hazard. MIW holds no copy of MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 in any revision, so no revision number, date or content of the Guidelines is asserted here.

14. Evolution five - environmental sustainability, and the honest limit

Two things must be separated here, and conflating them is the commonest substantive error on limb (b).

  • What the Code has always had. Environmental protection is in its title, in its objectives - avoidance of damage to the environment, in particular to the marine environment - and in element 2, which requires a safety AND environmental protection policy. The Code has never been a safety-only instrument, and elements 7, 8, 9 and 10 apply to pollution prevention exactly as they apply to safety.
  • What the Code does not contain. The instruments that drive environmental performance today - the SEEMP, EEDI, EEXI and the operational CII rating - are MARPOL Annex VI measures. They are not part of the ISM Code, and no amendment to the ISM Code introduced them.

What the Code contributes is the management framework through which those obligations are actually delivered on board - defined responsibilities, documented procedures, records, familiarisation, reporting of non-conformities, audit and review. A ship complies with a carbon intensity requirement through data, procedures and verified records, and the discipline that produces all three is the SMS. That is a real contribution and it is a different one from being the source of the obligation.

The closing judgement, and it is the answer to 'examine'. The ISM Code has evolved less by amendment than by generality. Because it regulates a management system rather than a hazard, it has been able to absorb automation, cyber risk and environmental obligation without being rewritten, and MSC.428(98) is the proof of the method: the Organization's response to an entirely new class of threat was to say address it in your existing system.

That flexibility is a genuine strength and it carries a corresponding weakness, which an honest examination should record. A Code that accommodates everything specifies nothing, so the quality of the response depends almost entirely on how seriously the individual company takes its own risk assessment - and the Code's own answer to that problem is the one already given in limb (a): the lack of effective and systematic implementation of its requirements is itself a major non-conformity.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Both limbs use the verb examine, which is stronger than describe. Limb (a) has three demands - the role of the SMS, its components, and the significance of continuous improvement - and the third is the analytical one. Limb (b) names three challenges and each must get its own heading, because the examiner will be looking for them by name.

The most common way to lose marks in limb (a) is to spend the whole limb listing the twelve elements. The list is worth about two marks; the role and the significance of continuous improvement are worth more, and they require argument rather than recall. Give the six functional requirements and the twelve elements compactly - a table does it in a third of the space - and spend the saved time on why the system exists and why the loop matters.

In limb (b) the strongest thing you can do is be honest: the Code has evolved mostly without being amended. Its text has been substantially unchanged since 1 January 2015, and it has absorbed automation, cyber risk and environmental obligation through the generality of what it already said. That is a more sophisticated answer than an invented amendment history, and it is also true.

The idea underneath the Code

For most of the twentieth century, maritime safety regulation answered one question: is the ship built and equipped correctly? It is a good question and it produced enormous gains. But it has a blind spot, and the blind spot is fatal in both senses. A ship can satisfy every construction, subdivision, fire and life-saving requirement and still be lost because of how she was operated - because nobody had written down who was responsible for what, because the pressure to sail on time reached the master with nothing to counterbalance it, because the same defect had been reported twice before and nothing had happened, and because there was no route by which a worry could travel from the engine room to somebody able to act on it.

None of that is visible to a surveyor examining a ship, and none of it can be fixed by a construction standard. So the Organization wrote an instrument that regulates the system instead: define the responsibilities and write them down; put a named person ashore who can reach the top of the company directly; state in the ship's own manual that the master may overrule anyone in the interests of safety; write procedures for the operations that matter; prepare for emergencies and practise; and report, investigate, analyse, audit, review and correct.

Once you see that the Code is a response to a diagnosis, everything in it becomes predictable. The Designated Person exists because concerns were being filtered out. The master's overriding authority is stated expressly because masters felt unable to refuse. Element 9 requires corrective action to prevent recurrence because the same defects kept coming back. And the audit machinery in element 12 exists because a goal-based instrument that nobody checks is a wish.

Functional requirements against elements - the map worth carrying

Functional requirement (1.4)Delivered mainly by
A safety and environmental protection policyElement 2
Instructions and procedures for safe operation and environmental protectionElements 7, 10, 11
Defined levels of authority and lines of communicationElements 3, 4, 5, 6
Procedures for reporting accidents and non-conformitiesElement 9
Procedures to prepare for and respond to emergenciesElement 8
Procedures for internal audits and management reviewsElement 12

If you can reproduce this map you can answer 'components' from either end, and you have shown the examiner that you understand the Code's architecture rather than having memorised a list. It also means that if you forget an element you can reconstruct it by asking which functional requirement is unaccounted for.

The three people the Code creates

Much of the Code's practical effect comes down to three roles, and it is worth knowing why each exists.

  • The Company. Element 1.1.2 defines it as the owner or any other organisation or person - manager, bareboat charterer - who has assumed responsibility for the operation of the ship and has agreed to take over all the duties and responsibilities the Code imposes. This definition exists so that responsibility cannot evaporate into a chain of management companies: somebody is the Company, and element 3.1 requires the owner to report that entity's details to the Administration.
  • The Designated Person. A person or persons ashore with direct access to the highest level of management. The words 'direct access' are the whole provision. The DPA exists so that a safety concern from a ship cannot be stopped by the superintendent, the operations manager or the commercial department - the very people whose decisions may be the problem.
  • The Master. Element 5.2 requires the SMS to contain a clear statement emphasising the master's authority, and gives him overriding authority and the responsibility to make decisions with respect to safety and pollution prevention and to request the Company's assistance as may be necessary. It is written into the company's own manual, so the master is not asserting a right against his employer - he is exercising one his employer has documented.

For a Chief Engineer the third and second are the practical ones: if a machinery defect makes a passage unsafe and the office disagrees, the Code has given the master the authority and has given you a documented route to the DPA. That is not an abstraction; it is the reason the elements are worth knowing.

Continuous improvement - why it deserves the emphasis

The stem singles it out, and the reason is that a goal-based instrument is only as good as its feedback loop. Since the Code does not tell you what your procedures must say, the only thing an auditor can meaningfully test is whether your system works and whether you know when it does not. Take away element 9 and element 12 and the Code becomes a requirement to own a set of manuals.

The detail that shows real understanding is element 9's requirement that corrective action include measures intended to prevent recurrence. That single phrase is the difference between an investigation that ends 'the valve was left shut' and one that ends 'the procedure did not require the valve to be checked, and has been changed'. The first closes a report; the second changes the system.

And the sharpest point of all is in the definitions: the lack of effective and systematic implementation of a requirement of the Code is a major non-conformity. A company with a beautiful, complete, entirely unchanged SMS, whose internal audits find nothing and whose non-conformity register is empty, has not achieved excellence. It has demonstrated that the loop is not running - and the Code says so.

Limb (b) - how to be right about evolution

The trap is to assume the Code must have been repeatedly amended to keep up. It has not. The operative text has been in force since 1 January 2015, and the honest structure of limb (b) is therefore:

  1. The origin and the shift - from regulating hardware to regulating management. This is the biggest evolution in the Code's history and it happened before the Code existed, in the diagnosis that produced it.
  2. Recommendation to mandatory - adoption as A.741(18) and then incorporation into SOLAS as chapter IX, bringing port State control and statutory certification with it. This is the second biggest.
  3. The amendment chain - MSC.104(73), MSC.179(79), MSC.195(80), MSC.273(85), MSC.353(92) - tightening Company responsibility, non-conformity handling and verification.
  4. Then the three named challenges, all of which the Code has absorbed through generality, with cyber the single instrument-level response at MSC.428(98).

Stating point 4 explicitly - that the Code adapts by being general rather than by being amended - is the observation that turns a list into an examination. And it sets up the honest closing criticism: a Code that can accommodate anything prescribes nothing, so its effectiveness rests on the seriousness of each company's own risk assessment.

Examiner traps

  • Putting the SEEMP, EEDI, EEXI or the CII inside the ISM Code. They are MARPOL Annex VI. The ISM Code supplies the management framework through which they are implemented, which is a different claim.
  • Inventing an amendment for cyber risk. MSC.428(98) is a resolution directing that cyber risk be addressed in the SMS. No element was added to the Code.
  • Quoting a revision number for the cyber guidelines. MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 has been revised more than once. Name the circular; do not date it unless you are certain.
  • Listing the twelve elements and stopping. The list is worth about two marks out of eight in limb (a).
  • Treating continuous improvement as a slogan. Name element 9, element 12 and the twelve-month audit interval, and the marks follow.
  • Saying the Code makes ships safe. It makes management systems auditable. Whether it makes ships safe depends on whether the loop is actually run - and the Code's own definition of a major non-conformity is what admits that.
  • Claiming the Code addresses autonomous ships. The operative text assumes a Company, a master and shipboard personnel.

If you only have ten minutes for this question

(a), eight lines. The ISM Code, adopted by A.741(18) and mandatory through SOLAS chapter IX, as amended through MSC.353(92) in force from 1 January 2015, regulates management rather than hardware. It works through a Safety Management System - a structured and documented system enabling Company personnel to implement effectively the Company safety and environmental protection policy - because safe operation cannot be prescribed the way scantlings can: the Code sets objectives, requires a system, lets the company write procedures suited to its operation, and then holds it to them. Element 1.4 requires six functional requirements: a policy; instructions and procedures for safe operation and environmental protection; defined levels of authority and lines of communication; procedures for reporting accidents and non-conformities; procedures for emergency preparedness and response; and procedures for internal audits and management reviews. Part A delivers these in twelve elements - policy, company responsibility, designated person, master's authority, resources and personnel, shipboard operations, emergency preparedness, reports and analysis, maintenance, documentation and company verification - and Part B adds certification, with a Document of Compliance to the Company for up to five years, annually verified within three months of the anniversary date, and a Safety Management Certificate to the ship. The SMS's role is to convert objectives into performable acts, to create the shore-to-ship link through the Designated Person and the master's overriding authority, to generate the objective evidence on which compliance is audited, and to correct itself. Continuous improvement is produced by element 9 - report, investigate, analyse and take corrective action including measures to prevent recurrence - and element 12 - internal audits at intervals not exceeding twelve months, periodic evaluation of effectiveness and timely corrective action. It is significant because without it the system describes a ship nobody operates, because it is what gives a goal-based Code any teeth, because it turns incidents into changed procedures, and because the Code itself treats the lack of effective and systematic implementation as a major non-conformity.

(b), six lines. The Code exists because ships that met every construction requirement were still being lost through management failure - undefined responsibility ashore, unopposed commercial pressure, defects reported without action, and no route for a concern to reach a decision-maker - so regulation shifted from hardware to management and from what can be inspected on a ship to what can be demonstrated about a company. Its first great evolution was from Assembly recommendation to mandatory instrument through SOLAS chapter IX, bringing port State control and statutory certification. Its second was the amendment chain - MSC.104(73), MSC.179(79), MSC.195(80), MSC.273(85), MSC.353(92) - tightening Company responsibility, non-conformity handling and verification. Since then it has evolved mostly without amendment. Technological advancement and automation are absorbed through elements 6, 7, 10 and 11 - familiarisation and competence, procedures for key operations, identification of critical equipment with regular testing of stand-by systems, and control of documents and software versions. Cyber risk drew the one instrument-level response: MSC.428(98) of 16 June 2017, bearing in mind MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3, which added no new element but directed that cyber risks be addressed within the existing SMS using its risk assessment, procedures, emergency preparedness, reporting, competence and audit machinery. Environmental sustainability has been in the Code's title, objectives and policy requirement since adoption, but the decarbonisation instruments - the SEEMP, EEXI and the CII - are MARPOL Annex VI measures outside the Code; what the ISM Code contributes is the management framework through which they are delivered. The Code has therefore evolved by generality rather than by amendment - a genuine strength, and a weakness, since a Code that accommodates everything prescribes nothing and depends on the seriousness of each company's own risk assessment.

Common mistakes

These are the places this particular question goes wrong, taken from the ambiguities identified before drafting and from the single error most likely to cost marks.

The one that costs most: Claiming the Code has been repeatedly amended to address technology, cyber risk and environmental sustainability. IT HAS NOT. Its text has been substantially unchanged since MSC.353(92) took effect on 1 January 2015, and it has absorbed all three largely through the GENERALITY of its existing requirements - the one instrument-level response being MSC.428(98), which directed that cyber risks be addressed WITHIN the existing SMS framework rather than by adding a new element. The second trap is putting the SEEMP, EEXI or the CII inside the ISM Code; they are MARPOL Annex VI instruments.

The wording of the stem invites four specific misreadings, and each is resolved in the answer rather than left to chance:

  • 'Components' of the SMS could mean the six FUNCTIONAL REQUIREMENTS at element 1.4 or the twelve PART A ELEMENTS. The answer gives both and shows how they relate, because the functional requirements are what the Code demands an SMS contain, and the elements are how the Code then specifies each of them. Giving only one is defensible; giving both and explaining the relationship is better and costs three lines.
  • 'Continuous improvement' is not a defined term in the Code and does not appear as a heading. It is an outcome produced by several elements working together - reporting and analysis at 9, maintenance at 10, and verification, review and evaluation at 12 - and the answer says so rather than pretending there is a clause called continuous improvement.
  • 'Evolution ... including technological advancements, cyber risks, and environmental sustainability' invites the claim that the Code has been repeatedly amended to address each. IT HAS NOT. The Code's text has been substantially unchanged since MSC.353(92), and it has accommodated all three largely through the GENERALITY of its existing requirements - with cyber the one exception, where MSC.428(98) directed that the risks be addressed within the existing SMS framework. The honest answer says this, and it is a stronger answer than an invented amendment history.
  • 'Environmental sustainability' is the loosest of the three. Environmental protection has been in the Code's title and objectives since adoption, but the decarbonisation instruments - the SEEMP, EEXI, the CII - sit in MARPOL Annex VI and NOT in the ISM Code. The answer distinguishes them, because conflating them is a substantive error.

Likely oral follow-up

The retrieval questions below are the ones this answer is built to survive. They are the same questions an examiner reaches for when testing whether a written answer was understood or reproduced.

  • Give the route for the SMS and ISM evolution question. — 8 + 8, and the verb is EXAMINE in both limbs. Sections 7-8 and 12-14 are the analytical parts and they carry the discriminating marks; the printed three challenges must each get their own heading.
  • State the six functional requirements for a Safety Management System. — The single most quotable list in the question and it maps directly onto the Part A elements. Requirement (3) is the one that answers what the Code was written to fix.
  • What is a Safety Management System, and why does the Code work through one? — The conceptual heart of limb (a). It explains why the Code says 'should establish procedures' rather than telling you what the procedures are.
  • Name the twelve Part A elements. — 'Components' in the printed stem. Give these and the six functional requirements and the components demand is fully answered.
  • How does the Code actually produce continuous improvement? — 'Significance of continuous improvement' is worth two marks and cannot be earned by asserting that improvement is important. Name the mechanism.
  • How did the ISM Code respond to cyber risk? — The best single illustration of how the Code evolves: not by growing, but by having been written generally enough that a threat nobody imagined in 1993 fits inside it.
  • Has the ISM Code been amended to deal with environmental sustainability and decarbonisation? — The commonest substantive error on limb (b), and correcting it demonstrates that you know which instrument does what.
  • Why was the ISM Code needed when SOLAS already existed? — The opening of limb (b) and the point on which the whole evolution argument rests.

Memory framework

The route, chunked: (a) EIGHT: WHAT IT IS (1), WHAT AN SMS IS (2), SIX FUNCTIONALS (3), TWELVE ELEMENTS (4), CERTIFICATES (5), THE ROLE (6), THE LOOP - HOW (7), THE LOOP - WHY (8). (b) SIX: WHY IT EXISTS (9), BECAME MANDATORY (10), AMENDED (11), then the THREE PRINTED CHALLENGES - TECH (12), CYBER (13), ENVIRONMENT (14).

Fifteen-second recall: ISM Code adopted by A.741(18), mandatory through SOLAS chapter IX, amended through MSC.353(92) (adopted 21 June 2013, in force 1 January 2015). It regulates MANAGEMENT, not hardware. SMS = a structured and documented system enabling Company personnel to implement effectively the Company safety and environmental protection policy. SIX functional requirements at 1.4: a safety and environmental protection policy; instructions and procedures for safe operation and environmental protection; defined levels of authority and lines of communication; procedures for reporting accidents and non-conformities; procedures to prepare for and respond to emergencies; procedures for internal audits and management reviews. TWELVE Part A elements; Part B is certification - DOC to the Company up to five years, annually verified within three months of the anniversary date, and the SMC to the ship. CONTINUOUS IMPROVEMENT = element 9 reporting and analysis with corrective action to prevent recurrence, plus element 12 internal audits at intervals NOT EXCEEDING TWELVE MONTHS (exceptionally extendable by not more than three), periodic evaluation of effectiveness, and timely corrective action. EVOLUTION: recommendation to mandatory; the amendment chain; and adaptation to technology, CYBER (MSC.428(98), 16 June 2017, bearing in mind MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3) and environment MOSTLY BY GENERALITY rather than by amendment.

The numbers and thresholds worth carrying exactly:

  • ISM Code adopted by resolution A.741(18); mandatory through SOLAS chapter IX
  • MSC.353(92) adopted 21 June 2013; its amendments in force 1 January 2015 - the operative text at this sitting
  • SIX functional requirements for an SMS at element 1.4
  • TWELVE elements in Part A; elements 13 to 16 in Part B
  • Element 12.1 - internal safety audits on board and ashore at intervals NOT EXCEEDING TWELVE MONTHS; in exceptional circumstances exceeded by NOT MORE THAN THREE MONTHS
  • Element 13.2 - the Document of Compliance issued for a period specified by the Administration NOT EXCEEDING FIVE YEARS
  • Element 13.4 - annual verification of the DOC within THREE MONTHS BEFORE OR AFTER the anniversary date
  • MSC.428(98) adopted 16 June 2017 - maritime cyber risk management in safety management systems

Keywords that unlock the rest: ISM Code, A.741(18), SOLAS chapter IX, MSC.353(92), safety management system, SMS, functional requirements, Document of Compliance, DOC, Safety Management Certificate, SMC, Designated Person Ashore, DPA, master's overriding authority, non-conformity, major non-conformity, objective evidence, observation, internal audit, twelve months, management review, corrective action, continuous improvement, emergency preparedness, familiarisation, critical equipment, MSC.428(98), cyber risk, MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3, SEEMP, environmental protection

Regulation and source map

Critical regulation: The ISM Code as adopted by resolution A.741(18) and amended through MSC.353(92), made mandatory by SOLAS chapter IX; together with resolution MSC.428(98) on maritime cyber risk management in safety management systems.

Every instrument this answer relies on, in the order the answer uses them:

  • ISM Code - International Management Code for the Safe Operation of Ships and for Pollution Prevention, adopted by resolution A.741(18)
  • ISM Code as amended by MSC.104(73), MSC.179(79), MSC.195(80), MSC.273(85) and MSC.353(92)
  • Resolution MSC.353(92), adopted 21 June 2013 - the amendments in force from 1 January 2015, the operative text at this sitting
  • SOLAS chapter IX - Management for the Safe Operation of Ships, which makes the ISM Code mandatory
  • SOLAS regulation IX/1.1 - the procedure for amending the ISM Code
  • ISM Code Part A, element 1 - General, including 1.2 objectives and 1.4 functional requirements for a Safety Management System
  • ISM Code Part A, elements 2 to 12 - policy, company responsibility, designated person, master's responsibility, resources and personnel, shipboard operations, emergency preparedness, reports and analysis of non-conformities, maintenance, documentation, and company verification review and evaluation
  • ISM Code Part B, elements 13 to 16 - certification and periodical verification, interim certification, verification, and forms of certificates
  • Resolution MSC.428(98), adopted 16 June 2017 - Maritime cyber risk management in safety management systems
  • MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 - Guidelines on maritime cyber risk management (identified; approved 2017, and revised since - see unresolved)
  • Resolution A.1118(30) - Revised Guidelines on the implementation of the ISM Code by Administrations (identified only; not read at source)
  • MARPOL Annex VI and the SEEMP - the environmental management instrument that sits alongside but outside the ISM Code

Evidence grades behind those instruments on this question: 34 read at source this session (P1), 3 authoritative secondary (P2), 5 carried from the verified MIW built set, and 6 engineering or legal judgement declared as such. 3 claims are stated at a lower evidence grade.

The sources themselves, with what was read and at what grade:

  • P1 PRIMARY - ISM Code, Parts A and B, read in full at source this session from the corpus holding. Element 1 (General) including the definitions at 1.1.1 to 1.1.12, the objectives at 1.2 and the six functional requirements at 1.4; element 2 (Safety and Environmental Protection Policy); element 3 (Company Responsibilities and Authority); element 4 (Designated Person(s)); element 5 (Master's Responsibility and Authority, including the overriding authority at 5.2); element 6 (Resources and Personnel); element 7 (Shipboard Operations); element 8 (Emergency Preparedness); element 9 (Reports and Analysis of Non-conformities, Accidents and Hazardous Occurrences); element 10 (Maintenance of the Ship and Equipment); element 11 (Documentation); element 12 (Company Verification, Review and Evaluation, including the twelve-month internal audit interval at 12.1 and its three-month exceptional extension); and Part B element 13 (Certification and Periodical Verification, including the five-year Document of Compliance and its annual verification within three months before or after the anniversary date).
  • P1 PRIMARY - ISM Code definitions at 1.1, read at source: Safety Management System as a structured and documented system enabling Company personnel to implement effectively the Company safety and environmental protection policy; Company; Document of Compliance; Safety Management Certificate; objective evidence; observation; non-conformity; and major non-conformity as an identifiable deviation posing a serious threat to personnel or the ship or a serious risk to the environment requiring immediate corrective action, or the lack of effective and systematic implementation of a requirement of the Code.
  • P1 PRIMARY - Resolution MSC.353(92), read at source this session: adopted 21 June 2013, amendments to the ISM Code adopted under article VIII(b)(iv) of SOLAS, and reciting resolution A.741(18) by which the Assembly adopted the Code and that it has become mandatory under SOLAS chapter IX. This is the last amendment held in the corpus and it is the operative text at this sitting.
  • P1 PRIMARY - Resolution MSC.428(98), Maritime cyber risk management in safety management systems, adopted 16 June 2017, read at source this session. It recognises the urgent need to raise awareness of cyber risk threats and vulnerabilities to support safe and secure shipping operationally resilient to cyber risks, bears in mind MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 on Guidelines on maritime cyber risk management approved by the Facilitation Committee at its forty-first session and by the MSC at its ninety-eighth session, and recalls resolution A.741(18).
  • P1 PRIMARY - the corpus amendment chain for the Code, held as official resolutions: A.741(18), MSC.104(73), MSC.179(79), MSC.195(80), MSC.273(85) and MSC.353(92). Their identities and their place in the chain are taken from the held set; only MSC.353(92) and MSC.428(98) were read in full.
  • INTERNAL_REUSE_VERIFIED - QP2406-Q5 (June 2024), read in full this session as the primary temporal control. It predates this sitting by two months and is the only ISM answer in the corpus anchored inside the same window.
  • INTERNAL_REUSE_VERIFIED - QP2606-Q5 (June 2026, the donor) and QP2502-Q3 (February 2025), both read in full this session for the house answer route and for the reversal check described in temporal_review.
  • ENGINEERING_JUDGEMENT - the analysis in limb (b) of what the Code does and does not do about technology, cyber and environmental sustainability, and the observation that the Code adapts by GENERALITY rather than by amendment. Declared as judgement.

Uncertainty and evidence limits

What this answer deliberately does not assert, and why. These are declared limits rather than omissions, and a candidate revising from this answer should know where MIW stopped.

  • NO ISM CODE AMENDMENT LATER THAN MSC.353(92) IS HELD OR ASSERTED. The corpus holds the chain A.741(18), MSC.104(73), MSC.179(79), MSC.195(80), MSC.273(85) and MSC.353(92). MSC.353(92) was adopted 21 June 2013 and its amendments entered into force 1 January 2015. The answer describes the Code as amended through MSC.353(92) and asserts NO later amendment in either direction.
  • THE REVISION STATUS OF MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 IS NOT ASSERTED. The circular is identified from the recitals of MSC.428(98), which were read at source and which record its approval by the Facilitation Committee at its forty-first session and by the MSC at its ninety-eighth session. MIW HOLDS NO COPY OF THE CIRCULAR IN ANY REVISION, so no revision number, no revision date and no content of the Guidelines is quoted or described in detail.
  • RESOLUTION A.1118(30) - Revised Guidelines on the implementation of the ISM Code by Administrations - IS IDENTIFIED ONLY. MIW holds no copy and it was not read at source; nothing is attributed to its content beyond its identity and subject matter.
  • THE CORPUS ISM CODE HOLDING IS A TRANSCRIPTION IN DOCX FORM, NOT AN IMO-PUBLISHED PDF OF THE CONSOLIDATED CODE. It was read in full and its content is consistent with the amendment chain held as official resolutions, but a reviewer should treat the element wording as a transcription rather than as a certified consolidation. This is recorded rather than glossed.
  • NO STATISTIC IS ASSERTED - no casualty reduction figure attributed to the ISM Code, no detention rate, no number of DOCs or SMCs issued, no cyber incident count. None can be sourced and MIW's evidence-declaration standard requires an unverifiable quantity to be omitted rather than given false precision.
  • NO NAMED CASUALTY IS DESCRIBED AS THE CAUSE OF THE CODE. The Code's origin in the loss of a ro-ro passenger ship and the public inquiry that followed is referred to at the level of what the inquiry found about shore management, which is the load-bearing point; no casualty is named and no finding is quoted.

Claims stated at a lower evidence grade:

  • The ISM Code as amended through MSC.353(92) is the operative text at this sitting, and no later amendment is asserted. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION) — Stated as a limitation of the held chain rather than as a positive finding. The corpus holds A.741(18), MSC.104(73), MSC.179(79), MSC.195(80), MSC.273(85) and MSC.353(92) as official resolutions; MSC.353(92) was read at source and is dated 21 June 2013 with entry into force 1 January 2015. A copy that predates a later amendment cannot prove one does not exist, so the answer asserts none in either direction.
  • The wording and element structure of the ISM Code quoted in this answer. (B_CURRENCY_CHECK) — THE CORPUS ISM HOLDING IS A TRANSCRIPTION IN DOCX FORM, NOT AN IMO-PUBLISHED PDF OF THE CONSOLIDATED CODE. It was read in full this session and its content is consistent with the amendment chain held as official resolutions, but a reviewer should treat the element wording as a transcription rather than as a certified consolidation and may wish to spot-check the intervals at 12.1 and 13.4 against a published edition.
  • The treatment of MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 and of resolution A.1118(30). (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION) — MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 is identified from the recitals of MSC.428(98), which were read at source. MIW HOLDS NO COPY OF THE CIRCULAR IN ANY REVISION, so NO REVISION NUMBER, DATE OR CONTENT is asserted - the Guidelines have been revised more than once and any revision stated would be a currency exposure. A.1118(30) is identified by subject matter only and was not read at source.
Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 14 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________(a) What the Code is, and how it binds
  2. __________(a) What a Safety Management System is, and why the Code works through one
  3. __________(a) The six functional requirements - what every SMS must contain
  4. __________(a) The components - Part A, elements 1 to 12
  5. __________(a) The certification layer - Part B
  6. __________(a) The role the SMS plays in implementing the Code
  7. __________(a) Continuous improvement - the mechanism
  8. __________(a) Continuous improvement - why it is the significant part
  9. __________(b) Why the Code exists, and the shift it represents
  10. __________(b) Evolution one - from recommendation to mandatory instrument
  11. __________(b) Evolution two - the amendment chain
  12. __________(b) Evolution three - technological advancement
  13. __________(b) Evolution four - cyber risk
  14. __________(b) Evolution five - environmental sustainability, and the honest limit
Flashcards 8

(a) 1 what the Code is and how it binds. 2 what an SMS is. 3 the six functional requirements. 4 the twelve Part A elements. 5 the certification layer. 6 the role the SMS plays in implementing the Code. 7 continuous improvement - the mechanism. 8 continuous improvement - why it is significant. (b) 9 why the Code exists and the shift from hardware to management. 10 recommendation to mandatory instrument. 11 the amendment chain. 12 technological advancement. 13 cyber risk. 14 environmental sustainability and the honest limit.

8 + 8, and the verb is EXAMINE in both limbs. Sections 7-8 and 12-14 are the analytical parts and they carry the discriminating marks; the printed three challenges must each get their own heading.

Element 1.4. Every company should develop, implement and maintain an SMS which includes: (1) a safety and environmental protection policy; (2) instructions and procedures to ensure safe operation of ships and protection of the environment in compliance with relevant international and flag State legislation; (3) defined levels of authority and lines of communication between, and amongst, shore and shipboard personnel; (4) procedures for reporting accidents and non-conformities with the provisions of the Code; (5) procedures to prepare for and respond to emergency situations; and (6) procedures for internal audits and management reviews.

The single most quotable list in the question and it maps directly onto the Part A elements. Requirement (3) is the one that answers what the Code was written to fix.

A structured and documented system enabling Company personnel to implement effectively the Company safety and environmental protection policy. The Code works through it because safety of operation cannot be prescribed the way scantlings or lifeboat capacity can - the right procedure depends on the ship, the trade, the crew and the company. So the Code sets OBJECTIVES (safety at sea, prevention of human injury or loss of life, avoidance of damage to the environment and to property) and REQUIRES A SYSTEM that delivers them, leaving the company to write procedures suited to its own operation and then holding it to what it wrote.

The conceptual heart of limb (a). It explains why the Code says 'should establish procedures' rather than telling you what the procedures are.

1 General (definitions, objectives, application, functional requirements). 2 Safety and Environmental Protection Policy. 3 Company Responsibilities and Authority. 4 Designated Person(s). 5 Master's Responsibility and Authority. 6 Resources and Personnel. 7 Shipboard Operations. 8 Emergency Preparedness. 9 Reports and Analysis of Non-conformities, Accidents and Hazardous Occurrences. 10 Maintenance of the Ship and Equipment. 11 Documentation. 12 Company Verification, Review and Evaluation. Part B then adds 13 Certification and Periodical Verification, 14 Interim Certification, 15 Verification and 16 Forms of Certificates.

'Components' in the printed stem. Give these and the six functional requirements and the components demand is fully answered.

Through three elements working together. ELEMENT 9 - the SMS must include procedures ensuring that non-conformities, accidents and hazardous situations are REPORTED to the Company, INVESTIGATED and ANALYSED with the object of improving safety and pollution prevention, and the Company must establish procedures for implementing CORRECTIVE ACTION INCLUDING MEASURES INTENDED TO PREVENT RECURRENCE. ELEMENT 12 - internal safety audits on board and ashore at intervals NOT EXCEEDING TWELVE MONTHS, exceptionally extendable by not more than three; periodic verification that those undertaking delegated ISM tasks conform; PERIODIC EVALUATION OF THE EFFECTIVENESS of the SMS; auditors independent of the areas audited where practicable; results brought to the attention of all personnel responsible; and TIMELY CORRECTIVE ACTION by the management personnel responsible. ELEMENT 10 supplies the maintenance and critical-equipment feedback that populates the loop.

'Significance of continuous improvement' is worth two marks and cannot be earned by asserting that improvement is important. Name the mechanism.

By resolution MSC.428(98), MARITIME CYBER RISK MANAGEMENT IN SAFETY MANAGEMENT SYSTEMS, adopted 16 June 2017. It recognised the urgent need to raise awareness of cyber risk threats and vulnerabilities to support safe and secure shipping that is operationally resilient to cyber risks, and bore in mind MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3, the Guidelines on maritime cyber risk management approved by the Facilitation Committee at its forty-first session and by the MSC at its ninety-eighth session. CRUCIALLY, NO NEW ELEMENT WAS ADDED TO THE CODE. The response was to direct that cyber risks be addressed within the EXISTING safety management system - using the Code's own risk assessment, procedures, familiarisation and audit machinery.

The best single illustration of how the Code evolves: not by growing, but by having been written generally enough that a threat nobody imagined in 1993 fits inside it.

NO, and saying so is the stronger answer. Environmental protection has been in the Code's TITLE and in its objectives since adoption - the objectives include avoidance of damage to the environment, and the policy required by element 2 is a safety AND ENVIRONMENTAL PROTECTION policy - so the Code has always had an environmental limb. But the decarbonisation instruments are NOT in the ISM Code: the SEEMP, EEXI and the CII rating are MARPOL Annex VI measures. What the ISM Code contributes is the management framework through which those obligations are implemented on board - procedures, responsibilities, records, audit and review - which is a real contribution and a different one.

The commonest substantive error on limb (b), and correcting it demonstrates that you know which instrument does what.

Because SOLAS regulates the SHIP and the failures were in the MANAGEMENT. Investigations into major casualties repeatedly found ships that complied with the construction and equipment requirements and were lost anyway, because responsibilities ashore were undefined, commercial pressure reached the master unopposed, defects had been reported before without action, and there was no route by which a concern could reach a decision-maker. None of that is curable by a construction standard. The ISM Code therefore regulates the SYSTEM: defined authority and lines of communication, a Designated Person with direct access to the highest level of management, the master's overriding authority expressly stated, reporting and analysis of non-conformities, and audit. It shifted compliance from what can be inspected on a ship to what can be demonstrated about a company.

The opening of limb (b) and the point on which the whole evolution argument rests.

Quick revision
15-second recall
ISM Code adopted by A.741(18), mandatory through SOLAS chapter IX, amended through MSC.353(92) (adopted 21 June 2013, in force 1 January 2015). It regulates MANAGEMENT, not hardware. SMS = a structured and documented system enabling Company personnel to implement effectively the Company safety and environmental protection policy. SIX functional requirements at 1.4: a safety and environmental protection policy; instructions and procedures for safe operation and environmental protection; defined levels of authority and lines of communication; procedures for reporting accidents and non-conformities; procedures to prepare for and respond to emergencies; procedures for internal audits and management reviews. TWELVE Part A elements; Part B is certification - DOC to the Company up to five years, annually verified within three months of the anniversary date, and the SMC to the ship. CONTINUOUS IMPROVEMENT = element 9 reporting and analysis with corrective action to prevent recurrence, plus element 12 internal audits at intervals NOT EXCEEDING TWELVE MONTHS (exceptionally extendable by not more than three), periodic evaluation of effectiveness, and timely corrective action. EVOLUTION: recommendation to mandatory; the amendment chain; and adaptation to technology, CYBER (MSC.428(98), 16 June 2017, bearing in mind MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3) and environment MOSTLY BY GENERALITY rather than by amendment.

Critical numbers
  • ISM Code adopted by resolution A.741(18); mandatory through SOLAS chapter IX
  • MSC.353(92) adopted 21 June 2013; its amendments in force 1 January 2015 - the operative text at this sitting
  • SIX functional requirements for an SMS at element 1.4
  • TWELVE elements in Part A; elements 13 to 16 in Part B
  • Element 12.1 - internal safety audits on board and ashore at intervals NOT EXCEEDING TWELVE MONTHS; in exceptional circumstances exceeded by NOT MORE THAN THREE MONTHS
  • Element 13.2 - the Document of Compliance issued for a period specified by the Administration NOT EXCEEDING FIVE YEARS
  • Element 13.4 - annual verification of the DOC within THREE MONTHS BEFORE OR AFTER the anniversary date
  • MSC.428(98) adopted 16 June 2017 - maritime cyber risk management in safety management systems
Critical regulation
The ISM Code as adopted by resolution A.741(18) and amended through MSC.353(92), made mandatory by SOLAS chapter IX; together with resolution MSC.428(98) on maritime cyber risk management in safety management systems.
Major trap
Claiming the Code has been repeatedly amended to address technology, cyber risk and environmental sustainability. IT HAS NOT. Its text has been substantially unchanged since MSC.353(92) took effect on 1 January 2015, and it has absorbed all three largely through the GENERALITY of its existing requirements - the one instrument-level response being MSC.428(98), which directed that cyber risks be addressed WITHIN the existing SMS framework rather than by adding a new element. The second trap is putting the SEEMP, EEXI or the CII inside the ISM Code; they are MARPOL Annex VI instruments.

Q2 SUA Convention - Objectives, Obligations and Enforcement
16 marks		 The gap in the law that the Convention was written to close → The instruments and their dates → The primary objectives → Where the Convention applies → Key provisions one - the offences created in 1988 → Key provisions two - what the 2005 Protocol added → How the scheme aims to enhance maritime security → Obligation one - criminalize and penalize (article 5) → Obligation two - establish jurisdiction (article 6) → Obligation three - custody, preliminary inquiry and notification (article 7) → Obligation four - extradite or prosecute (article 10) → Obligation five - extradition and mutual legal assistance (articles 11, 12 and 8) → Obligation six - prevention, cooperation and reporting (articles 13 and 14) → The enforcement mechanism at sea - boarding under article 8bis Convention for the Suppression of Unlawful Acts against the Safety of Maritime Navigation, 1988, as extended by its Protocol of 2005; distinguished from SOLAS chapter XI-2 and the ISPS Code, which are the preventive regime.
SUA Convention adopted at Rome in 1988; entered into force in 1992		 Answering as though SUA were a shipboard security regime. It is not - it creates no ship security plan, no ship security officer, no security levels and no declaration of security. Those are ISPS. SUA is a criminal-law and jurisdiction treaty that operates through national courts. The second trap is writing limb (b) as a second pass over the offences: the stem asks what STATES MUST IMPLEMENT, which is articles 5, 6, 7, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14 and the article 8bis boarding procedure.

The third is answering limb (c) with a general impression of seriousness instead of the Code's three branches: total loss of the ship, OR a death, OR severe damage to the environment.

The second trap is claiming MEPC 81 adopted a market-based measure - it did not; it adopted two Annex VI and Protocol I amendment resolutions on unrelated subjects.

NO NUMERICAL VALUE IS ASSERTED ON THIS QUESTION - this is deliberate and is recorded in the answer object		 Answering in fuel per day instead of fuel per tonne-mile. In fuel per day a bigger, faster ship is always worse and the question becomes unanswerable. The second trap is asserting that bigger is always better - it is better ONLY IF SHE IS FULL, and the cargo availability, utilisation and ballast-leg limits are where the marks in limb (a) actually are. The third is treating the question as an EEDI question; the stem asks about naval architecture and mentions efficiency as the purpose.
Q6 ISO 8217 Marine Fuel Standards and the Bunker Delivery Note
16 marks		 The standard, named and placed → How the specification is organised → The salient features of the specification → The mandatory floor beneath the specification - MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.3 → How a fuel oil analysis report is read → Corrective action - density, viscosity and ignition quality → Corrective action - catalytic fines → Corrective action - water, sediment, stability and compatibility → Corrective action - sulphur and flashpoint → Corrective action - ash, metals and micro-carbon residue → The decision that sits above all the corrective actions → What it is, and where it comes from → Its contents, retention and inspection → The representative sample, and why the BDN matters MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18 (Fuel oil availability and quality) and appendix V (Information to be included in the bunker delivery note), in the text operative at this sitting - that is, BEFORE the amendments adopted by MEPC.385(81) on 22 March 2024, which did not enter into force until 1 August 2025.
MARPOL Annex VI appendix V - the bunker delivery note contains at least TEN items of information		 WRITING THE POST-2025 REGULATION 18. MEPC.385(81) split paragraph 5 into 5.1 and 5.2, creating a separate BDN regime for low-flashpoint and gas fuels, redefined 'fuel oil' as any fuel delivered to and intended for use on board, inserted a definition of 'gas fuel', and rewrote paragraph 4. NONE of that was in force in August 2024. The second trap is treating ISO 8217 as a regulation - it is a commercial specification, and MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.3 is the mandatory floor. The third is quoting an ISO 8217 limit value or edition year, neither of which MIW can verify.
Q7 Salvage Convention - Award Criteria, Duties and LOF 2000
16 marks		 The Convention, and where the stem's facts have got to → The scheme Article 13 sits inside → The Article 13 criteria → How the criteria are applied → Who pays, and what can reduce or extinguish the award → The salvor's duties → The duties of the owner and the master → The mutual character of the duties, the master's authority, and breach → What LOF 2000 is, and what it is not → When the Convention applies → When the LOF applies → How a dispute is actually resolved under an LOF → The relationship, stated as a rule → Why the arrangement exists at all International Convention on Salvage, 1989 - articles 6, 8, 12 and 13 in particular; with LOF 2000 and SCOPIC identified as COMMERCIAL CONTRACT outside the Convention.
International Convention on Salvage, 1989 - in force 1996; replaced the Brussels Convention of 1910		 Treating LOF 2000 as though it were a legal instrument that competes with the Convention. It is a standard-form CONTRACT. It fixes the forum, the governing law, the security arrangements and the machinery for fixing the sum; the CONVENTION supplies the substantive principles the arbitrator applies. The second trap is forgetting that Article 8 imposes duties on the SHIPOWNER AND MASTER as well as on the salvor - including the duty to ACCEPT REDELIVERY - which is half of limb (b). The third is answering on Article 14 special compensation, which this stem does not ask for.
Q8 Recent Developments in Marine Turbocharger Application
16 marks		 Why the turbocharger is where the gains now are → The common thread, and the division that organises the answer → What it is → How it is arranged and controlled → What it buys, and what it costs → What it is → How it works in both directions → What it buys, and what it costs → What it is → The problem it solves → What it buys, and what it costs → Choosing between them → What these developments mean for the operating engineer → Where this fits in the wider picture MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 - the energy efficiency indices that give these developments their commercial value; and MARPOL Annex VI regulation 13 with the NOx Technical File, which constrains what may be altered on a certified engine. Both named by identity and function only.
NO NUMERICAL VALUE IS ASSERTED ON THIS QUESTION - this is deliberate and is recorded in the answer object		 Treating all three as the same device. They are not: the POWER TURBINE is a separate machine fed by bypassed gas, while the HYBRID and the ELECTRO-ASSIST put the electrical machine on the turbocharger's own shaft; and the power turbine and hybrid HARVEST surplus energy at high load while the electro-assist SUPPLIES a deficit at low load. The second trap is claiming a percentage saving or a maker's figure - none is verifiable and none is needed. The third is forgetting to say AT WHAT PART OF THE LOAD RANGE each device works, which is what actually determines whether it is worth fitting.
Q9 Safety Management System and the Evolution of the ISM Code
16 marks		 What the Code is, and how it binds → What a Safety Management System is, and why the Code works through one → The six functional requirements - what every SMS must contain → The components - Part A, elements 1 to 12 → The certification layer - Part B → The role the SMS plays in implementing the Code → Continuous improvement - the mechanism → Continuous improvement - why it is the significant part → Why the Code exists, and the shift it represents → Evolution one - from recommendation to mandatory instrument → Evolution two - the amendment chain → Evolution three - technological advancement → Evolution four - cyber risk → Evolution five - environmental sustainability, and the honest limit The ISM Code as adopted by resolution A.741(18) and amended through MSC.353(92), made mandatory by SOLAS chapter IX; together with resolution MSC.428(98) on maritime cyber risk management in safety management systems.
ISM Code adopted by resolution A.741(18); mandatory through SOLAS chapter IX		 Claiming the Code has been repeatedly amended to address technology, cyber risk and environmental sustainability. IT HAS NOT. Its text has been substantially unchanged since MSC.353(92) took effect on 1 January 2015, and it has absorbed all three largely through the GENERALITY of its existing requirements - the one instrument-level response being MSC.428(98), which directed that cyber risks be addressed WITHIN the existing SMS framework rather than by adding a new element. The second trap is putting the SEEMP, EEXI or the CII inside the ISM Code; they are MARPOL Annex VI instruments.
